	<item>
		<title>Production MLOps at GS Caltex with Amazon SageMaker Canvas, part 1</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/production-mlops-at-gs-caltex-with-amazon-sagemaker-canvas-part-1/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[WonGu Rho]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 13 Nov 2026 23:22:09 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker Canvas]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">760cbc8b70c56b832a6d83eb6dfab47ce2fe8a3b</guid>

					<description>GS Caltex connects Amazon SageMaker Canvas to Amazon SageMaker Pipelines so process engineers' no-code models run in production. This post shows how ML engineers reuse validated preprocessing flows and models, cutting deployment from about a week to a few hours and running batch inference every 30 minutes.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://www.gscaltex.com/en" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GS Caltex&lt;/a&gt; uses &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/canvas/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker Canvas&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/pipelines/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker Pipelines&lt;/a&gt; to connect process engineers’ models to production workflows. The South Korean energy and chemical company divides model development and operations between engineers with complementary skills.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Process engineers build and validate models in SageMaker Canvas without writing code, drawing on their process knowledge. Machine learning (ML) engineers configure SageMaker Pipelines workflows for deployment and scheduled inference.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Moving assets from SageMaker Canvas into SageMaker Pipelines still required integration work. ML engineers had to set data paths for production, supply the model configuration, and connect preprocessing and inference jobs. GS Caltex automated this handoff while reusing the assets created in SageMaker Canvas.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;GS Caltex reports that its ML engineers now deploy models within a few hours, compared with about a week before. The company runs batch inference every 30 minutes, reducing the need for continuously running endpoints. GS Caltex reports that this has meaningfully lowered its inference costs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post explains how GS Caltex connects SageMaker Canvas to SageMaker Pipelines and reuses validated preprocessing flows and models in production.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The machine learning operations (MLOps) workflow has two paths: preparing a new model version for deployment, and running batch inference with the deployed model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="from-a-validated-model-to-a-repeatable-workflow"&gt;From a validated model to a repeatable workflow&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can use SageMaker Canvas to prepare data visually, build models, and generate batch predictions. You can also register validated models in Amazon SageMaker Model Registry from SageMaker Canvas. GS Caltex built a separate handoff process to turn these assets into executable SageMaker Pipelines steps.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Process engineers understand the variables and data behind a process and assess whether a model’s predictions are useful. ML engineers turn the resulting assets into production jobs and manage model versions and execution history.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Area&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What is prepared in SageMaker Canvas&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What is managed in the production workflow&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Data preparation&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A preprocessing flow built with Data Wrangler&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Preprocessing jobs in Amazon SageMaker Processing&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Model development&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A trained model and evaluation results&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Model versions and approval status in Model Registry&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Prediction&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Validation of model predictions&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Batch inference every 30 minutes using an approved model&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Operations&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Development and validation of individual models&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Step execution history, stored results, and failure handling&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="reference-architecture"&gt;Reference architecture&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows how process engineers hand off SageMaker Canvas assets to ML engineers for deployment and scheduled inference. ML engineers use Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) for preprocessing flows and Model Registry for model versions, then schedule pipeline runs through Amazon EventBridge.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ML-21878-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ML-21878-1.png" alt="Process engineers build models in SageMaker Canvas. ML engineers connect preprocessing flows in S3 and models in Model Registry to SageMaker Pipelines, with Amazon EventBridge triggering batch inference." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: SageMaker Canvas assets connected to a SageMaker Pipelines workflow for scheduled batch inference&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;ML engineers receive the preprocessing flow and model from SageMaker Canvas and review the data formats and prediction results. After that review, they approve the model in Model Registry and deploy it. This preparation takes place when a new model version is deployed. Subsequent inference runs reuse the deployed model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon EventBridge starts each scheduled run. SageMaker Pipelines prepares the input data from Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3), runs preprocessing and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/batch-transform.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker Batch Transform&lt;/a&gt;, and stores the results in S3. ML engineers can inspect step status and Amazon CloudWatch logs to monitor the workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The production pipeline reuses the preprocessing logic and trained model created in SageMaker Canvas. ML engineers configure the input and output paths, set the order of execution, and write the code that connects approval and scheduling to the workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before you begin, prepare the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/canvas-custom-models.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;model built and evaluated in SageMaker Canvas&lt;/a&gt;, along with its &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/canvas-data-prep.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Data Wrangler preprocessing flow&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;CSV data for inference and an S3 bucket to store the files.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-roles.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker execution role&lt;/a&gt; and an &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/pipeline-eventbridge.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon EventBridge role with permission to start the pipeline&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A Python environment with Boto3 and SageMaker Python SDK 2.x installed.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The full reference implementation is available in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-canvas-to-pipelines-mlops" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt;. The code examples in this post focus on the connections between components. The repository uses a sample loan classification model, so adjust the configuration to match your model’s input and output formats.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="technical-implementation"&gt;Technical implementation&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following steps cover preprocessing reuse, model approval and deployment, pipeline construction, and scheduled inference.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="run-the-sagemaker-canvas-preprocessing-flow-with-sagemaker-processing"&gt;1. Run the SageMaker Canvas preprocessing flow with SageMaker Processing&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A preprocessing flow created with Data Wrangler in SageMaker Canvas records the data sources and transformation steps. To set up the reference implementation, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/canvas-export-data.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;export data to Amazon S3&lt;/a&gt; using SageMaker Processing, then retrieve the job settings and the &lt;code&gt;.flow&lt;/code&gt; file used by that job.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the export settings, turn off &lt;strong&gt;Auto job configuration&lt;/strong&gt; and select &lt;strong&gt;SageMaker Processing&lt;/strong&gt;. After the export starts, open the Processing job details on the Amazon SageMaker AI console. Record the container image URI, the S3 URI of the input named &lt;code&gt;flow&lt;/code&gt;, the execution role, and the output settings. Use these values to configure the pipeline step that runs the &lt;code&gt;.flow&lt;/code&gt; file.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The SageMaker execution role must have permissions to read the required S3 data, write the results, and access the container image. Choose the instance type and count based on the volume of data you need to process.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Keep the preprocessing logic validated by the process engineer, and adjust the data sources, paths, and other settings for the production environment. Check the container image and output node against the original job configuration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Item to check&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What to do&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Input location&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Check the S3 URI referenced by the data source in the &lt;code&gt;.flow&lt;/code&gt; file. Uploading raw data to another location does not update this reference.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Container image&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Use the image from the Processing job created during the S3 export, and pin a version that reproduces the validated transformations.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Output ID&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Use the output node and output name from the exported configuration. If the required format is &lt;code&gt;{node_id}.{output_name}&lt;/code&gt;, match it exactly.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Learned transformation values&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Preserve values learned during training, such as scaling and encoding parameters. Do not refit them on each inference batch.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Validation&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Compare the columns, data types, and transformed values produced by SageMaker Canvas and Processing for the same input.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Some &lt;code&gt;.flow&lt;/code&gt; files contain execution metadata such as &lt;code&gt;internal_metadata.dw_job.container_uri&lt;/code&gt;. Check the actual Processing job settings rather than assuming that every export contains this field. If you move the workflow to another AWS Region, verify that the container image is available there.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The reference implementation separates intermediate and final outputs by execution under &lt;code&gt;runs/&amp;lt;execution-id&amp;gt;/&lt;/code&gt;. Its &lt;code&gt;PrepareInput&lt;/code&gt; step writes data to a fixed S3 location referenced by the &lt;code&gt;.flow&lt;/code&gt; file. Prevent executions that share that input path from overlapping.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To isolate input data for each execution as well, add a preparation step that updates the source URI in a copy of the &lt;code&gt;.flow&lt;/code&gt; file. Declaring a SageMaker Pipelines parameter does not automatically replace a URI inside the file.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="handle-differences-between-training-and-inference-inputs"&gt;2. Handle differences between training and inference inputs&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The target column is one difference between training and inference data. A flow built with training data can contain transformations that expect the target column in the input schema. Inference data does not contain that known outcome.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The reference implementation adds a &lt;code&gt;PrepareInput&lt;/code&gt; step before preprocessing. If the target column is missing, this step adds it with a placeholder value. After preprocessing, &lt;code&gt;DropTargetColumn&lt;/code&gt; removes the column. Match the placeholder’s data type and column position to the training schema, and pass only the expected features to the model in their original order.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-text"&gt;PrepareInput -&amp;gt; PreprocessNewData -&amp;gt; DropTargetColumn
             -&amp;gt; BatchPredict -&amp;gt; AssembleResults&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use this approach only when the placeholder target does not affect feature values. A flow that filters rows by the target, derives features from it, or applies target encoding needs additional treatment. In those cases, separate the transformations and learned state required for inference.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Validation should cover column order and count, data types, missing-value handling, and row count. After removing the target, check that the remaining columns match the feature order used during training.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="register-approve-and-deploy-the-model"&gt;3. Register, approve, and deploy the model&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Register the validated SageMaker Canvas model version in &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/canvas-register-model.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Model Registry&lt;/a&gt;. The registered version can include the model files, inference configuration, and evaluation results. ML engineers review this information before changing the approval status.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The model file, such as &lt;code&gt;model.tar.gz&lt;/code&gt;, must be used with the correct inference container and environment settings. A model package in Model Registry groups this information together. In this batch architecture, deployment creates a SageMaker model resource from an approved package for Batch Transform to reference.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following example checks a package that has already been approved, then creates the model resource. Supply &lt;code&gt;model_package_arn&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;model_name&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;sagemaker_role_arn&lt;/code&gt; during deployment. This example uses a unique model name for each version.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import boto3

sm_client = boto3.client("sagemaker")
package = sm_client.describe_model_package(
    ModelPackageName=model_package_arn
)

if package["ModelPackageStatus"] != "Completed":
    raise RuntimeError("Model package is not ready")
if package["ModelApprovalStatus"] != "Approved":
    raise RuntimeError("Model package is not approved")

sm_client.create_model(
    ModelName=model_name,
    ExecutionRoleArn=sagemaker_role_arn,
    PrimaryContainer={"ModelPackageName": model_package_arn},
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Model Registry records approval status, but it does not enforce an approval check on every inference request. Check that status in the deployment code and retain the selected package ARN and model name in the pipeline configuration. If approval is revoked, update the schedules and execution configurations that use the model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Registration and deployment take place when a model version is released. The pipeline that runs every 30 minutes creates Batch Transform jobs using that model, so it does not need to register the model again on each run.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="connect-preprocessing-inference-and-result-assembly"&gt;4. Connect preprocessing, inference, and result assembly&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The inference pipeline has five steps.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Function&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Input and output&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;PrepareInput&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Add a placeholder target column if needed&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;New inference data -&amp;gt; data with the target column supplied&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;PreprocessNewData&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Run the SageMaker Canvas preprocessing flow&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Prepared input and &lt;code&gt;.flow&lt;/code&gt; -&amp;gt; transformed data&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;DropTargetColumn&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Remove the target column and CSV header&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Transformed data -&amp;gt; feature CSV without a header&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;BatchPredict&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Run batch inference with the approved model&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Feature CSV -&amp;gt; prediction files&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;AssembleResults&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Combine the preprocessed data with predictions&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Preprocessed data and prediction files -&amp;gt; final result CSV&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Configure each job before connecting the steps. In the following example, &lt;code&gt;prepare_args&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;preprocess_args&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;drop_target_args&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;assemble_args&lt;/code&gt; hold the step arguments returned by &lt;code&gt;run()&lt;/code&gt; calls on processors that use a &lt;code&gt;PipelineSession&lt;/code&gt;. The processors and &lt;code&gt;transformer&lt;/code&gt; share the &lt;code&gt;pipeline_session&lt;/code&gt; object. Configure the &lt;code&gt;transformer&lt;/code&gt; with the previously deployed &lt;code&gt;model_name&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from sagemaker.workflow.pipeline import Pipeline
from sagemaker.workflow.steps import ProcessingStep, TransformStep

prepare = ProcessingStep(
    name="PrepareInput", step_args=prepare_args
)
preprocess = ProcessingStep(
    name="PreprocessNewData",
    step_args=preprocess_args,
    depends_on=[prepare],
)
drop_target = ProcessingStep(
    name="DropTargetColumn",
    step_args=drop_target_args,
    depends_on=[preprocess],
)

# The DropTargetColumn job defines an output named "features".
features_uri = (
    drop_target.properties.ProcessingOutputConfig
    .Outputs["features"].S3Output.S3Uri
)
predict = TransformStep(
    name="BatchPredict",
    step_args=transformer.transform(
        data=features_uri,
        content_type="text/csv",
        split_type="Line",
    ),
)
assemble = ProcessingStep(
    name="AssembleResults",
    step_args=assemble_args,
    depends_on=[predict],
)

pipeline = Pipeline(
    name="canvas-process-inference",
    steps=[prepare, preprocess, drop_target, predict, assemble],
    sagemaker_session=pipeline_session,
)
pipeline_arn = pipeline.upsert(role_arn=sagemaker_role_arn)["PipelineArn"]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following execution graph shows the five steps in the sample loan model’s inference workflow. They prepare inputs, apply preprocessing, remove the target column, run batch inference, and assemble results. The execution was in the &lt;code&gt;Executing&lt;/code&gt; state when this screenshot was taken.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 440px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ML-21878-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ML-21878-2.png" alt="Execution graph for the sample loan model showing PrepareInput, PreprocessNewData, DropTargetColumn, BatchPredict, and AssembleResults" width="430"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: SageMaker Pipelines execution graph for the sample loan model&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use &lt;code&gt;depends_on&lt;/code&gt; to control execution order, and pass data through step output properties. For example, &lt;code&gt;assemble_args&lt;/code&gt; must receive the location of the preprocessed data and &lt;code&gt;predict.properties.TransformOutput.S3OutputPath&lt;/code&gt;. The output name &lt;code&gt;"features"&lt;/code&gt; belongs to &lt;code&gt;DropTargetColumn&lt;/code&gt;. The Data Wrangler step uses the output ID defined in its preprocessing flow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the reference implementation, &lt;code&gt;AssembleResults&lt;/code&gt; combines the preprocessed data and predictions by row position. Before using it in production, add checks that file order and row counts match, and stop result generation if they do not. If preprocessing sorts or removes rows, maintain an explicit mapping between source record IDs and predictions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="schedule-inference-every-30-minutes"&gt;5. Schedule inference every 30 minutes&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;GS Caltex runs inference every 30 minutes. The following example uses an &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/pipeline-eventbridge.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon EventBridge rule to schedule SageMaker Pipelines executions&lt;/a&gt;. Create the pipeline and verify it with a manual run before attaching the schedule.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import boto3

events_client = boto3.client("events")
rule_name = "canvas-process-inference-every-30-minutes"

events_client.put_rule(
    Name=rule_name,
    ScheduleExpression="rate(30 minutes)",
    State="ENABLED",
)

result = events_client.put_targets(
    Rule=rule_name,
    Targets=[{
        "Id": "canvas-process-inference",
        "Arn": pipeline_arn,
        "RoleArn": eventbridge_role_arn,
    }],
)
if result["FailedEntryCount"]:
    raise RuntimeError(result["FailedEntries"])&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Set &lt;code&gt;eventbridge_role_arn&lt;/code&gt; to a role that trusts the Amazon EventBridge service principal, &lt;code&gt;events.amazonaws.com&lt;/code&gt;, and has &lt;code&gt;sagemaker:StartPipelineExecution&lt;/code&gt; permission for the target pipeline. The &lt;code&gt;pipeline_arn&lt;/code&gt; value identifies the pipeline created in the previous step.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The expression &lt;code&gt;rate(30 minutes)&lt;/code&gt; schedules runs at 30-minute intervals. To run on the hour and half hour, use &lt;code&gt;cron(0/30 * * * ? *)&lt;/code&gt;. Decide which data window each run processes in the input preparation step, and record it with the execution.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="operational-considerations"&gt;Operational considerations&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before enabling scheduled runs, check the total cost, execution time, and handling of invalid or mismatched data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="evaluate-batch-costs-across-the-full-workflow"&gt;Evaluate batch costs across the full workflow&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/pipelines/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker Pipelines&lt;/a&gt; is a serverless workflow orchestration service. It manages execution order and dependencies without a workflow server for you to provision or maintain. Processing and Batch Transform jobs use separate compute resources and are billed according to instance type and duration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A provisioned real-time endpoint is a good fit for requests that need a consistently low-latency response. Batch Transform suits workloads that can process a set of records together. It releases the job’s compute resources when the job finishes, so you can run predictions without keeping an inference endpoint running continuously for each model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Consideration&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Provisioned real-time endpoint&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;SageMaker Pipelines with Batch Transform&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Resource lifecycle&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Instances remain available to serve requests&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Resources are provisioned and released for each job&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cost drivers&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Endpoint uptime and capacity&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Job duration, frequency, instance count, and supporting services&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Suitable workloads&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Immediate responses and ongoing online requests&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Processing accumulated data at scheduled intervals&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Adding a model&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Endpoint capacity and ongoing hosting costs&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Additional processing, job duration, and concurrency limits&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A 30-minute schedule produces 48 scheduled runs per day. Include preprocessing, instance startup, inference, and postprocessing when estimating total runtime. The cost comparison should also account for Processing, S3, CloudWatch, and other services used by the workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Assess the cost of each workload using its measured usage, including the supporting services and the full runtime of every scheduled job.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="check-runtime-and-overlapping-executions"&gt;Check runtime and overlapping executions&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A schedule that starts a run every 30 minutes does not guarantee that each run finishes within that interval. Measure the full pipeline runtime with representative data, including instance startup and the time needed to retry failed jobs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Decide how to handle a scheduled run that arrives before the previous execution has finished. Controlling parallel steps within a pipeline does not prevent separate pipeline executions from overlapping. Check active executions, prevent duplicate processing of the same input window, and separate the output paths for each run.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Record the input data window, preprocessing flow version, model package ARN, pipeline execution ARN, and output location for each run. These details help reproduce results and investigate errors.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="validate-outputs-and-failure-behavior"&gt;Validate outputs and failure behavior&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Complete the following checks before you enable the schedule:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Run a validation dataset through SageMaker Canvas and Processing. Compare the output columns, data types, row counts, and key transformed values.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Check that adding a placeholder target does not change feature values, and that the target is removed from the model input.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Run Batch Transform with the approved model version and verify the association between source records and predictions.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Confirm that an invalid input schema or preprocessing failure prevents downstream inference and result publication.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Measure runtime and cost, test overlapping executions and reprocessing of the same input window, and then enable the 30-minute schedule.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Add CloudWatch alerts for job failures and processing delays, together with data quality or model monitoring as needed. Set initial thresholds from measured runtime and data characteristics, and review them when the model or data volume changes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="adoption-approach-and-results"&gt;Adoption approach and results&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With a staged rollout, a team can verify the handoff before standardizing it across models. The following sections describe an adoption approach and the results GS Caltex reported.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="move-from-model-development-to-regular-operation"&gt;Move from model development to regular operation&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Start by validating the path to production for one model, then standardize the steps that repeat across deployments. The following sequence is a suggested adoption approach based on the GS Caltex experience.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Stage&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Goal&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scope&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Crawl&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Reproduce development results in production&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Validate the SageMaker Canvas model, register it in Model Registry, and run the pipeline manually&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Walk&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Establish reliable scheduled operation&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Check approvals, schedule runs at the required interval, add failure alerts, and manage outputs by execution&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Run&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Reuse the operating process across models&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Introduce common templates, continuous integration and continuous delivery (CI/CD), change validation, and procedures for transferring operational responsibility&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As automation expands, process engineers continue to assess whether the predictions are useful for the process. ML engineers manage pipeline execution and changes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="results-at-gs-caltex"&gt;Results at GS Caltex&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;GS Caltex reduced the repeated work needed to connect SageMaker Canvas models to production. ML engineers now deploy models within a few hours, compared with about a week previously. They also reuse the assets that process engineers validated in SageMaker Canvas.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Process engineers began proposing applications in other processes after seeing their models used in production. ML engineers spent less time repeating integration work and could focus on operating and monitoring the pipelines.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Outcome&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Reported result&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Model deployment time&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Previously about a week. Now within a few hours&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Inference frequency&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Every 30 minutes&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cost impact&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Meaningful cost savings&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Engineering work&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Process engineers proposed new applications; ML engineers focused on pipeline operation and monitoring&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up-test-resources"&gt;Clean up test resources&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For a test deployment, disable the Amazon EventBridge rule and remove its targets first. After active pipeline executions and jobs have finished, delete the test pipeline and its SageMaker model resources.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you created a real-time endpoint for testing, delete it as well. Remove input, intermediate, and prediction files from S3, and clean up CloudWatch logs according to your retention policy. Check whether other executions or audit requirements still depend on model files and Model Registry versions before deleting them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At GS Caltex, process engineers create and validate models in SageMaker Canvas, and ML engineers use those assets to run production workflows in SageMaker Pipelines. This handoff reduced repeated integration work. GS Caltex reports that ML engineers now complete model deployments within a few hours, compared with about a week before.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The handoff needs to include the preprocessing flow, input schema, inference configuration, and approval process alongside the model files. Verify that the workflow reproduces the expected results and can run reliably on the required schedule.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To try this approach, start with one model you have validated in SageMaker Canvas and use the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-canvas-to-pipelines-mlops" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt;. See the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/pipelines.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker Pipelines developer guide&lt;/a&gt; for details on defining and running workflows. Check preprocessing and batch prediction results with a manual execution, then schedule runs at the interval your application needs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In part 2, we show how GS Caltex used Amazon Bedrock to automate moving existing models onto this pipeline.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Route coding agent requests to the right model on Amazon Bedrock with NVIDIA NeMo Switchyard</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/route-coding-agent-requests-to-the-right-model-on-amazon-bedrock-with-nvidia-nemo-switchyard/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Aswath Ram A Srinivasan]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 13 Nov 2026 22:30:12 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">f4831ed5c33ec5088dfe090f067c6a3a1c207b47</guid>

					<description>Coding agents work in tasks, and the number that matters is cost per solved task, not price per token. In this post, you use the AWS CDK to deploy LiteLLM with the NVIDIA NeMo Switchyard routing plugin on Amazon Bedrock, then measure cost per solved task for a routed model group against single-model baselines.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Coding agents such as Claude Code and OpenAI Codex CLI work in tasks. A developer asks for a change, and the agent reads files, edits code, and runs tests until the work is done. One task can take a few model calls or dozens. What the developer pays for is the task, not one call.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The price per token alone doesn’t tell you what a whole task costs. The total depends on how many turns a model needs, how much history each turn replays, and how often a task has to be retried or never finishes. You pay for every attempt, including attempts on tasks that never finish. So when you route a coding agent’s requests between models, judge the routing by cost per solved task: the total cost of your runs divided by the number of runs whose tests pass.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/Switchyard" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;NVIDIA NeMo Switchyard&lt;/a&gt; is an open source library that helps an AI agent choose which model handles each request. You define the models that it can use and the strategy that it follows. Its &lt;a href="https://github.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/Switchyard/blob/21075c6652152dac88a5fbc6b878ca37d7617c1c/examples/litellm/README.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;LiteLLM integration&lt;/a&gt; supports two strategies. Stage routing reads the recent tool activity and results in the request history, and then picks one of two candidate models. Random routing picks from the configured models, with optional weights, for traffic splits and A/B comparisons. Another option you can explore is LiteLLM, who also recently shipped a native built-in &lt;a href="https://docs.litellm.ai/blog/switchyard-tool-aware-routing" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Switchyard stage router&lt;/a&gt; as an auto-router strategy.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you use the AWS Cloud Development Kit (AWS CDK) to deploy LiteLLM, an open source gateway for large language models (LLMs), with the Switchyard routing plugin in your AWS account. LiteLLM calls the models on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;. You then use the sample’s benchmark runners to measure cost per solved task for a routed model group and for single-model baseline groups, so that you can evaluate routing against your own workloads.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; The sample installs &lt;code&gt;switchyard-server&lt;/code&gt; 0.3.0 from crates.io and runs the published LiteLLM v1.102.0 image. NVIDIA documents &lt;code&gt;switchyard-server&lt;/code&gt; 0.3.0 as a demo and evaluation component, and its configuration can change between releases. This sample uses the experimental Switchyard LiteLLM integration and its configuration and APIs may change between releases. This is sample code, for non-production usage. Work with your security and legal teams to meet your organizational security, regulatory, and compliance requirements before deployment. Deploy the sample in a non-production account and test it before relying on it.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 1 shows the architecture of the solution. LiteLLM and the Switchyard plugin run in one container image on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ecs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Container Service (Amazon ECS)&lt;/a&gt; on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/fargate/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Fargate&lt;/a&gt;, in private subnets.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/ML-22318-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/ML-22318-1.png" alt="Architecture showing coding agents reaching an Application Load Balancer that routes to LiteLLM and Switchyard on Amazon ECS Fargate, which calls models on Amazon Bedrock" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Solution architecture&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A request flows through these steps. The numbers match the diagram.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The coding agent sends an OpenAI-compatible request with the model field set to &lt;code&gt;switchyard&lt;/code&gt;, the LiteLLM model group that Switchyard routes, and the shared gateway key in the &lt;code&gt;Authorization&lt;/code&gt; header. The endpoint is your hostname over HTTPS, with an &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/certificate-manager/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Certificate Manager (ACM)&lt;/a&gt; certificate on the load balancer, and only the IPv4 CIDR range that you allow can reach it. With a hosted zone in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/route53/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Route 53&lt;/a&gt;, Route 53 resolves the hostname to the load balancer.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/waf/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS WAF&lt;/a&gt; filters the request with IP reputation, known bad input, and per-IP rate-limit rules. The &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/elasticloadbalancing/application-load-balancer/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Application Load Balancer&lt;/a&gt; forwards only &lt;code&gt;/v1/chat/completions&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;/v1/models&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;/health/liveliness&lt;/code&gt; to the ECS task in a private subnet, and answers every other path with 404.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The task runs one container: LiteLLM with the embedded Switchyard plugin. LiteLLM validates the gateway key and calls the plugin to pick a model. Stage chooses between a capable model and a lower-cost efficient model. It reads the tool activity and results in the request history, so the agent must send the full conversation history. Random chooses from a weighted pool. Neither strategy calls a judge model.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;LiteLLM calls the selected model through the Amazon Bedrock &lt;code&gt;Converse&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;ConverseStream&lt;/code&gt; API, with the task’s &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM)&lt;/a&gt; role for credentials. The call goes through an Amazon Bedrock Runtime interface endpoint in the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/vpc/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Virtual Private Cloud (Amazon VPC)&lt;/a&gt;, so inference traffic stays on the AWS network.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The response or token stream returns through LiteLLM and the load balancer. The &lt;code&gt;x-litellm-model-name&lt;/code&gt; response header names the model that answered. The response body’s &lt;code&gt;model&lt;/code&gt; field stays &lt;code&gt;switchyard&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;LiteLLM listens on port 4000 in the container and is the load balancer’s target, and the task’s security group admits only the load balancer. The path allowlist keeps the LiteLLM admin UI, API docs, and key management routes unreachable. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ecr/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR)&lt;/a&gt; holds the gateway image. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/secrets-manager/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Secrets Manager&lt;/a&gt; generates the secret behind the gateway key, and the container adds the &lt;code&gt;sk-&lt;/code&gt; prefix when it starts. The task definition passes the profile files in &lt;code&gt;LITELLM_CONFIG_TEXT&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;SWITCHYARD_CONFIG_TEXT&lt;/code&gt;, and the startup script writes them to &lt;code&gt;/tmp&lt;/code&gt; before it starts LiteLLM. Logs go to one &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/a&gt; log group, &lt;code&gt;/&amp;lt;stack name&amp;gt;/litellm&lt;/code&gt;, which keeps them for 30 days, and Container Insights collects metrics. The VPC spans two Availability Zones, and one NAT gateway provides outbound access for image pulls and other AWS services.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The plugin supports Stage routing, which needs exactly two candidates, and Random routing, which needs at least two and splits traffic by weight for A/B tests. Classifier-backed strategies, such as Task and Composite routing, need intermediate model calls that this integration doesn’t support. See the &lt;a href="https://nvidia-nemo.github.io/Switchyard/routing_algorithms/overview/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Switchyard routing overview&lt;/a&gt; for the full catalog.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To follow this walkthrough, you need the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A non-production AWS account with Amazon Bedrock access to Claude Opus 5.5 and NVIDIA Nemotron 3 Super 120B. The pair has been tested in US East (N. Virginia). For other AWS Regions, check &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-region-compatibility.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Regional availability by models&lt;/a&gt; and tool-calling support.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI) v2 with configured credentials and an explicit AWS Region, in an account and Region that are &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cdk/v2/guide/bootstrapping-env.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;bootstrapped for the AWS CDK&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Node.js 20 or later, npm, Docker with BuildKit, and Python 3.11 or later.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A hostname that you own, with either a public Route 53 hosted zone for its domain or an ACM certificate for it in the deployment Region. A short plain-HTTP test needs neither.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="walkthrough"&gt;Walkthrough&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You define the model group, configure Stage, deploy and test the gateway, and measure cost. Start by cloning the sample repository:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;git clone https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-route-coding-agent-requests-Amazon-Bedrock-nemo-switchyard.git
cd sample-route-coding-agent-requests-Amazon-Bedrock-nemo-switchyard&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-1-define-the-model-group-in-litellm"&gt;Step 1: Define the model group in LiteLLM&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A LiteLLM model group is one public model name with one or more deployments behind it. The sample keeps its configuration in deployment profiles under &lt;code&gt;profiles/&lt;/code&gt;. Each profile pairs &lt;code&gt;litellm.yaml&lt;/code&gt;, which defines the models and gateway settings, with &lt;code&gt;switchyard.toml&lt;/code&gt;, which controls how Switchyard picks a model. The following excerpt of &lt;code&gt;profiles/stage/litellm.yaml&lt;/code&gt; defines the routed &lt;code&gt;switchyard&lt;/code&gt; group, two single-model baseline groups, and the plugin registration:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;model_list:
  - model_name: switchyard
    litellm_params:
      model: bedrock/converse/global.anthropic.claude-opus-5-5
      aws_region_name: os.environ/AWS_REGION
  - model_name: switchyard
    litellm_params:
      model: bedrock/converse/nvidia.nemotron-super-3-120b
      aws_region_name: os.environ/AWS_REGION
  # One-model baseline groups, same model strings as the routed candidates.
  - model_name: claude-opus-5-5
    litellm_params:
      model: bedrock/converse/global.anthropic.claude-opus-5-5
      aws_region_name: os.environ/AWS_REGION
  - model_name: nemotron-super-3-120b
    litellm_params:
      model: bedrock/converse/nvidia.nemotron-super-3-120b
      aws_region_name: os.environ/AWS_REGION

# The same object selects a deployment and applies supported request changes.
router_settings:
  plugins:
    - switchyard_gateway.configured.ROUTING_PLUGIN
litellm_settings:
  callbacks:
    - switchyard_gateway.configured.ROUTING_PLUGIN
  drop_params: true
  request_timeout: 600
  num_retries: 2
general_settings:
  master_key: os.environ/LITELLM_MASTER_KEY&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Both candidates share &lt;code&gt;model_name: switchyard&lt;/code&gt;, so your client sends &lt;code&gt;"model": "switchyard"&lt;/code&gt; and Switchyard picks a candidate. List the capable model first and the efficient model second, because Stage reads the roles from that order.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;claude-opus-5-5&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;nemotron-super-3-120b&lt;/code&gt; groups are single-model baselines. Each one always answers with its own model, so you can compare the routed group’s cost and pass rate with each model alone in the same deployment. The Stage plugin alone rejects a group without two candidates, so the image registers a small wrapper, &lt;code&gt;switchyard_gateway.configured.ROUTING_PLUGIN&lt;/code&gt;. It applies Switchyard to groups with two or more unique candidates and passes single-model groups through. The configuration registers the wrapper twice: to pick the model and to apply the request changes that Switchyard records.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every model uses &lt;code&gt;bedrock/converse/&amp;lt;id&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt;, so every candidate goes through the Amazon Bedrock &lt;code&gt;Converse&lt;/code&gt; API, and validation rejects any other route. The LiteLLM &lt;code&gt;request_timeout&lt;/code&gt; of 600 seconds matches the load balancer’s 10-minute idle timeout.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The file holds no credentials. LiteLLM reads the gateway key from the environment, and the task role supplies the AWS credentials. Never add credentials such as &lt;code&gt;aws_access_key_id&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;aws_secret_access_key&lt;/code&gt;, or &lt;code&gt;api_key&lt;/code&gt; under &lt;code&gt;litellm_params&lt;/code&gt;: the stack copies the whole &lt;code&gt;litellm.yaml&lt;/code&gt; into the task definition and the AWS CloudFormation template in plain text.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The stack derives the task role’s Amazon Bedrock permissions from this file. For a global cross-Region inference profile, the foundation-model resource uses a &lt;code&gt;*&lt;/code&gt; Region and a trailing &lt;code&gt;*&lt;/code&gt;, so it also matches other model IDs with the same prefix. Review the derived policy before you deploy.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;global.&lt;/code&gt; prefix marks a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/cross-region-inference.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;global cross-Region inference profile&lt;/a&gt;, and Amazon Bedrock might process those requests in supported commercial AWS Regions worldwide. If processing location matters, use a geographic profile or an in-Region model ID instead. For Nemotron 3 Super, the profile uses the in-Region model ID &lt;code&gt;nvidia.nemotron-super-3-120b&lt;/code&gt;, which you can switch for any model for testing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-2-configure-stage-routing"&gt;Step 2: Configure Stage routing&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Switchyard reads its algorithm and settings from &lt;code&gt;switchyard.toml&lt;/code&gt;. The default profile, &lt;code&gt;profiles/stage/switchyard.toml&lt;/code&gt;, configures Stage:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-toml"&gt;# Model inventory is in litellm.yaml. This file configures selection behavior.
algorithm = "stage"
# Prefer the efficient candidate when history signals are inconclusive.
picker = "efficient_first"
confidence_threshold = 0.5
# Inspect the three most recent tool results for execution signals.
recent_window = 3
only_on_wrong_signal_escalation = true&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The settings work as follows:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;picker = "efficient_first"&lt;/code&gt;: Stage uses the efficient model when the signals in the history are inconclusive. A new request has no tool results yet, so it goes to Nemotron 3 Super.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;confidence_threshold&lt;/code&gt;: the confidence cutoff for the execution signals that Stage scores, from 0 through 1.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;recent_window = 3&lt;/code&gt;: Stage examines the three most recent tool results.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;only_on_wrong_signal_escalation = true&lt;/code&gt;: the Switchyard default. It controls when Stage adds an optional escalation note, and this profile sets no note.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Stage also accepts optional handoff notes and per-tier system prompts (&lt;code&gt;escalation_note&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;deescalation_note&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;capable_system_prompt&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;efficient_system_prompt&lt;/code&gt;). The sample profile sets none of them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For an A/B test, the Random profile in &lt;code&gt;profiles/random/&lt;/code&gt; uses the same models with &lt;code&gt;algorithm = "random"&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;weights = [0.5, 0.5]&lt;/code&gt;, one weight per unique candidate in declaration order. To deploy it, add &lt;code&gt;-c routingProfile=random&lt;/code&gt; to the &lt;code&gt;cdk diff&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;cdk deploy&lt;/code&gt; commands in Step 3.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The stack validates both files together when it synthesizes, and the preflight script runs the same checks. To check a profile change on its own, without AWS calls, run &lt;code&gt;npm run validate:profile -- stage&lt;/code&gt;. Invalid settings stop LiteLLM when it starts, and unsupported message shapes fail before inference.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="optional-run-the-gateway-locally"&gt;Optional: Run the gateway locally&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can try the gateway on your machine before you deploy anything. Docker Compose builds the same image and reads the same profile files that the stack deploys to Amazon ECS. Run from the repository root:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;npm ci
cp .env.example .env

export LITELLM_MASTER_KEY="sk-$(openssl rand -hex 24)"
eval "$(aws configure export-credentials --format env)"

ROUTING_PROFILE=stage docker compose up --build --wait
GATEWAY_URL=http://127.0.0.1:4000 ROUTING_PROFILE=stage scripts/smoke-test.sh&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The key that you export takes precedence over the placeholder in &lt;code&gt;.env&lt;/code&gt;, so Compose and the smoke test use the same one. AWS credentials come only from your shell and never go in &lt;code&gt;.env&lt;/code&gt; or the profile files. Compose publishes the gateway on &lt;code&gt;127.0.0.1&lt;/code&gt; only. There’s no load balancer locally, so the smoke test skips its closed-path checks. The local gateway still calls Amazon Bedrock, and those calls are billed. When you’re finished, run &lt;code&gt;docker compose down&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-3-deploy-the-gateway"&gt;Step 3: Deploy the gateway&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The gateway serves HTTPS on a hostname that you own. Set &lt;code&gt;GATEWAY_DOMAIN&lt;/code&gt; to that hostname and &lt;code&gt;HOSTED_ZONE&lt;/code&gt; to an existing Route 53 public hosted zone that contains it. The AWS CDK then creates a DNS-validated ACM certificate and the alias record. From the repository root, install the dependencies, run the read-only preflight check, and deploy:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;npm ci

export AWS_REGION=us-east-1
export AWS_DEFAULT_REGION="$AWS_REGION"
eval "$(aws configure export-credentials --format env)"
export CDK_DEFAULT_REGION="$AWS_REGION"
export CDK_DEFAULT_ACCOUNT="$(aws sts get-caller-identity --query Account --output text)"
export ALLOWED_CIDR="$(curl -4 -fsS --connect-timeout 5 --max-time 15 https://checkip.amazonaws.com)/32"
export GATEWAY_DOMAIN=llm-gateway.example.com
export HOSTED_ZONE=example.com

scripts/preflight.sh
npx cdk diff -c allowedCidr="$ALLOWED_CIDR" \
  -c domainName="$GATEWAY_DOMAIN" -c hostedZone="$HOSTED_ZONE"
npx cdk deploy -c allowedCidr="$ALLOWED_CIDR" \
  -c domainName="$GATEWAY_DOMAIN" -c hostedZone="$HOSTED_ZONE"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If your domain’s DNS isn’t in Route 53, use an existing ACM certificate instead. Set &lt;code&gt;CERTIFICATE_ARN&lt;/code&gt; to an ACM certificate for the hostname in the deployment Region, and replace &lt;code&gt;-c hostedZone="$HOSTED_ZONE"&lt;/code&gt; with &lt;code&gt;-c certificateArn="$CERTIFICATE_ARN"&lt;/code&gt; in both commands. The AWS CDK then creates no certificate and no DNS record. After the deployment, create a CNAME record (or a Route 53 alias) that points the hostname at the &lt;code&gt;LoadBalancerDnsName&lt;/code&gt; output.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The required &lt;code&gt;allowedCidr&lt;/code&gt; value limits which networks can reach the load balancer, and the command sets it to your current public IPv4 address. Keep it to your own &lt;code&gt;/32&lt;/code&gt;: everyone who shares an allowed address, such as an office NAT or a VPN exit, can reach the endpoint. The stack refuses to synthesize without &lt;code&gt;allowedCidr&lt;/code&gt;, with a world-open range such as &lt;code&gt;0.0.0.0/0&lt;/code&gt;, or with an IPv6 range.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Preflight checks the configuration, your tools, and your AWS access, including access to each model in the profile, without making inference requests. The AWS CDK builds the gateway image, publishes it to Amazon ECR, and deploys the stack. Deployment prints these outputs:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Output&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What it is&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;GatewayUrl&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The base URL. The OpenAI-compatible API is under &lt;code&gt;/v1&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;MasterKeySecretArn&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The Secrets Manager secret behind the gateway key. The full key is &lt;code&gt;sk-&lt;/code&gt; followed by the secret value.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;MasterKeyCommand&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A command that prints the gateway key.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;SmokeTestCommand&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The smoke test command, with the stack name, Region, and profile filled in.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;RoutingProfile&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The active routing profile, stage by default.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;ClusterName, ServiceName&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The ECS cluster and service. The key rotation steps use them.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;LoadBalancerDnsName&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Only with certificateArn: the name to point your hostname at.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Always give clients the &lt;code&gt;https://&lt;/code&gt; URL. Port 80 stays open only to redirect to HTTPS, and a client configured with &lt;code&gt;http://&lt;/code&gt; has already sent the key and its first request in clear text when the redirect arrives.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The load balancer idle timeout is 10 minutes, for long agent responses. The web ACL uses the Amazon IP reputation list and the known bad inputs rule group. It also limits each IP address to 2,000 requests per 5 minutes. It leaves out the generic body rules for cross-site scripting, SQL injection, and body size, which the source code in agent requests triggers. The &lt;code&gt;allowedCidr&lt;/code&gt; range and the gateway key still apply.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Quick test over plain HTTP.&lt;/strong&gt; Without HTTPS settings, synth stops with an error. For a short test without a domain, you can opt in to plain HTTP on the load balancer’s DNS name:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;npx cdk deploy -c allowedCidr="$ALLOWED_CIDR" -c allowPlainHttp=true&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With &lt;code&gt;allowPlainHttp=true&lt;/code&gt;, the AWS CDK prints a warning every time it synthesizes the stack. The gateway key in the &lt;code&gt;Authorization&lt;/code&gt; header, your prompts, the tool results and source code that your agent sends, and the model responses cross the network unencrypted between your client and the load balancer. Use the opt-in only for a short test with non-sensitive code from your own &lt;code&gt;/32&lt;/code&gt;. Then rotate the key, and run &lt;code&gt;cdk destroy&lt;/code&gt; when you finish. To move the same stack to HTTPS later, deploy it again with the HTTPS settings and without &lt;code&gt;allowPlainHttp&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-4-test-the-gateway"&gt;Step 4: Test the gateway&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the same terminal that you used for the deployment, read the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudformation/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS CloudFormation&lt;/a&gt; stack outputs and build the gateway key:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;export STACK_NAME=SwitchyardGateway

output() {
  aws cloudformation describe-stacks --region "$AWS_REGION" --stack-name "$STACK_NAME" \
    --query "Stacks[0].Outputs[?OutputKey=='$1'].OutputValue" --output text
}

export GATEWAY_URL="$(output GatewayUrl)"
export LITELLM_MASTER_KEY="sk-$(aws secretsmanager get-secret-value --region "$AWS_REGION" \
  --secret-id "$(output MasterKeySecretArn)" --query SecretString --output text)"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Send a request to the &lt;code&gt;switchyard&lt;/code&gt; model group:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;curl -sS -i --connect-timeout 10 --max-time 180 "$GATEWAY_URL/v1/chat/completions" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $LITELLM_MASTER_KEY" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"model":"switchyard","messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Reply with the word hello."}],"max_tokens":128}'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Look for HTTP 200 and a response. The &lt;code&gt;x-litellm-model-name&lt;/code&gt; response header shows the selected model as the full LiteLLM model string. A new request has no tool results to score, so Stage picks the efficient model, &lt;code&gt;bedrock/converse/nvidia.nemotron-super-3-120b&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To check health, authentication, and routing, run the smoke test:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;ROUTING_PROFILE="$(output RoutingProfile)" scripts/smoke-test.sh&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The smoke test checks the health endpoint, that closed paths such as &lt;code&gt;/ui&lt;/code&gt; get the load balancer’s own 404, that a request without a key gets 401, and that &lt;code&gt;/v1/models&lt;/code&gt; lists every model group. For Stage, it checks that an ordinary request selects the efficient model and that a recorded history of repeated failures escalates to the capable model. Then it sends one request to each baseline group. The request and the smoke test make billable Amazon Bedrock calls.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To try your own agent, use &lt;code&gt;$GATEWAY_URL/v1&lt;/code&gt; as its OpenAI-compatible base URL, the gateway key as its API key, and &lt;code&gt;switchyard&lt;/code&gt; as the model. Stage needs the full supported request history with every request. The upstream adapter supports text messages, function tool calls, and text tool results, and unsupported message shapes fail before inference. The load balancer forwards the Chat Completions path but not the OpenAI Responses or Anthropic Messages paths. Clients that use those APIs get a 404 until you add the path, test it against the local container, and redeploy.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-5-measure-cost-per-solved-task"&gt;Step 5: Measure cost per solved task&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The sample’s coding runner, &lt;code&gt;bench/run.py&lt;/code&gt;, lets a small coding agent list and read files, replace source files, and run the supplied unit tests through the gateway. A task is solved when its tests pass at the end, within six model turns by default. List the tasks and start with &lt;code&gt;slugify&lt;/code&gt; on the routed group:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;python3 bench/run.py --list-tasks
python3 bench/run.py --tasks slugify --model switchyard --max-turns 6&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The terminal shows the task, the selected models, the tool actions, the test results, and the final code diff. Each run saves its starting files, final workspace, transcript, patch, and result under &lt;code&gt;bench/results/&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Task&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What the agent must do&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;slugify&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Fix a short function that builds URL slugs.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;roman&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Write a function that converts integers to Roman numerals.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;calc&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Write an expression parser without &lt;code&gt;eval&lt;/code&gt;, with precedence, a power operator, functions, and error handling.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;lru_ttl&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Write a thread-safe least recently used (LRU) cache with per-entry expiry and exact counters.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To give the arithmetic parser task room to run tests and attempt repairs, raise the turn limit:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;python3 bench/run.py --tasks calc --model switchyard --max-turns 12&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After the runs, the runner prints one row per model group, with RUNS, SOLVED, ERRORS, COST/TASK, and COST/SOLVED columns. Cost per solved task is the estimated cost of all runs on a group divided by the runs whose tests passed. The runner prices each turn from the model in its &lt;code&gt;x-litellm-model-name&lt;/code&gt; header and the token usage in the response, using the PRICES table in &lt;code&gt;bench/run.py&lt;/code&gt;. A turn with cached input, a model without a PRICES entry, or a response without the header or token counts stays unpriced instead of counting as zero. Add a PRICES entry when you add a model to a profile, and check &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock pricing&lt;/a&gt; for current prices. A few runs on two tasks demonstrate the workflow. They aren’t enough to draw conclusions about cost or quality.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A group shows n/a with the reason when any of its runs is unpriced, and COST/SOLVED shows &lt;code&gt;-&lt;/code&gt; when no run was solved. To see where the cost went, check the turns: the terminal prints the model that served each turn, and the transcript under &lt;code&gt;bench/results/&lt;/code&gt; records it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To compare whole conversations, &lt;code&gt;bench/multiturn.py&lt;/code&gt; runs an agentic loop over each coding task, with the project files in the first prompt and the test output fed back after every edit. It also runs four scripted chats of seven turns that alternate simple turns with design, debugging, and code-writing turns, and it replays the full history on every turn. Turns with a cheap check (expected text, a word limit, bullets, or assertions against the returned function) record whether the check passed. The design responses aren’t graded.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;python3 bench/multiturn.py --tasks slugify --scripts backend api --max-spend 2&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;--max-conv-cost&lt;/code&gt; option (default 1 USD) caps each conversation, and &lt;code&gt;--max-spend&lt;/code&gt; stops the runner from starting new conversations. The summary shows, for each model group, the coding conversations that passed, the chat checks that passed, and the estimated cost per coding conversation, per chat, and in total.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The runners execute model-written Python on your machine. The subprocess that runs the tests has no credentials in its environment, but it isn’t an operating system sandbox: the code can read any file that your user can read and can reach the network. Use a disposable development environment with no long-lived AWS credentials on disk, and protect the files under &lt;code&gt;bench/results/&lt;/code&gt;, which contain prompts, model responses, tool output, and generated code.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="interpret-your-results"&gt;Interpret your results&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The sample’s tasks are small and illustrative. To decide whether routing fits your workload, replace or extend them with tasks that represent your own code base, and keep the following in mind as you read the runner’s output.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Start with SOLVED, then read COST/SOLVED –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;A group that solves fewer tasks can show a lower COST/TASK and still cost more per solved task, because failed runs add cost without adding solved tasks. Compare the routed group’s COST/SOLVED with each baseline group’s only after you’ve compared how many tasks each group solved.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Look at the hard tasks separately –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Aggregate numbers can hide a single task that drives the result. Break results down by task. Stage decides from failures in the history, so it can’t route a hard task to the capable candidate in advance. An escalated turn also replays the whole failed attempt, which can make a recovery more expensive than a run that started on the capable candidate. If a few hard tasks dominate your cost, consider routing them to a dedicated model group, or choose an efficient candidate that solves more of them.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Run enough repeats –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Model outputs vary from run to run. With only a handful of runs per group, a difference of one or two solved tasks can be noise. Increase &lt;code&gt;--repeats&lt;/code&gt; until the differences you care about are stable, and apply a statistical test, such as Fisher’s exact test for solve rates, before you act on a gap.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Account for the turn limit –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;A low &lt;code&gt;--max-turns&lt;/code&gt; value penalizes a model that explores before it edits, because it leaves fewer turns to repair a failing edit. Set the limit to match how your agents run in practice.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Account for output caps –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;If a group’s replies hit &lt;code&gt;max_tokens&lt;/code&gt;, the measured cost is a lower bound, and the shortened replies also shorten the history that later turns replay. Check the transcripts for truncated replies before you compare chat costs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Treat costs as estimates –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;The runners estimate cost from token usage and the PRICES table, not from your bill. Prompt caching, batch inference, and pricing changes all affect your actual cost. Confirm with &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock pricing&lt;/a&gt; and your AWS Cost and Usage Reports.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Test the settings you plan to run –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Results apply only to the models, profile settings, and tasks that you measured. If you change a candidate, the Stage settings, or switch to the Random profile, measure again.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use these readings to choose among three options for each workload: the routed group, a single-model group, or a profile with different candidates or settings. Rerun the same measurement when you change models, prompts, or agent behavior.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, delete the stack. The AWS CDK needs an &lt;code&gt;allowedCidr&lt;/code&gt; value to synthesize the app, but the value doesn’t matter here:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;npx cdk destroy -c allowedCidr=203.0.113.0/24&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;CloudFormation deletes the master key secret with no recovery window, so copy the key first if you need it. The gateway image stays in the AWS CDK bootstrap repository in Amazon ECR.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we showed how to deploy LiteLLM with the NVIDIA NeMo Switchyard routing plugin on AWS, configure Stage and Random routing across models on Amazon Bedrock, and measure cost per solved task for a routed model group alongside single-model baselines. Because the total cost of an agentic task depends on turns, history, and retries, price per token alone isn’t a reliable guide. Measuring cost per solved task on your own tasks gives you the evidence to choose a configuration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, deploy the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-route-coding-agent-requests-Amazon-Bedrock-nemo-switchyard" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;sample repository&lt;/a&gt; in a development account, and measure Stage routing and single-model baselines on tasks from your own workload. To learn more, see the &lt;a href="https://github.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/Switchyard/blob/main/examples/litellm/README.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Switchyard LiteLLM plugin guide&lt;/a&gt; and the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/what-is-bedrock.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock User Guide&lt;/a&gt;. Share your questions in the comments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt;
