			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Architecture patterns for fast session startup on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/architecture-patterns-for-fast-session-startup-on-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-runtime/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Evandro Franco]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 13 Nov 2026 22:02:39 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Best Practices]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Expert (400)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">31a4c32eac66b0c9392f69ee418d5e09aecaf4e5</guid>

					<description>Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime V2 snapshots your initialized environment and restores it for each new session, so session start no longer grows with image size. This post explains how V2 starts a session, how to measure session start time, and four patterns to keep work off the first request.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Agents today review code, process claims, and run unattended for hours. Many also sit behind interactive experiences where a person waits for the first response. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/agents-tools-runtime.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime&lt;/a&gt; gives these agents a fully managed, serverless environment: each session runs in its own isolated microVM, the platform scales to zero when there’s no work, and you only pay for the resources your sessions consume.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/the-new-agentcore-runtime-elastic-optimized-and-consistently-fast-starts/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore runtime&lt;/a&gt; with &lt;code&gt;platformVersion&lt;/code&gt; set to “V2” changes how sessions start. Instead of initializing your container for every session, it prepares your environment once, snapshots it, and restores that snapshot for each new session. Session start times are faster and more consistent. V2 also reclaims memory your sessions don’t use, so your bill tracks what your agent uses.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For most agents, switching to V2 is all it takes. Some agents need restructuring to get the full benefit, and for chat and voice experiences even 2 seconds is too slow. This post explains how V2 starts a session, how to measure session start time, and how to keep work off the first request, including build hooks, which AgentCore runtime now supports. We cover four patterns, from changes to your agent’s code to starting sessions before users need them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="which-pattern-do-you-need"&gt;Which pattern do you need?&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Your situation&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What to do&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Section&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A new session in about 2 seconds is fine&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Switch to V2 and do all initialization before &lt;code&gt;app.run()&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Keep work off the first request&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Your agent needs large files, a CLI, or a browser early, and can’t hold them in its process&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Add a build hook to load and prime them while the snapshot is built&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Working with hooks&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;You need sub-second, and users engage before they send their first request (opening the app or a chat window)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Start the session in the background when the user engages&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Pre-warm on user engagement&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-v2-starts-a-session"&gt;How V2 starts a session&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore runtime runs each session in a new microVM. On V1, AgentCore pulls your container image from Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR), starts the container, and runs your initialization code before it can process the first request. The image pull dominates, so cold start grows with image size.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;V2 does that work once. When you create or update a runtime, AgentCore runtime starts your container in a fresh microVM and waits for the first healthy ping (&lt;code&gt;GET /ping&lt;/code&gt;), which must arrive within 120 seconds. It then clears the operating system’s file cache, to keep the snapshot small, freezes the microVM, and stores its memory and disk as a snapshot.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;This is a change from V1: Every create or update now takes a few minutes&lt;/strong&gt; while the snapshot is built.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, &lt;strong&gt;init&lt;/strong&gt; means everything your agent does before &lt;code&gt;/ping&lt;/code&gt; reports healthy. With the AgentCore Python SDK, that’s everything before &lt;code&gt;app.run()&lt;/code&gt;. If you run your own HTTP server, in &lt;code&gt;Node.js&lt;/code&gt; or another language, report healthy only after your initialization finishes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/13/ML-22099-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/13/ML-22099-1.png" alt="Build flow where the container starts once, reports healthy, then AgentCore clears the file cache, freezes the microVM, and stores a snapshot" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Build, once per create or update&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When a new session arrives, AgentCore restores the snapshot into a new microVM. Your process resumes exactly where it was frozen, with its imports, objects, and clients in place. Files from your image are loaded lazily: each block is fetched the first time a session reads it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/13/ML-22099-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/13/ML-22099-2.png" alt="Invoke flow where each new session restores the snapshot into a fresh microVM, the process resumes, and image files are fetched on first read" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Invoke, every new session&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Three facts follow from these flows, and the rest of this post builds on them:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Memory comes back whole.&lt;/strong&gt; Anything your process held at snapshot time, and anything in a RAM-backed file system such as &lt;code&gt;/dev/shm&lt;/code&gt;, is available at memory speed from the first access.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;File reads don’t carry over.&lt;/strong&gt; The file cache is cleared before the freeze, so reading a file during init doesn’t make it fast in a restored session.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Image files are fetched on first read.&lt;/strong&gt; The first read of a block comes from snapshot storage and is then cached closer to the host.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following table shows what that means for the work a typical agent does, depending on where it does it:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Work&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Done during init&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Done in the handler&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Import libraries&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;In memory when the session starts&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Read from the image in every new session&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Create clients (for example, &lt;code&gt;boto3&lt;/code&gt;)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Restored with endpoint and credential resolution done. Sockets reconnect on first use&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Built from scratch in every new session&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Open network connections&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Stale after the restore. Close them before the snapshot&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Opened fresh&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Load static data from the image and keep it in an object&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;In memory when the session starts&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Read from the image in every new session&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Read a file from the image without keeping the data&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;No effect, because the file cache is cleared&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Read from the image in every new session&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you know AWS Lambda SnapStart, this will feel familiar. Marc Brooker’s post on how SnapStart loads snapshots and the SnapStart priming strategies post are useful background.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Everything in the table assumes your agent can keep what it needs in its own process. That isn’t always possible. A CLI that runs as a separate process and exits, or a browser binary, is read during init and then gone, so a restored session fetches it from the image again. For these cases, you can add a build hook, covered in “Working with hooks.” Figure 3 shows where it runs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/13/ML-22099-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/13/ML-22099-3.png" alt="Build and invoke flow with hooks, where a memory warmup call runs before the snapshot and a disk warmup call runs after a test restore" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Build and invoke with hooks&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="measure-before-you-optimize"&gt;Measure before you optimize&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Latency in an agentic application isn’t one number. A request spends time in AgentCore runtime getting a session ready to run your code, then in your code: model calls, tool calls, sub-agents, and streaming the response back. You fix these in different ways, so start by finding out which one dominates.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore runtime publishes both as &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/observability-runtime-metrics.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch metrics&lt;/a&gt; in the &lt;code&gt;AWS/Bedrock-AgentCore&lt;/code&gt; namespace. &lt;code&gt;Latency&lt;/code&gt; is the time to first byte. &lt;code&gt;OverheadLatency&lt;/code&gt; is the part that belongs to the platform, such as starting the microVM and restoring the snapshot on a new session.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What you want&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;How to get it&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;TTFB (Time to first byte)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;Latency&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Runtime time&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;OverheadLatency&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Your code’s time&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;Latency&lt;/code&gt; − &lt;code&gt;OverheadLatency&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Init time&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Measured in your code at startup (see the following example). It isn’t part of any request on V2&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Init doesn’t appear in these metrics because on V2 it runs once, while the snapshot is built, and not in each session. It adds to how long a create or update takes to reach READY, not to request latency. To see how long it takes, log it once before &lt;code&gt;app.run()&lt;/code&gt;. The line appears in your runtime’s build logs:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import time
INIT_START = time.time()  # first line of your module
# imports, clients, data loading ...
print(f"init_ms={round((time.time() - INIT_START) * 1000)}")
app.run()&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;How to read the split:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;High &lt;code&gt;OverheadLatency&lt;/code&gt; on new sessions:&lt;/strong&gt; the time is in session start. Use the patterns later in this post to start sessions before users need them.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Your code’s time is much higher on the first request than on the second in the same session:&lt;/strong&gt; work that belongs in init is running in your handler. Add custom spans in AgentCore Observability (a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore) around tool setup, file loads, and late imports to find it, then move it into init.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Report percentiles such as P90 and P99, or trimmed means such as TM90, which averages the fastest 90% of requests and drops the slowest tail. A plain average hides the slow requests. &lt;code&gt;OverheadLatency&lt;/code&gt; covers requests to running sessions as well as new ones, so its distribution depends on how many of your requests start a new session. Compare runs with a similar mix.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Test at the rate of new sessions you expect in production, not with a handful of sessions after a deploy.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To include network time from your client, measure on the client as well. Time the invoke call, have your handler return how long it ran, and subtract. Both timestamps come from the same machine, so clock differences between the client and the microVM don’t matter. If you stream, very small events can be held back until a few hundred bytes of output build up, so time to first event can read higher than session start.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="keep-work-off-the-first-request"&gt;Keep work off the first request&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The usual cold start advice is to import lazily and keep initialization small. On V2, do the opposite: init runs once, when the snapshot is built, and work deferred to the handler is paid by every new session. This applies to &lt;em&gt;static&lt;/em&gt; work: imports, clients, and data from your image. Dynamic data can’t be snapshotted. The next section covers it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The agents that notice most&lt;/strong&gt; are the ones that read large files from their image at request time: weights for a small local model, embeddings, datasets, large libraries, a browser binary for automation, or a CLI toolchain that a coding agent runs as subprocesses. Reading many small files, as compiling a TypeScript project does, has the same effect. An agent that imports a few libraries and calls a hosted model mostly needs to get its imports and clients right.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="how-to-restructure-your-agent"&gt;How to restructure your agent&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Create objects during init, not only imports.&lt;/strong&gt; Creating an object loads the modules it depends on and keeps them in active memory. Initialize local tools that use external libraries during init too, not when the agent first calls them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Create your clients during init.&lt;/strong&gt; Endpoint resolution, credential resolution, and the client’s modules all come back with the snapshot. Sockets don’t, but &lt;code&gt;boto3&lt;/code&gt; clients reconnect cleanly on the first call. Some other network clients first try the stale socket, which adds hundreds of milliseconds, so close their connections before the snapshot.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Keep static data in memory,&lt;/strong&gt; such as prompts, skill front matter, configuration, and small indexes. Hold the data in an object, not only a read of it:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;INDEX = pathlib.Path("/opt/data/index.bin").read_bytes()  # during init: keep the bytes&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Keep tools running instead of starting them per request.&lt;/strong&gt; A subprocess launched in the handler reads its binary and libraries from the image in every new session. A long-running helper started during init, such as a language server or a worker process with its imports done, comes back as part of process memory. Talk to it over a pipe or socket.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Copy files you can’t hold in memory to a RAM-backed disk.&lt;/strong&gt; Toolchains like &lt;code&gt;tsc&lt;/code&gt; run as separate programs that read their own files. Copy them to &lt;code&gt;/dev/shm&lt;/code&gt; or a ramfs mount during init so their reads come from memory.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Ship compiled bytecode.&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;code&gt;uv pip install&lt;/code&gt; doesn’t compile by default, so late imports also compile from source. Set &lt;code&gt;UV_COMPILE_BYTECODE=1&lt;/code&gt; or use &lt;code&gt;pip install --compile&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The example below builds the agent, its model, and its clients during init, so every session inherits them:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import json, pathlib
import boto3
import pandas as pd  # import during init, including rarely used libraries
from strands import Agent
from strands.models import BedrockModel
from bedrock_agentcore.runtime import BedrockAgentCoreApp

MODEL_ID = "us.anthropic.claude-haiku-5-5"

# Init: runs once, before the snapshot. Every new session inherits these objects.
PROMPTS = json.loads(pathlib.Path("prompts.json").read_text())
ddb = boto3.client("dynamodb")
agent = Agent(model=BedrockModel(model_id=MODEL_ID), system_prompt=PROMPTS["system"])

app = BedrockAgentCoreApp()

@app.entrypoint
def invoke(payload):
    return agent(payload["prompt"])

app.run()  # /ping answers only after this line&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The trade-off:&lt;/strong&gt; time to first byte on a new session is the time to start the microVM and restore the snapshot, plus the time your handler takes. Moving work into init shortens the handler, but anything you hold in memory makes the snapshot larger. You also pay for that held memory in every session. Measure end-to-end session start before and after the change, so you know you’ve reduced latency and not only moved it from the handler to the restore.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="reference-card"&gt;Reference card&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Data&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Approach&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Large files the first request needs, such as a model, a CLI, or a browser&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Hold them in memory during init, or prime them with a build hook&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Large reference data read selectively&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Mount Amazon S3 Files or Amazon Elastic File System (Amazon EFS) during init, through an access point (requires virtual private cloud (VPC) mode)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Files the agent rarely reads&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Leave them on the image, or move them to Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3), S3 Files, or Amazon EFS&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Files the session creates&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;No change needed&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="working-with-hooks"&gt;Working with hooks&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A build hook builds on init and doesn’t replace it. Do what you can in init and use the hook for what init can’t cover. You expose an HTTP endpoint in your container, and AgentCore runtime sends it a &lt;code&gt;POST&lt;/code&gt; request twice while it builds your runtime:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Before the snapshot.&lt;/strong&gt; On the build microVM, after your agent reports healthy and before the freeze. Anything your agent loads here and keeps in memory is part of the snapshot, so every session starts with it.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;After a test restore.&lt;/strong&gt; On a separate microVM that restores the snapshot to check it. Nothing from this call is snapshotted. Use it to read the files your sessions will need, which shapes what restored sessions fetch first.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;X-Amzn-Bedrock-AgentCore-Runtime-Warmup-Type&lt;/code&gt; header tells your agent which call it’s handling: &lt;code&gt;memory&lt;/code&gt; for the first, &lt;code&gt;disk&lt;/code&gt; for the second. The two calls share 120 seconds, so the &lt;code&gt;disk&lt;/code&gt; call gets whatever time the &lt;code&gt;memory&lt;/code&gt; call leaves. Return a &lt;code&gt;2xx&lt;/code&gt; response only after the work is done. Any other response, a failed connection, or running out of time fails the create or update, and AgentCore runtime doesn’t retry. The &lt;code&gt;build-logs-&lt;/code&gt; streams in your runtime’s log group show which call failed and why.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Hooks are available on V2 runtimes. Configure them with &lt;code&gt;hooksConfiguration&lt;/code&gt; when you create or update the runtime:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;client = boto3.client("bedrock-agentcore-control")
client.create_agent_runtime(
    agentRuntimeName="my_agent",
    agentRuntimeArtifact={"containerConfiguration": {"containerUri": IMAGE_URI}},
    roleArn=ROLE_ARN,
    networkConfiguration={"networkMode": "PUBLIC"},
    platformVersion="V2",
    hooksConfiguration={
        "build": {
            "warmup": {
                "action": {"httpPost": {"path": "/warmup", "port": 8080}}
            }
        }
    },
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The port is optional and defaults to your agent’s serving port. If you set it, use a port from 8000 to 12000 that isn’t reserved by the platform. The path can’t match a route AgentCore runtime already uses on that port, such as &lt;code&gt;POST /invocations&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;GET /ping&lt;/code&gt;. &lt;code&gt;UpdateAgentRuntime&lt;/code&gt; replaces the whole hook configuration, so include &lt;code&gt;hooksConfiguration&lt;/code&gt; in every update where you want to keep the hook.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the agent, handle both call types on the same endpoint:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import asyncio
from fastapi import FastAPI, Request, Response

app = FastAPI()

# Files that each session reads when it starts.
STARTUP_FILES = ["/app/models/tokenizer.json", "/app/config/agent.json"]

def load_shared_state():
    # Load what every session needs into memory, such as heavy imports,
    # model weights, and caches.
    ...

def read_startup_files():
    # Read the files that a session reads when it starts, so that new
    # sessions can use them faster.
    for path in STARTUP_FILES:
        with open(path, "rb") as f:
            while f.read(1024 * 1024):
                pass

@app.get("/ping")
def ping():
    return {"status": "Healthy"}

@app.post("/warmup")
async def warmup(request: Request):
    # Header lookup in Starlette and FastAPI is case-insensitive.
    warmup_type = request.headers.get(
        "X-Amzn-Bedrock-AgentCore-Runtime-Warmup-Type", ""
    ).lower()
    # Run the work in a worker thread so that /ping keeps answering.
    if warmup_type == "memory":
        await asyncio.to_thread(load_shared_state)
    elif warmup_type == "disk":
        await asyncio.to_thread(read_startup_files)
    # Respond only after the work is done.
    return Response(status_code=200)

@app.post("/invocations")
async def invoke(request: Request):
    payload = await request.json()
    # Per-request work runs here on every session.
    return {"response": payload.get("prompt", "")}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For the full configuration reference, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/runtime-lifecycle-hooks.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Configure lifecycle hooks for AgentCore runtime&lt;/a&gt;. For a complete code example, see the &lt;a href="https://github.com/evandrofranco/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/02-host-your-agent/01-runtime/03-advanced/17-strands-decider-hooks" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;sample code&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="snapshot-pitfalls-what-must-stay-in-the-handler"&gt;Snapshot pitfalls: What must stay in the handler&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Everything created during init is frozen into the snapshot until your next deploy, and every session starts with the same copy. For most objects that’s the point. For the values below, it’s a bug, and for random values, a security issue.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Load dynamic data in the handler, once per session.&lt;/strong&gt; Tool catalogs from AgentCore Gateway (a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore), feature flags, and price lists change after you deploy. Load them in the handler and cache them at module level, so each session fetches them once. Sessions can last up to 8 hours, so give the cache an expiry:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;_tools, _loaded_at = None, 0.0

def get_tools():
    global _tools, _loaded_at
    if _tools is None or time.time() - _loaded_at &amp;gt; 300:  # wall clock: refresh after 5 minutes
        _tools, _loaded_at = load_gateway_tools(), time.time()
    return _tools&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Call &lt;code&gt;get_tools()&lt;/code&gt; from the handler and pass the result to the agent you created during init.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Create per-session values in the handler.&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Value&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Where to create it&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Why&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Random values, nonces, secrets, keys&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;In the handler, with &lt;code&gt;secrets&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;os.urandom()&lt;/code&gt;, or &lt;code&gt;uuid.uuid4()&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Library random state (Python’s &lt;code&gt;random&lt;/code&gt;, NumPy) created during init is identical in every session. Values from the operating system are unique&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Current and elapsed time&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;In the handler, with &lt;code&gt;time.time()&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;An init timestamp is fixed at snapshot time, and &lt;code&gt;time.monotonic()&lt;/code&gt; doesn’t count the time the snapshot spent stored&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Timers, rate limiters, TTL caches&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Use wall-clock time, or reset them on the first request&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Anything measuring elapsed time with a monotonic clock sees almost no gap across the restore&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Credentials and tokens you fetch yourself&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Refresh them when they expire&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Values loaded during init can expire before a session starts&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Know the limits.&lt;/strong&gt; A session ends when its idle timeout expires (&lt;code&gt;idleRuntimeSessionTimeout&lt;/code&gt;: 15 minutes by default, from 60 seconds up to 8 hours), when it reaches its maximum lifetime (&lt;code&gt;maxLifetime&lt;/code&gt;: 8 hours by default and at most), or when you stop it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="sub-second-start-the-session-before-the-user-needs-it"&gt;Sub-second: Start the session before the user needs it&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Suppose your new session takes about 2 seconds to start, including all the timing breakdown covered in the preceding sections. A session that’s already running answers in about 200 ms. For a chat or voice experience, the way to sub-second is to make sure the session has started before the user’s first request arrives. There are two ways to do that.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="pre-warm-on-user-engagement"&gt;Pre-warm on user engagement&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Most interactive applications get a signal before the first prompt: the user opens the app, a chat window, or a support page. Use it. Generate a session ID, send the agent a warm-up request in the background, and keep the session ID with the conversation. By the time the user has typed their first message, the session is running.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# Backend, when the user opens the chat (don't block the UI on it)
session_id = f"chat-{uuid.uuid4().hex}"  # session IDs need at least 33 characters
agentcore.invoke_agent_runtime(agentRuntimeArn=ARN, runtimeSessionId=session_id,
                               payload=json.dumps({"action": "prime"}).encode())
save_conversation(user_id, session_id)  # every later turn reuses this session ID

# Agent handler: answer the warm-up before any model call
@app.entrypoint
def invoke(payload):
    if payload.get("action") == "prime":
        return {"primed": True}
    return agent(payload["prompt"])&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This pattern needs no extra infrastructure and fully hides session start whenever users take more than about 2 seconds to send their first message. It costs a session for each user who engages but never sends a prompt. A short idle timeout keeps that small.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On V2, AgentCore runtime moves most of the work of starting a session to deployment time. Your container starts once, and every new session restores the result: memory comes back whole, and files are fetched on first read.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To keep session startup time near 2 seconds, do your static work during init, hold the data the first request needs in memory, and keep dynamic and per-session values in the handler. For files your process can’t hold, add a build hook. To get under a second, start the session before the user needs it: on user engagement when you get a signal.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, set &lt;code&gt;platformVersion&lt;/code&gt; to V2 when you create or update a runtime, then review &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/runtime-v2-optimize.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Optimize your agent for AgentCore runtime V2&lt;/a&gt;. For questions about your workload, reach out to your AWS account team.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>OpenFold3 on Amazon SageMaker JumpStart: Predict biomolecular structures</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/openfold3-on-amazon-sagemaker-jumpstart-predict-biomolecular-structures/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Malav Shastri]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 13 Nov 2026 21:53:40 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker JumpStart]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">4dea8ce77a40d45986adf65199b169c60e84bcc1</guid>

					<description>Deploy OpenFold3-preview from Amazon SageMaker JumpStart to predict 3D structures of proteins, DNA, RNA, and ligand complexes in your own AWS account. The post covers asynchronous inference, multi-GPU parallelism, bringing your own MSAs for accuracy and privacy, and scaling to zero to reduce cost.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;You can deploy &lt;strong&gt;OpenFold3-preview&lt;/strong&gt; from &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/jumpstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/a&gt;. The OpenFold Consortium and the AlQuraishi Lab at Columbia University developed &lt;a href="https://github.com/aqlaboratory/openfold-3" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OpenFold3&lt;/a&gt; as an open source biomolecular structure prediction model. With a few lines of code, you can typically deploy OpenFold3 into your own AWS account. You can then predict the 3D structures of proteins, DNA, RNA, small-molecule ligands, and the complexes they form. The endpoint runs with network isolation, which you can use to help keep your sequences within your AWS account.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Computational drug discovery teams now use structure prediction as a core part of their work. Teams use it in target identification, antibody and protein engineering, and structure-based virtual screening. Teams have found running these models challenging. You need GPU infrastructure, a working CUDA and PyTorch stack, large model checkpoints, and a serving layer that handles requests that take minutes rather than milliseconds. SageMaker JumpStart can handle much of that work for you. OpenFold3 runs in an AWS-maintained container on a managed Amazon SageMaker AI endpoint. The endpoint is designed to handle request queuing and GPU warmup, and you can add autoscaling.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we show you how to:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Deploy OpenFold3-preview on a SageMaker &lt;strong&gt;asynchronous inference&lt;/strong&gt; endpoint.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Submit structure prediction jobs and interpret the results, including predicted structures and confidence scores.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Run several predictions in parallel on &lt;strong&gt;multi-GPU instances&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Supply your own &lt;strong&gt;multiple sequence alignments (MSAs)&lt;/strong&gt; to improve prediction accuracy while helping keep your sequences private.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Help reduce costs by &lt;strong&gt;scaling your endpoint to zero&lt;/strong&gt; when it’s idle.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="overview-of-openfold3"&gt;Overview of OpenFold3&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;OpenFold3 is an all-atom structure prediction model. It predicts the joint 3D structure of a set of molecules from their sequences. It isn’t limited to single protein chains: It can fold multi-chain protein complexes, protein–nucleic acid assemblies, and protein–ligand complexes in one prediction.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Attribute&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Details&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Developer&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;OpenFold Consortium / AlQuraishi Lab, Columbia University&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Model type&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;All-atom biomolecular structure prediction&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Inputs&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Protein, DNA, and RNA sequences. Small-molecule ligands as SMILES&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Outputs&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Predicted structures in mmCIF format, with confidence metrics (pLDDT, PAE, PDE)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Version&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;OpenFold3 0.4.1, &lt;code&gt;of3-p2-155k&lt;/code&gt; checkpoint&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;License&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Apache 2.0&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;JumpStart model ID&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;huggingface-proteinfolding-openfold3&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Supported instances&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;ml.g6.2xlarge&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;ml.g6.4xlarge&lt;/code&gt; (default), &lt;code&gt;ml.g6.12xlarge&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The OpenFold3 developers release its code and model weights under the Apache 2.0 license, and they publish the training data and methods. Research organizations and pharmaceutical companies can inspect the model, fine-tune it, and build on it under the terms of that license. The developers describe OpenFold3-preview as a research preview.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="use-cases"&gt;Use cases&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Target and complex structure prediction&lt;/strong&gt; – Predict structures for targets that have no experimental structure, including multi-chain assemblies and protein–nucleic acid complexes.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Protein–ligand co-folding&lt;/strong&gt; – Fold a protein together with a small molecule supplied as SMILES to generate binding-pose hypotheses for structure-based design.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Antibody and protein engineering&lt;/strong&gt; – Evaluate designed variants by predicting their structures and comparing confidence across candidates.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;High-throughput screening pipelines&lt;/strong&gt; – Queue large batches of prediction jobs against an autoscaling endpoint and process the results directly from Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3).&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="why-openfold3-uses-asynchronous-inference"&gt;Why OpenFold3 uses asynchronous inference&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Most JumpStart models, such as large language models and embedding models, deploy to &lt;strong&gt;real-time&lt;/strong&gt; endpoints. A real-time endpoint returns its response within a single request, and that request is capped at 60 seconds. Structure prediction works differently. Folding time grows with the number and length of the chains in a query, and real-world proteins and complexes often take several minutes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For this reason, OpenFold3 on JumpStart runs on &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/async-inference.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker Asynchronous Inference&lt;/a&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;You upload the request to Amazon S3 and call &lt;code&gt;InvokeEndpointAsync&lt;/code&gt;, which returns immediately.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The endpoint queues the request and processes it, with an invocation timeout of up to 1 hour.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The endpoint writes the result, or the error details, to an S3 location you choose. You can poll for it or get a notification through Amazon Simple Notification Service (Amazon SNS).&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two properties of asynchronous endpoints suit scientific workloads especially well. First, they queue bursts of requests internally, so you can submit a large batch of sequences without building your own job queue. Second, they can &lt;strong&gt;scale to zero instances&lt;/strong&gt; when the queue is empty, which helps you avoid paying for idle instances between experiments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To follow this walkthrough, you need:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account, and a SageMaker AI execution role for the endpoint. Following least privilege, this AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) role needs read and write access only to the S3 prefix that you use for requests and results. For more information, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-roles.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;How to use SageMaker AI execution roles&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;IAM permissions for the identity that runs the code: to create, describe, invoke, and delete the model, endpoint configuration, and endpoint, plus &lt;code&gt;iam:PassRole&lt;/code&gt; for the execution role. For the optional scale-to-zero section, it also needs AWS Application Auto Scaling and Amazon CloudWatch alarm permissions. In production, scope these permissions to your resources rather than using broad managed policies such as &lt;code&gt;AmazonSageMakerFullAccess&lt;/code&gt;. For more information, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/async-inference-create-endpoint-prerequisites.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Complete the prerequisites&lt;/a&gt; for asynchronous inference.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Access to &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/studio/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker Studio&lt;/a&gt;, a SageMaker notebook instance, or a local IDE.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The &lt;a href="https://sagemaker.readthedocs.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker Python SDK&lt;/a&gt; v3 and py3Dmol, which you can install with &lt;code&gt;pip install "sagemaker&amp;gt;=3,&amp;lt;4" py3Dmol&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Service quota for at least one &lt;code&gt;ml.g6.4xlarge&lt;/code&gt; endpoint instance in your AWS Region. You also need quota for &lt;code&gt;ml.g6.12xlarge&lt;/code&gt; if you follow the multi-GPU section. You can check your quotas and request increases in the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/servicequotas/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Service Quotas console&lt;/a&gt; under &lt;strong&gt;Amazon SageMaker&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Important:&lt;/strong&gt; SageMaker endpoints are billed per instance-hour while they’re running, including while they’re idle. To avoid unexpected charges, follow the &lt;strong&gt;Clean up&lt;/strong&gt; section, or configure scale-to-zero as described later in this post. For pricing details, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI pricing&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="discover-openfold3-in-sagemaker-jumpstart"&gt;Discover OpenFold3 in SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open SageMaker Studio and choose &lt;strong&gt;JumpStart&lt;/strong&gt; in the navigation pane.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Search for &lt;strong&gt;OpenFold3&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open the model card to review the model description, license, and supported instance types.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open the &lt;strong&gt;example notebook&lt;/strong&gt; from the model card. It contains the complete asynchronous deployment walkthrough shown in this post.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="deploy-openfold3-with-the-sagemaker-python-sdk"&gt;Deploy OpenFold3 with the SageMaker Python SDK&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;First, build the model from its JumpStart configuration and define an &lt;code&gt;AsyncInferenceConfig&lt;/code&gt;. The output and failure paths are the S3 locations where the endpoint writes results and error details.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import json, time, uuid
import boto3
from sagemaker.core.helper.session_helper import Session
from sagemaker.core.inference_config import AsyncInferenceConfig
from sagemaker.core.jumpstart.configs import JumpStartConfig
from sagemaker.core.training.configs import Compute
from sagemaker.serve.model_builder import ModelBuilder

session = Session()
bucket = session.default_bucket()          # sagemaker-{region}-{account-id}
prefix = "openfold3-async"

model_builder = ModelBuilder.from_jumpstart_config(
    jumpstart_config=JumpStartConfig(
        model_id="huggingface-proteinfolding-openfold3",
        accept_eula=True,
    ),
    compute=Compute(instance_type="ml.g6.4xlarge"),
)
model = model_builder.build()

async_config = AsyncInferenceConfig(
    output_path=f"s3://{bucket}/{prefix}/output",
    failure_path=f"s3://{bucket}/{prefix}/failure",
)

endpoint = model_builder.deploy(inference_config=async_config)
endpoint_name = endpoint.endpoint_name
print(f"Deployed asynchronous endpoint: {endpoint_name}")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Setting &lt;code&gt;accept_eula=True&lt;/code&gt; confirms that you’ve reviewed and accepted the model’s license terms, which is required to deploy this model. If you run this code outside SageMaker Studio, also pass your SageMaker execution role to &lt;code&gt;ModelBuilder.from_jumpstart_config&lt;/code&gt; with &lt;code&gt;role_arn="&amp;lt;role-arn&amp;gt;"&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The endpoint stores your requests and results in Amazon S3, and they contain your sequences and predicted structures. For proprietary sequences, consider using a dedicated S3 bucket or prefix with server-side encryption using an AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS) key that you manage. To encrypt results with your key, pass its ARN to &lt;code&gt;AsyncInferenceConfig&lt;/code&gt; as &lt;code&gt;kms_key_id&lt;/code&gt;. Restrict access with a bucket policy that allows only the roles that run this workflow, and add an S3 Lifecycle rule to expire request and result objects that you don’t need to keep. For more information, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/security-best-practices.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Security best practices for Amazon S3&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deployment typically completes within minutes. This includes a warmup step in which the container runs a short prediction on each GPU, so that the GPUs are warmed up when the endpoint reaches &lt;code&gt;InService&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tip:&lt;/strong&gt; G6 capacity can be limited in some Regions. If a deployment fails with &lt;code&gt;InsufficientInstanceCapacity&lt;/code&gt;, retry later, choose a different supported instance size, or deploy in another AWS Region where the model is available.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="run-a-structure-prediction"&gt;Run a structure prediction&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A request is a JSON document with one or more named &lt;strong&gt;queries&lt;/strong&gt;, and each query lists its &lt;strong&gt;chains&lt;/strong&gt;. All chains in a query are folded together as one complex. Separate queries are folded independently.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each chain has these fields:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;molecule_type&lt;/code&gt; – &lt;code&gt;protein&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;dna&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;rna&lt;/code&gt;, or &lt;code&gt;ligand&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;chain_ids&lt;/code&gt; – a list of chain identifiers, such as &lt;code&gt;["A"]&lt;/code&gt;. To specify identical copies, such as a homodimer, use &lt;code&gt;["A", "B"]&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;sequence&lt;/code&gt; – the residue sequence, for protein, DNA, and RNA chains.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;smiles&lt;/code&gt; – a SMILES string, used for ligand chains instead of &lt;code&gt;sequence&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following example folds human ubiquitin, a well-characterized 76-residue protein:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;s3 = boto3.client("s3")
smr = boto3.client("sagemaker-runtime")

payload = {
    "queries": {
        "ubiquitin": {
            "chains": [
                {
                    "molecule_type": "protein",
                    "chain_ids": ["A"],
                    "sequence": "MQIFVKTLTGKTITLEVEPSDTIENVKAKIQDKEGIPPDQQRLIFAGKQLEDGRTLSDYNIQKESTLHLVLRLRGG",
                }
            ]
        }
    }
}

# 1. Stage the request in S3
input_key = f"{prefix}/input/{uuid.uuid4().hex}.json"
s3.put_object(Bucket=bucket, Key=input_key, Body=json.dumps(payload).encode())

# 2. Submit asynchronously -- returns immediately
resp = smr.invoke_endpoint_async(
    EndpointName=endpoint_name,
    ContentType="application/json",
    InputLocation=f"s3://{bucket}/{input_key}",
    InvocationTimeoutSeconds=3600,
)
output_location, failure_location = resp["OutputLocation"], resp["FailureLocation"]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Then poll S3 for either the result or a failure record:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;def split(uri):
    b, _, k = uri.removeprefix("s3://").partition("/")
    return b, k

result = None
for _ in range(120):                                    # up to ~30 minutes
    for loc, is_failure in ((output_location, False), (failure_location, True)):
        try:
            body = s3.get_object(Bucket=split(loc)[0], Key=split(loc)[1])["Body"].read()
        except s3.exceptions.NoSuchKey:
            continue
        if is_failure:
            raise RuntimeError(body.decode())
        result = json.loads(body)
    if result:
        break
    time.sleep(15)

if result is None:
    raise TimeoutError(f"No result yet; check {output_location}")
if result.get("status") == "error":
    raise RuntimeError(result["error"])&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A request with invalid input, such as a malformed chain or an unsupported MSA name, still produces a result object, but with &lt;code&gt;"status": "error"&lt;/code&gt; and an &lt;code&gt;error&lt;/code&gt; message instead of structures. The last check surfaces that message.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Prediction time grows with the size of the input. In our tests on ml.g6.4xlarge, smaller proteins completed more quickly than larger proteins.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="interpret-the-results"&gt;Interpret the results&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The response contains a &lt;code&gt;status&lt;/code&gt;, a list of predicted &lt;code&gt;structures&lt;/code&gt; in mmCIF format, and their matching &lt;code&gt;confidences&lt;/code&gt;. By default, OpenFold3 generates five diffusion samples per query, so each query returns five candidate structures. Each sample comes with two confidence files: a detailed one (&lt;code&gt;*_confidences.json&lt;/code&gt;) and a summary (&lt;code&gt;*_confidences_aggregated.json&lt;/code&gt;). The summary includes a &lt;code&gt;sample_ranking_score&lt;/code&gt; that you can use to pick the best sample.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import os
os.makedirs("structures", exist_ok=True)
for s in result["structures"]:
    path = os.path.join("structures", os.path.basename(s["filename"]))
    with open(path, "w") as f:
        f.write(s["content"])
    print("saved", path, f'({s["size_bytes"]/1024:.0f} KB)')

summaries = {c["filename"]: c["data"] for c in result["confidences"]
             if c["filename"].endswith("_aggregated.json")}
for name, m in sorted(summaries.items(), key=lambda kv: -kv[1]["sample_ranking_score"]):
    print(f'{name}: ranking={m["sample_ranking_score"]:.2f} pLDDT={m["avg_plddt"]:.1f} '
          f'pTM={m["ptm"]:.2f} ipTM={m["iptm"]:.2f}')&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can open the &lt;code&gt;.cif&lt;/code&gt; files in any structure viewer, such as &lt;a href="https://pymol.org/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;PyMOL&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://www.cgl.ucsf.edu/chimerax/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;ChimeraX&lt;/a&gt;, or &lt;a href="https://molstar.org/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Mol*&lt;/a&gt;. To view a structure inline in a notebook, use &lt;code&gt;py3Dmol&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import py3Dmol   # pip install py3Dmol
view = py3Dmol.view(width=600, height=450)
view.addModel(result["structures"][0]["content"], "cif")
# pLDDT is stored in the B-factor field: red = low confidence, blue = high
view.setStyle({"cartoon": {"colorscheme": {"prop": "b", "gradient": "roygb", "min": 50, "max": 90}}})
view.zoomTo(); view.show()&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/12/ML-22226-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/12/ML-22226-1.png" alt="OpenFold3 predicted structure of ubiquitin colored by per-atom pLDDT confidence, from red for low to blue for high" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: OpenFold3’s predicted structure of ubiquitin, rendered with the preceding code and colored by per-atom confidence (pLDDT). All figures in this post were rendered with 3Dmol.js (Rego and Koes, &lt;a href="http://dx.doi.org/10.1093/bioinformatics/btu829"&gt;Bioinformatics, 2015&lt;/a&gt;).&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The prediction shows ubiquitin’s compact fold: a mixed beta-sheet packed against a single alpha-helix. Most of the structure is blue (high confidence). Only the flexible C-terminal tail, at the top of the image, shifts toward yellow and red, which matches the tail’s known flexibility in experimental structures.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The confidence metrics tell you how much to trust each prediction:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;pLDDT (predicted local distance difference test)&lt;/strong&gt; – Per-atom confidence in the local structure, on a scale of 0–100. Higher is better, and values above about 70 generally indicate a reliable local structure. The summary file reports the average as &lt;code&gt;avg_plddt&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;pTM and ipTM (predicted TM-score and interface pTM)&lt;/strong&gt; – Overall confidence in the fold, and in how chains and ligands are positioned relative to each other, on a scale of 0–1. For complexes, ipTM is the key number. The summary file also reports &lt;code&gt;chain_pair_iptm&lt;/code&gt; for each pair of chains.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;PAE (predicted aligned error)&lt;/strong&gt; and &lt;strong&gt;PDE (predicted distance error)&lt;/strong&gt; – Per-residue-pair error estimates, in the detailed confidence file. Use them to judge the relative placement of domains and chains.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="fold-a-complex"&gt;Fold a complex&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To predict a complex, list several chains in the same query. The following example co-folds human carbonic anhydrase II with its catalytic zinc ion and the drug acetazolamide. This is a well-studied complex with experimental structures in the Protein Data Bank (for example, PDB 3HS4):&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;ca2_sequence = (
    "MSHHWGYGKHNGPEHWHKDFPIAKGERQSPVDIDTHTAKYDPSLKPLSVSYDQATSLRILNNGHAFNVEFDDSQDKAVLKGG"
    "PLDGTYRLIQFHFHWGSLDGQGSEHTVDKKKYAAELHLVHWNTKYGDFGKAVQQPDGLAVLGIFLKVGSAKPGLQKVVDVLDS"
    "IKTKGKSADFTNFDPRGLLPESLDYWTYPGSLTTPPLLECVTWIVLKEPISVSSEQVLKFRKLNFNGEGEPEELMVDNWRPAQ"
    "PLKNRQIKASFK"
)

payload = {
    "queries": {
        "ca2_acetazolamide": {
            "chains": [
                {"molecule_type": "protein", "chain_ids": ["A"], "sequence": ca2_sequence},
                {"molecule_type": "ligand",  "chain_ids": ["B"], "smiles": "[Zn+2]"},
                {"molecule_type": "ligand",  "chain_ids": ["C"], "smiles": "CC(=O)Nc1nnc(s1)S(=O)(=O)N"},
            ]
        }
    }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Protein sequences in these examples are from UniProt (&lt;a href="https://www.uniprot.org/uniprotkb/P0CG48" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;P0CG48&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://www.uniprot.org/uniprotkb/P00918" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;P00918&lt;/a&gt;). You submit and read this request the same way as the ubiquitin example. For complexes like this one, prediction quality depends heavily on providing an MSA, as the next sections show.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="run-predictions-in-parallel-on-multi-gpu-instances"&gt;Run predictions in parallel on multi-GPU instances&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A single OpenFold3 prediction runs on one GPU, so a multi-GPU instance doesn’t make an individual prediction faster. Instead, you can run several predictions at once. On a multi-GPU instance such as &lt;code&gt;ml.g6.12xlarge&lt;/code&gt;, which has four GPUs, the container assigns each concurrent request its own GPU. To use all the GPUs, set &lt;code&gt;max_concurrent_invocations_per_instance&lt;/code&gt; to the number of GPUs on the instance (four, for &lt;code&gt;ml.g6.12xlarge&lt;/code&gt;):&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;multi_gpu_builder = ModelBuilder.from_jumpstart_config(
    jumpstart_config=JumpStartConfig(
        model_id="huggingface-proteinfolding-openfold3",
        accept_eula=True,
    ),
    compute=Compute(instance_type="ml.g6.12xlarge"),
)
multi_gpu_model = multi_gpu_builder.build()

async_config = AsyncInferenceConfig(
    output_path=f"s3://{bucket}/{prefix}/output",
    failure_path=f"s3://{bucket}/{prefix}/failure",
    max_concurrent_invocations_per_instance=4,   # = number of GPUs on the instance
)

multi_gpu_endpoint = multi_gpu_builder.deploy(inference_config=async_config)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This creates a second endpoint, so remember to delete it as well when you’re done (see &lt;strong&gt;Clean up&lt;/strong&gt;).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In our tests, four concurrent predictions each ran on a separate GPU, with throughput gains that grew with protein size. For larger proteins, the cost per prediction that we measured on &lt;code&gt;ml.g6.12xlarge&lt;/code&gt;, based on On-Demand instance pricing, was close to that of &lt;code&gt;ml.g6.4xlarge&lt;/code&gt;. Your results depend on your inputs and Region. For pricing, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI pricing&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;code&gt;ml.g6.12xlarge&lt;/code&gt; capacity can be limited. If it isn’t available in your Region, you can increase throughput by scaling out across several &lt;code&gt;ml.g6.4xlarge&lt;/code&gt; instances with the autoscaling configuration described later in this post.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use the following table to choose an instance:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Workload&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Recommended instance&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Why&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Interactive exploration by a single user&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;ml.g6.2xlarge&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;ml.g6.4xlarge&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;One GPU handles one prediction at a time at the lowest hourly cost of the supported instances&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Team use or bursts of concurrent requests&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;ml.g6.12xlarge&lt;/code&gt; with &lt;code&gt;max_concurrent_invocations_per_instance=4&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Four predictions run in parallel instead of queuing&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;High-throughput screening&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;ml.g6.12xlarge&lt;/code&gt; with autoscaling&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Highest throughput per instance of the supported instances. Scales out with queue depth and back to zero&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="improve-prediction-accuracy-with-your-own-msas"&gt;Improve prediction accuracy with your own MSAs&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;OpenFold3 can take a &lt;strong&gt;multiple sequence alignment (MSA)&lt;/strong&gt; as an additional input for each protein chain. An MSA is a set of evolutionarily related sequences aligned to your query. Co-variation across those sequences signals which residues are likely to be in contact, so MSAs can substantially improve accuracy for many proteins and complexes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To help keep your sequences within your AWS account, the JumpStart endpoint runs with &lt;strong&gt;network isolation&lt;/strong&gt; and doesn’t call external MSA servers. Instead, it accepts &lt;strong&gt;precomputed MSAs&lt;/strong&gt; in the request. You generate alignments with the pipeline and sequence databases you already use, on infrastructure you control, and pass them to the endpoint. This design can help drug discovery programs that need to keep proprietary sequences private.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you don’t supply an MSA, the endpoint predicts from the sequence alone. That can work well for small, well-characterized proteins such as ubiquitin. For most real-world targets and complexes, include an MSA. The rest of this section shows how, and how much it improves results.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="bring-your-own-msa"&gt;Bring your own MSA&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each protein chain accepts a &lt;code&gt;precomputed_msa&lt;/code&gt; field: a list of alignments, each with a &lt;code&gt;name&lt;/code&gt; and the alignment &lt;code&gt;content&lt;/code&gt; in A3M or Stockholm format. For a single merged alignment, such as the output of a ColabFold MMseqs2 search, use the name &lt;code&gt;colabfold_main&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;with open("ca2_colabfold.a3m") as f:
    ca2_msa = f.read()

payload["queries"]["ca2_acetazolamide"]["chains"][0]["precomputed_msa"] = [
    {"name": "colabfold_main", "content": ca2_msa}
]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This example has one protein chain. If a query has several different protein chains, compute an MSA for each one and attach it to that chain’s &lt;code&gt;precomputed_msa&lt;/code&gt; field. Identical copies listed together under one &lt;code&gt;chain_ids&lt;/code&gt; entry, such as a homodimer, share a single MSA.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The endpoint also accepts other alignment names, such as &lt;code&gt;uniref90_hits&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;mgnify_hits&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;bfd_uniclust_hits&lt;/code&gt;, if you generate MSAs with a different pipeline. Because the request is staged in S3, you can send large alignments. In our tests, alignments of several megabytes worked.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can generate MSAs with tools such as &lt;a href="https://github.com/sokrypton/ColabFold" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;ColabFold&lt;/a&gt; (MMseqs2) or HHblits or JackHMMER. To help keep proprietary sequences inside your environment, run the search on your own AWS infrastructure against locally hosted sequence databases, rather than sending sequences to a public server. For example, ColabFold’s &lt;code&gt;colabfold_batch&lt;/code&gt; command uses the public ColabFold MSA server by default, so for proprietary sequences, use &lt;code&gt;colabfold_search&lt;/code&gt; with locally hosted databases instead.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="how-much-difference-does-an-msa-make"&gt;How much difference does an MSA make?&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We folded the carbonic anhydrase II, zinc, and acetazolamide complex from the previous section twice on the same endpoint: once in single-sequence mode, and once with an MSA from a ColabFold MMseqs2 search.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Metric (first of 5 samples)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Without MSA&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;With MSA&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Average pLDDT&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;30&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;98&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;pTM&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0.22&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0.98&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;ipTM&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0.49&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0.98&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Zinc–His94/His96/His119 distance&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2.3–9.4 Å&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2.0 Å each&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Acetazolamide nitrogen–zinc distance&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;5.6 Å&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2.0 Å&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Prediction time&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;~1.5 minutes&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;~1.5 minutes&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With the MSA, OpenFold3 recovers the known binding mode. The zinc ion is coordinated by the three active-site histidines, and the acetazolamide sulfonamide nitrogen binds directly to the zinc, matching the experimental structure. Without the MSA, the protein fold has low confidence and the binding site isn’t formed. Adding the MSA didn’t noticeably change prediction time.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/12/ML-22226-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/12/ML-22226-2.png" alt="Carbonic anhydrase II predictions without an MSA on the left and with an MSA on the right, each overlaid on the gray PDB 3HS4 crystal structure" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: The same carbonic anhydrase II, zinc, and acetazolamide query folded without an MSA (left) and with an MSA (right). Each prediction is colored by pLDDT and superimposed on the experimental crystal structure (gray, PDB &lt;a href="https://www.rcsb.org/structure/3HS4"&gt;3HS4&lt;/a&gt;; Sippel et al., 2009).&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Superimposed on the crystal structure, the MSA-based prediction matches it closely: the protein backbone deviates by 0.2 Å (Cα RMSD), the zinc ion by less than 0.1 Å, and the acetazolamide heavy atoms by 0.4 Å RMSD. The single-sequence prediction is misfolded, with a Cα RMSD of 22 Å.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/12/ML-22226-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/12/ML-22226-3.png" alt="Close-up of the predicted binding site showing the zinc ion coordinated by His94, His96, His119, and the acetazolamide sulfonamide nitrogen" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Close-up of the binding site in the MSA-based prediction. The predicted zinc ion (purple) is coordinated by His94, His96, and His119 and by the sulfonamide nitrogen of acetazolamide (green). The crystal pose (gray) is almost entirely hidden behind the prediction.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Carbonic anhydrase II with acetazolamide is one of the most extensively studied complexes in the Protein Data Bank, and closely related structures are likely part of OpenFold3’s training data. Treat this example as a demonstration of the workflow and of how much an MSA matters, not as a benchmark of accuracy on novel targets.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When you compare OpenFold3 results against other tools or published benchmarks, compare like with like: single-sequence results with single-sequence results, and MSA-based results with MSA-based results.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="help-reduce-cost-scale-to-zero-when-idle"&gt;Help reduce cost: Scale to zero when idle&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Asynchronous endpoints can scale down to &lt;strong&gt;zero instances&lt;/strong&gt; when no requests are queued, and scale back up when new requests arrive. This pattern suits structure prediction work, which often runs in bursts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;aas = boto3.client("application-autoscaling")
resource_id = f"endpoint/{endpoint_name}/variant/AllTraffic"

aas.register_scalable_target(
    ServiceNamespace="sagemaker",
    ResourceId=resource_id,
    ScalableDimension="sagemaker:variant:DesiredInstanceCount",
    MinCapacity=0,
    MaxCapacity=4,
)

# Scale out and in to keep about 4 queued requests per instance
aas.put_scaling_policy(
    PolicyName="openfold3-backlog",
    ServiceNamespace="sagemaker",
    ResourceId=resource_id,
    ScalableDimension="sagemaker:variant:DesiredInstanceCount",
    PolicyType="TargetTrackingScaling",
    TargetTrackingScalingPolicyConfiguration={
        "TargetValue": 4.0,
        "CustomizedMetricSpecification": {
            "MetricName": "ApproximateBacklogSizePerInstance",
            "Namespace": "AWS/SageMaker",
            "Dimensions": [{"Name": "EndpointName", "Value": endpoint_name}],
            "Statistic": "Average",
        },
        "ScaleInCooldown": 600,
        "ScaleOutCooldown": 300,
    },
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Target tracking scales the endpoint down to zero after the queue has been empty for a while. In our tests, that took about 15–20 minutes. However, target tracking doesn’t scale the endpoint back up from zero. To start an instance when a request arrives and no instances are running, add a step-scaling policy that’s triggered by the &lt;code&gt;HasBacklogWithoutCapacity&lt;/code&gt; metric:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;cw = boto3.client("cloudwatch")

step = aas.put_scaling_policy(
    PolicyName="openfold3-scale-from-zero",
    ServiceNamespace="sagemaker",
    ResourceId=resource_id,
    ScalableDimension="sagemaker:variant:DesiredInstanceCount",
    PolicyType="StepScaling",
    StepScalingPolicyConfiguration={
        "AdjustmentType": "ChangeInCapacity",
        "MetricAggregationType": "Average",
        "Cooldown": 300,
        "StepAdjustments": [{"MetricIntervalLowerBound": 0, "ScalingAdjustment": 1}],
    },
)

cw.put_metric_alarm(
    AlarmName=f"{endpoint_name}-has-backlog-without-capacity",
    Namespace="AWS/SageMaker",
    MetricName="HasBacklogWithoutCapacity",
    Dimensions=[{"Name": "EndpointName", "Value": endpoint_name}],
    Statistic="Average",
    Period=60,
    EvaluationPeriods=2,
    DatapointsToAlarm=2,
    Threshold=1,
    ComparisonOperator="GreaterThanOrEqualToThreshold",
    TreatMissingData="missing",
    AlarmActions=[step["PolicyARN"]],
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When a request arrives at an endpoint with zero instances, it waits in the queue while an instance starts. The first request after scaling from zero will take longer to complete as it includes time for the alarm to trigger, the instance to start, plus the prediction itself. Later requests in the same burst run at normal speed. This trade-off can work well for batch workloads. For interactive use, set &lt;code&gt;MinCapacity=1&lt;/code&gt; to keep one instance warm. For more details, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/async-inference-autoscale.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Autoscale an asynchronous endpoint&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To stop incurring charges, delete the resources you created when you’re finished. If you configured autoscaling, remove it first: delete the Amazon CloudWatch alarm, then deregister the scalable target. Then delete each endpoint, its endpoint configuration, and its model, including the multi-GPU endpoint if you deployed it:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# 1. If you configured autoscaling, remove it before deleting the endpoint
cw.delete_alarms(AlarmNames=[f"{endpoint_name}-has-backlog-without-capacity"])
aas.deregister_scalable_target(
    ServiceNamespace="sagemaker",
    ResourceId=resource_id,
    ScalableDimension="sagemaker:variant:DesiredInstanceCount",
)

# 2. Delete the endpoints, endpoint configurations, and models
#    (include only the ones you deployed)
from sagemaker.core.resources import EndpointConfig

for m, ep in ((model, endpoint), (multi_gpu_model, multi_gpu_endpoint)):
    endpoint_config = EndpointConfig.get(endpoint_config_name=ep.endpoint_name)
    m.delete()
    ep.delete()
    endpoint_config.delete()&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you no longer need the request and result objects, you can also delete them from &lt;code&gt;s3://&amp;lt;bucket&amp;gt;/openfold3-async/&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With OpenFold3 on SageMaker JumpStart, research and drug discovery teams can deploy an open, inspectable structure prediction model into their own AWS account with a few lines of code. Asynchronous inference handles long-running folds and bursty batch workloads, and multi-GPU instances run predictions in parallel. Network isolation helps you keep proprietary sequences private, and because you can bring your own MSAs, you can benefit from MSAs without sending sequences to an external server. With scale-to-zero, you stop paying for endpoint instances after the queue has been empty long enough for the endpoint to scale in. Other charges, such as for Amazon S3 storage, still apply.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The code in this post is sample code for experimentation. Before you use it with proprietary or regulated data, work with your security and legal teams to meet your organization’s security, regulatory, and compliance requirements.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, search for &lt;strong&gt;OpenFold3&lt;/strong&gt; in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/jumpstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/a&gt;, or open the example notebook from the model card. To learn more about the features used in this post, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/async-inference.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Asynchronous inference&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/async-inference-autoscale.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Autoscale an asynchronous endpoint&lt;/a&gt;. To discuss your structural biology workloads, contact your AWS account team.&lt;/p&gt;
