		<item>
		<title>Cost-optimizing AssemblyAI speech-to-text inference on AWS with Amazon EC2 Spot</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/cost-optimizing-assemblyai-speech-to-text-inference-on-aws-with-amazon-ec2-spot/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Belair Albahagwi]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 13 Nov 2026 19:35:35 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon EC2]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">62c3e92270a83395aaeaae770a977c92ced34043</guid>

					<description>Learn how AssemblyAI runs cost-optimized speech-to-text inference at scale on AWS, using Amazon ECS, Amazon EC2 Spot GPU instances, and an Amazon SQS-based retry architecture to achieve 70% cost savings while maintaining SLA commitments across European Regions.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Speech-to-text inference is computationally intensive. Each audio file triggers a pipeline of deep learning models (acoustic models, language models, and post-processing stages) running on NVIDIA GPUs to meet latency requirements. At scale, this means thousands of GPU instances running continuously.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://www.assemblyai.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AssemblyAI&lt;/a&gt; needed a way to run GPU-intensive inference workloads cost-effectively at scale, without sacrificing the availability and latency guarantees their customers depend on. Running entirely on On-Demand Instances would be cost-prohibitive. Spot Instances offer significant savings, while Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2) can reclaim them with a 2-minute warning when capacity is needed elsewhere. AssemblyAI needed an architecture that absorbs Spot interruptions gracefully while maintaining service level agreement (SLA) commitments. Additionally, customers in regulated industries, including healthcare, finance, and legal, must keep audio data within specific geographic boundaries. They implement strict data residency configurations in European AWS Regions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Available on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/marketplace/pp/prodview-n24l7xlhzr4o6" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Marketplace&lt;/a&gt;, AssemblyAI provides an API for transcription, speaker detection, sentiment analysis, chapter detection, and personally identifiable information (PII) redaction. Their models transcribe and analyze speech for high-volume workloads across industries including technology, media, healthcare, and financial services.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we show you how AssemblyAI runs cost-optimized AI inference at scale for their speech-to-text platform. The architecture uses Amazon Elastic Container Service (Amazon ECS), Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2) Spot Instances, and Amazon Simple Queue Service (Amazon SQS). Together, these services combine a flexible fleet of NVIDIA GPUs, ECS capacity providers, and a queue-based retry mechanism. The result is significant cost savings while maintaining SLA commitments across European AWS Regions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AssemblyAI’s inference platform runs on Amazon ECS with compute capacity managed through ECS capacity providers. The fleet mixes Amazon EC2 Spot Instances and On-Demand Instances across multiple NVIDIA GPU instance families, running a single inference model per GPU at a scale of thousands of instances. The architecture is built on four pillars that work together to deliver cost-optimized and resilient inference:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon ECS capacity providers mixing Spot and On-Demand.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Multi-family GPU Spot diversification across EC2 instance types (such as g4dn.xlarge, g4dn.2xlarge, g5.xlarge, g5.4xlarge, and g6.2xlarge).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Queue-based retry architecture using Amazon SQS for interruption recovery.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Regional deployment across US West (Oregon) us-west-2 and Europe (London) eu-west-2 for data residency compliance.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram illustrates how this solution is deployed:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/06/ML-20884-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/06/ML-20884-1.png" alt="AssemblyAI inference architecture: Amazon ECS GPU Spot tasks poll an Amazon SQS queue, an AWS Lambda function returns interrupted jobs, and Amazon CloudWatch monitors the fleet" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: AssemblyAI’s inference architecture on Amazon ECS with Amazon EC2 Spot GPU instances and an Amazon SQS-based retry mechanism&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An Amazon ECS cluster runs within a virtual private cloud (VPC) across multiple Availability Zones. The cluster hosts GPU-backed ECS tasks (shown as ECS Task, GPU Spot 1 and ECS Task GPU Spot 2) that poll the queue and process inference jobs. Each task retrieves audio files from Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) and writes the completed transcription to a results store, which AssemblyAI configures to deliver the output to the end customer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When a Spot Instance receives an interruption notice, Amazon EventBridge invokes the Spot Interruption Handler (an AWS Lambda function). The handler returns the in-flight job to the Main Job SQS Queue so another healthy ECS task can pick it up. Customers configure their system to route jobs that exhaust retry attempts to a Dead Letter SQS Queue for investigation. Amazon CloudWatch Metrics monitors the health of the fleet and the Spot interruption events, providing observability across the system.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="amazon-ecs-capacity-providers-and-spot-diversification"&gt;Amazon ECS capacity providers and Spot diversification&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To optimize inference costs without compromising reliability, AssemblyAI configures multiple capacity providers in a single capacity provider strategy within each ECS cluster. An On-Demand capacity provider serves as an always-on baseline, and a Spot capacity provider spans different GPU instance families and Availability Zones to serve spikes of demand. Each capacity provider associates with an Amazon EC2 Auto Scaling group. Amazon ECS managed scaling automates the scaling of GPU instances based on the capacity provider weights and the pending inference tasks. When the task queue grows, the Spot capacity provider requests additional instances from the diversified pool. When demand drops, instances scale in gracefully.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;One of the most effective Spot best practices is to be flexible across a wide range of instance types to increase the chances of getting the aggregate compute capacity needed. Rather than depending on a single GPU instance family, AssemblyAI’s infrastructure spans three Amazon EC2 GPU instance families:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;g4dn instances&lt;/strong&gt; – Powered by NVIDIA T4 GPUs, providing a cost-effective baseline for standard speech recognition models.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;g5 instances&lt;/strong&gt; – Equipped with NVIDIA A10G GPUs, delivering higher throughput for more demanding model architectures.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;g6 instances&lt;/strong&gt; – Featuring NVIDIA L4 GPUs, offering improved performance-per-dollar for larger models including large language model-based inference.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;By spreading demand across instance families and sizes, AssemblyAI draws from a significantly larger pool of available Spot capacity. Using the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/autoscaling/ec2/userguide/allocation-strategies.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;price-capacity optimized allocation strategy&lt;/a&gt;, Amazon EC2 provisions instances from the Spot capacity pools with the highest available capacity at the lowest price, reducing the likelihood of interruptions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="queue-based-retry-for-interruption-resilience"&gt;Queue-based retry for interruption resilience&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Maintaining quality of service during Spot interruptions was a core design requirement. AssemblyAI decouples job submission from execution using Amazon SQS. When a transcription request arrives, it’s placed into an Amazon SQS queue. Amazon ECS tasks running on GPU instances poll the queue, pull jobs, and execute the inference pipeline. Individual jobs typically take between 15 seconds and several minutes depending on audio length.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When a Spot Instance receives a 2-minute interruption notice, the retry flow works as follows:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In-flight jobs that can complete within the 2-minute window finish normally. Jobs that need more time are returned to the Amazon SQS queue, where they become immediately available for other workers.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Another healthy GPU instance picks up the message, potentially on a different instance type or in a different Availability Zone, and completes the inference.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To reduce recovery time, AssemblyAI creates custom Amazon Machine Images (AMIs) containing model artifacts and container images in approximately 2 minutes. For end customers, the impact of a Spot interruption is a small increase in processing time, but the transcription completes successfully and is delivered as expected.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="regional-deployment-for-data-residency"&gt;Regional deployment for data residency&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Running in multiple AWS Regions allows AssemblyAI to support customers in regulated industries, including healthcare, financial services, and legal services, where audio data must never leave specific geographic boundaries. Each AWS Region runs a fully independent copy of the inference architecture: its own Amazon SQS queues, Amazon ECS clusters, capacity providers, and GPU fleets. Multiple GPU instance families (g4dn, g5, g6) are available in these European Regions with competitive Spot pricing. This instance diversity supports a reliable Spot-based inference service while meeting strict data residency requirements.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="results"&gt;Results&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;By running Amazon EC2 Spot Instances with GPUs in production, AssemblyAI achieved 70 percent cost savings on inference compute compared to On-Demand pricing. As the platform grew nearly 4x year-over-year in compute demand, the Spot-based architecture kept unit economics sustainable. Operating at this scale, AssemblyAI learned that diversifying across instance families and sizes is the most effective lever for minimizing Spot interruption rates. The g4dn, g5, and g6 families tap into independent capacity pools, so when one family experiences capacity pressure, the others remain available. Designing fault tolerance from day one with queue-based retries and fast warm-up turns Spot interruptions into routine events while maintaining SLA commitments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Teams running batch or near real-time AI workloads with similar characteristics (short-lived, independently retriable jobs on GPU compute) can apply this pattern. Start by diversifying instance families and building queue-based fault tolerance to take advantage of cost-effective Spot infrastructure without compromising availability.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/ecs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;To get started&lt;/a&gt; with GPU tasks on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ecs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon ECS&lt;/a&gt;, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonECS/latest/developerguide/ecs-gpu.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon ECS for GPU workloads&lt;/a&gt;. To evaluate Spot availability for your workload, explore using &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-solutions-library-samples/guidance-for-ec2-spot-placement-score-tracker#using-spot-placement-scores-with-accelerated-compute-platforms" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Spot placement scores for accelerated compute&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSEC2/latest/UserGuide/spot-best-practices.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon EC2 Spot Instances best practices&lt;/a&gt;. AssemblyAI’s Speech-to-Text API is available on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/marketplace" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Marketplace&lt;/a&gt;. To learn more about their platform, visit &lt;a href="https://www.assemblyai.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;assemblyai.com&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Cost attribution and controls for AI developer and productivity tools on Amazon Bedrock</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/cost-attribution-and-controls-for-ai-developer-and-productivity-tools-on-amazon-bedrock/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Aamna Najmi]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 13 Nov 2026 18:44:02 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">94b792ec6f46dfd34db3ccc1fda8cfa7fee6df93</guid>

					<description>Amazon Bedrock provides built-in, zero-instrumentation cost attribution that traces every inference call back to the IAM principal that made it. This post shows how to attribute per-user Bedrock costs for AI developer and productivity tools using IAM Identity Center, session tags, and CUR 2.0, and how to enforce per-user usage limits.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock provides built-in, zero-instrumentation cost attribution that traces every inference call back to the IAM principal that made it. The identity flows along with organizational tags, into AWS Cost and Usage Reports (CUR 2.0) and AWS Cost Explorer while keeping data inside the AWS security boundary. This post shows you how to use these capabilities to answer three questions that every FinOps and AI Platform leader asks as AI tool adoption scales: What is the real per-user cost? Which teams generate the most spend, and does that spend correlate with the value they deliver? How should next year’s budget scale with headcount and adoption?&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The approach works identically regardless of which AI coding or productivity tool makes the call. Claude Code, Claude Desktop, OpenAI Codex, ChatGPT Work, and OpenCode all invoke Amazon Bedrock through one of two inference endpoints: &lt;code&gt;bedrock-runtime&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;bedrock-mantle&lt;/code&gt;, and Bedrock’s attribution mechanism operates at the endpoint layer, beneath any tool-specific logic. You configure identity propagation once, and every tool that routes inference through Bedrock inherits per-user cost visibility automatically.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;By the end of this post, you will have AWS IAM Identity Center configured with your external identity provider for per-user attribution, session tags (team, cost center) flowing into CUR 2.0, Amazon Athena queries and dashboard options for ongoing cost visibility, and a clear understanding of Bedrock-native usage controls to enforce spending limits.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-amazon-bedrock-attribution-works"&gt;How Amazon Bedrock attribution works&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock records the IAM identity of every inference caller with no changes required to your API calls. When a user invokes a model through either the &lt;code&gt;bedrock-runtime&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;bedrock-mantle&lt;/code&gt; endpoint, Bedrock captures the caller’s full IAM ARN, including the session name for federated users and publishes it to CUR 2.0 in the &lt;code&gt;line_item_iam_principal&lt;/code&gt; column. In billing data, usage is aggregated per principal and per usage type for each export interval: one hour in an hourly export, one day in a daily export. CUR does not contain per-request cost. For per-prompt detail, use model invocation logs or request metadata tagging.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Optionally, you can attach tags to IAM principals or pass session tags during federation to add organizational dimensions. These tags appear in CUR 2.0 with the &lt;code&gt;iamPrincipal/&lt;/code&gt; prefix and in Cost Explorer as filterable dimensions. The mechanism supports four access patterns: IAM users, per-application roles, federated identities, and large language model (LLM) gateways proxying to Bedrock. For a comparison of all four, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/introducing-granular-cost-attribution-for-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Introducing granular cost attribution for Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;. This post follows the federated identity pattern, which is the natural fit for AI coding and productivity tools where each developer or knowledge worker authenticates individually.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock offers additional attribution mechanisms beyond IAM principal tracking that organizations can layer depending on their needs (see Appendix A for a comparison). With Application Inference Profiles, you can tag spend by workload on the &lt;code&gt;bedrock-runtime&lt;/code&gt; endpoint. With Projects and Workspaces, you can isolate and tag spend on the &lt;code&gt;bedrock-mantle&lt;/code&gt; endpoint. Per-request metadata tagging provides per-prompt token-level detail in model invocation logs for workloads that need granularity finer than finer than CUR 2.0’s per-usage-type aggregation per export interval. You can combine these, for example, IAM principal attribution for per-user dollars on your invoice alongside per-request metadata tags for per-prompt detail in logs. The choice depends on your goal (see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/cost-management.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Track usage and costs in Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-attribution-problem"&gt;The attribution problem&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The developer and productivity tools run on each user’s machine and call Amazon Bedrock directly, or through an LLM gateway. Users authenticate by assuming a shared IAM role, so every API call appears as &lt;code&gt;assumed-role/&amp;lt;role&amp;gt;/&amp;lt;session-name&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt; in billing. If every user assumes the role with the same fixed session name, or calls go through a gateway’s own role, CUR aggregates spend to a single principal. With IAM Identity Center, the session name is the user’s sign-in name, so each user gets their own principal. You can further enrich it with session tags (team, cost center, project). These fields flow into CUR 2.0’s &lt;code&gt;line_item_iam_principal&lt;/code&gt; column and cost allocation tags with the &lt;code&gt;iamPrincipal/&lt;/code&gt; prefix, making per-user spend queryable alongside the dollars, with zero changes to your Bedrock API calls.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Claude Platform on AWS and Claude for Enterprise route inference through Anthropic-managed infrastructure. For Claude Platform on AWS, usage is visible by workspace and API key in Anthropic’s admin console, and workspace tags flow to AWS billing. For Claude for Enterprise, per-user usage is visible in Anthropic’s admin console and Usage and Cost API. Spend limits are enforced natively by Anthropic.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before starting, make sure you have:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/resources/create-account/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS account&lt;/a&gt; with IAM permissions to access Bedrock models you intend to use.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/singlesignon/latest/userguide/enable-identity-center.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;IAM Identity Center enabled&lt;/a&gt; in your management account.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An external identity provider (Okta, Microsoft Entra ID, or Ping Identity) with SCIM provisioning capability.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/userguide/cli-chap-getting-started.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS CLI v2&lt;/a&gt; configured on developer machines.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/awsaccountbilling/latest/aboutv2/iam-principal-cost-allocation.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Cost and Usage Report&lt;/a&gt; (CUR 2.0) enabled with IAM principal data and delivered to an S3 bucket.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/awsaccountbilling/latest/aboutv2/cost-alloc-tags.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Cost allocation tags&lt;/a&gt; activated from the management (payer) account.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An Athena table configured to query CUR 2.0 data (see the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/aws-cudos-framework-deployment" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub sample&lt;/a&gt; to get started).&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-1-configure-iam-identity-center-with-your-external-idp"&gt;Step 1: Configure IAM Identity Center with your external IdP&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Configure your external identity provider as the identity source in AWS IAM Identity Center. Navigate to IAM Identity Center → Settings → Identity source → Change identity source. Select “External identity provider” and configure SAML 2.0 or SCIM connectivity with your IdP. Enable SCIM provisioning to automatically synchronize users and groups, including organizational attributes like team and department.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Configure attribute mapping for session tags&lt;/strong&gt; by navigating to Settings → Attributes for access control. Map your IdP’s organizational attributes to session tags that will flow into CUR 2.0. These attributes propagate as session tags (&lt;code&gt;aws:PrincipalTag/Team&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;aws:PrincipalTag/CostCenter&lt;/code&gt;) on every API call the user makes, including every Bedrock invocation through both the &lt;code&gt;bedrock-runtime&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;bedrock-mantle&lt;/code&gt; endpoints.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-2-create-a-permission-set-for-bedrock-access"&gt;Step 2: Create a permission set for Bedrock access&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Create a permission set in Identity Center that grants Bedrock invocation access. Each user gets their own session within this permission set. This is the foundation of per-user attribution. The specific IAM actions depend on which Bedrock endpoint the tool calls.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Tools that call the &lt;code&gt;bedrock-mantle&lt;/code&gt; endpoint (such as Codex and ChatGPT Work) require &lt;code&gt;bedrock-mantle:CreateInference&lt;/code&gt; and related actions scoped to a Project resource. Tools that call the &lt;code&gt;bedrock-runtime&lt;/code&gt; endpoint (such as Claude Code and Claude Desktop) require &lt;code&gt;bedrock:InvokeModel&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream&lt;/code&gt; scoped to the relevant foundation model or inference profile ARNs. You can combine both in a single permission set if your organization uses tools across both endpoints.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "Statement": [
        {
            "Sid": "BedrockMantleAccess",
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Action": [
                "bedrock-mantle:CreateInference",
                "bedrock-mantle:CountTokens"
            ],
            "Resource": “arn:aws:bedrock-mantle:::project/”
        },
        {
            "Sid": "BedrockModelAccess",
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Action": [
                "bedrock:InvokeModel",
                "bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream",
                "bedrock:ListInferenceProfiles",
                "bedrock:GetInferenceProfile"
            ],
			“Resource”: [
			“arn:aws:bedrock:::inference-profile/“,
			“arn:aws:bedrock:::application-inference-profile/“,
			“arn:aws:bedrock:::foundation-model/”
			]
        }
    ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; These resource patterns are a starting point. Narrow them to the specific inference profiles and Projects that you approve. Assign this permission set to the relevant groups synchronized from your IdP. Policy changes are instant and universal, audit is centralized through AWS CloudTrail with each user’s identity, and users do not receive long-lived AWS credentials. &lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-3-configure-user-workstations"&gt;Step 3: Configure user workstations&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Users configure their AWS CLI profile once, no custom scripts, credential helpers, or browser automation required. The following &lt;code&gt;~/.aws/config&lt;/code&gt; entry establishes the SSO session:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-ini"&gt;[profile bedrock]
sso_session = corp
sso_account_id = 111122223333
sso_role_name = BedrockDeveloperAccess
region = us-east-1

[sso-session corp]
sso_start_url = https://your-org.awsapps.com/start
sso_region = us-east-1
sso_registration_scopes = sso:account:access&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Users authenticate by running &lt;code&gt;aws sso login --profile bedrock&lt;/code&gt;, which opens the browser, authenticates through the IdP, and caches credentials locally. The AWS CLI and SDKs refresh the access token automatically until the IAM Identity Center session ends (8 hours by default). The core trade-off in configuring session duration is between security posture and operational convenience.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-4-point-tools-at-amazon-bedrock"&gt;Step 4: Point tools at Amazon Bedrock&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each AI tool needs only a standard configuration change to route inference through Bedrock. Because Bedrock attribution operates at the endpoint layer, no tool-specific instrumentation is required. The tool authenticates using the AWS credential chain established in Step 3, and Bedrock captures the caller identity automatically.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Claude Desktop&lt;/strong&gt; reads third-party inference settings from OS-native managed preferences (&lt;code&gt;/Library/Managed Preferences/com.anthropic.claudefordesktop.plist&lt;/code&gt; on macOS, &lt;code&gt;HKLM\SOFTWARE\Policies\Claude&lt;/code&gt; on Windows). Author the configuration once in the in-app window (Developer → Configure Third-Party Inference) and choose &lt;strong&gt;Export&lt;/strong&gt; to produce a &lt;code&gt;.mobileconfig&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;.reg&lt;/code&gt; for your mobile device management (MDM) system. For per-user attribution, use in-app AWS sign-in, each user authenticates against IAM Identity Center inside the application, with no AWS CLI on the device:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-text"&gt;inferenceProvider bedrock
inferenceBedrockRegion us-east-1
inferenceBedrockSsoStartUrl https://your-org.awsapps.com/start
inferenceBedrockSsoRegion us-east-1
inferenceBedrockSsoAccountId 111122223333
inferenceBedrockSsoRoleName BedrockDeveloperAccess
inferenceModels ["us.anthropic.claude-sonnet-5","us.anthropic.claude-opus-5"]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Configuration is read at launch. Users must quit and reopen the application. If developers already run AWS CLI v2, set &lt;code&gt;inferenceBedrockProfile&lt;/code&gt; instead and the app runs &lt;code&gt;aws sso login&lt;/code&gt; for them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Claude Code CLI reads the standard AWS SDK credential chain, including SSO profiles. If &lt;code&gt;AWS_BEARER_TOKEN_BEDROCK&lt;/code&gt; is set, Claude Code authenticates with that key and does not use the credential chain, so calls are attributed to the IAM user behind the key. Disable it when you want per-user SSO attribution. &lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Set &lt;code&gt;awsAuthRefresh&lt;/code&gt; in the settings file to automatically re-authenticate when credentials expire:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "awsAuthRefresh": "aws sso login --profile bedrock",
    "env": {
        "CLAUDE_CODE_USE_BEDROCK": "1",
        "AWS_PROFILE": "bedrock",
        "AWS_REGION": "us-east-1"
    }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Pin model versions before fleet rollout so a new release doesn’t change what users invoke:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;export ANTHROPIC_DEFAULT_OPUS_MODEL='us.anthropic.claude-opus-5'
export ANTHROPIC_DEFAULT_SONNET_MODEL='us.anthropic.claude-sonnet-5'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Codex and ChatGPT Work&lt;/strong&gt; read &lt;code&gt;~/.codex/config.toml&lt;/code&gt;. Both the desktop app and CLI consume the same configuration layers. For the &lt;code&gt;bedrock-mantle&lt;/code&gt; endpoint:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-toml"&gt;model_provider = "amazon-bedrock"
model = "openai.gpt-5.6-sol"

[model_providers.amazon-bedrock.aws]
region = "us-east-1"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For the &lt;code&gt;bedrock-runtime&lt;/code&gt; endpoint, use inference profile IDs:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-toml"&gt;model_provider = "amazon-bedrock-runtime"
model = "us.openai.gpt-5.6-sol"
web_search = "disabled"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Set &lt;code&gt;AWS_PROFILE&lt;/code&gt; in the environment for both endpoints. For the desktop app, place environment variables in &lt;code&gt;~/.codex/.env&lt;/code&gt; since desktop applications may not inherit shell environment variables. If &lt;code&gt;AWS_BEARER_TOKEN_BEDROCK&lt;/code&gt; is set, Codex uses it before the SSO profile and calls are attributed to the IAM user behind that API key. Unset it when you want per-user SSO attribution.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-5-enable-cost-attribution-in-cur-2.0"&gt;Step 5: Enable cost attribution in CUR 2.0&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;From the AWS Billing Console (Billing → Data Exports → Create export), configure delivery to S3 in Parquet format with hourly or daily granularity. Select “Include caller identity (IAM principal) allocation data” to populate the &lt;code&gt;line_item_iam_principal&lt;/code&gt; column. Bedrock’s IAM principal cost tracking works for both &lt;code&gt;bedrock-runtime&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;bedrock-mantle&lt;/code&gt; endpoints.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Creating the export in the management account captures all accounts’ Bedrock usage. If you created a CUR 2.0 data export before IAM principal attribution launched, you must create a new export with the caller identity option selected.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Activate cost allocation tags after users have signed in and made at least one Bedrock call. Tags appear in Billing only after the principal has made a call. In the management account, open Billing → Cost allocation tags, filter by type IAM principal, and activate &lt;code&gt;Team&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;CostCenter&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws ce update-cost-allocation-tags-status \
--cost-allocation-tags-status TagKey=Team,Status=Active
aws ce update-cost-allocation-tags-status \
--cost-allocation-tags-status TagKey=CostCenter,Status=Active&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Allow up to 24 hours for tags to appear and up to 48 hours to see them in CUR 2.0 and Cost Explorer. Activation is not retroactive. Bedrock meters and bills input, output, cache-read, and cache-write tokens separately. A single hour of activity under one IAM principal typically produces four CUR rows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-6-query-cost-data-with-amazon-athena"&gt;Step 6: Query cost data with Amazon Athena&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Once CUR 2.0 is delivered to S3 and an Athena table is configured, query usage and cost at your desired granularity. The following query extracts per-user cost:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;SELECT
  regexp_extract(line_item_iam_principal, 'assumed-role/[^/]+/(.+)$', 1) AS user_name,
  line_item_usage_type,
  SUM(line_item_unblended_cost) AS total_cost_usd,
  SUM(line_item_usage_amount)   AS usage_units
FROM cur_table
WHERE line_item_product_code IN ('AmazonBedrock', 'AmazonBedrockService')
  AND line_item_iam_principal IS NOT NULL
  AND line_item_line_item_type = 'Usage'
  AND billing_period = '2026-09'
GROUP BY 1, 2
ORDER BY total_cost_usd DESC;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For daily spend trend per cost center:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;SELECT
    DATE(line_item_usage_start_date) AS usage_date,
    element_at(tags, 'iamPrincipal/CostCenter') AS cost_center,
    SUM(line_item_unblended_cost) AS daily_cost_usd
FROM cur_table
WHERE line_item_product_code IN ('AmazonBedrock', 'AmazonBedrockService')
    AND line_item_iam_principal IS NOT NULL
    AND line_item_line_item_type = 'Usage'
    AND billing_period = '2026-09'
GROUP BY 1, 2
ORDER BY 1, daily_cost_usd DESC;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Athena charges by data scanned. Columnar Parquet and the &lt;code&gt;billing_period&lt;/code&gt; filter keep queries inexpensive.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-7-visualization"&gt;Step 7: Visualization&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS Cost Explorer requires zero setup. Group by tags once they are activated. AWS Quick Sight connects to Athena for interactive dashboards with anomaly detection that alerts on unexpected spend spikes. You can integrate with the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/amazon-bedrock-cost-attribution-with-amazon-athena-and-cudos/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CUDOS dashboard&lt;/a&gt;, an open-source Cloud Intelligence Dashboards (CID) framework that provides detailed cost insights. Grafana with the Athena plugin renders CUR data as time series alongside existing infrastructure metrics.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="implementing-per-user-usage-controls"&gt;Implementing per-user usage controls&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Once per-user cost visibility is established through IAM principal attribution in CUR 2.0, organizations need enforcement mechanisms to help prevent uncontrolled accumulation. Numerous approaches exist today, each trading enforcement latency against operational complexity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS Budgets with budget actions&lt;/strong&gt; is the lightest option requiring no custom infrastructure. Set a budget threshold filtered by IAM principal tags, and attach an action (such as applying a deny policy) that triggers when the threshold is breached. The trade-off: AWS Budgets updates up to three times a day, and Bedrock charges can take up to 24 hours to appear in billing data, so a high-velocity user can overshoot by up to a day before enforcement takes effect.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon CloudWatch alarms and AWS Lambda&lt;/strong&gt; offer near-real-time enforcement using Bedrock’s native CloudWatch metrics (&lt;code&gt;InputTokenCount&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;OutputTokenCount&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;Invocations&lt;/code&gt;) filtered by model ID or inference profile. Enforcement latency drops to 1–5 minutes. To get user-level granularity with this approach, use Application Inference Profiles or per-user roles.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon DynamoDB-based quota tracking&lt;/strong&gt; enforces limits at the authentication layer. A Lambda function increments per-user token counters in DynamoDB and withholds credentials once a limit is exceeded. Enforcement takes effect at the next credential refresh, not on individual API calls, but removes the billing-data lag inherent in budget-based approaches.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;LLM gateway proxy&lt;/strong&gt; delivers per-invocation blocking across Bedrock models and SDK workloads, at the cost of highest operational complexity and added latency on every request. See &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/multi-provider-generative-ai-gateway/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Multi-Provider Generative AI Gateway&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Organizations typically layer these options: CUR 2.0 for reporting, a gateway or DynamoDB-based enforcement for real-time control, and AWS Budgets as a backstop. For the full reference, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/cost-management.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;How to limit usage and cost on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock’s IAM principal attribution gives you per-user cost tracking for every AI developer and productivity tool that routes inference through Bedrock, with no custom credential scripts, per-user IAM roles, or additional infrastructure. Users authenticate with &lt;code&gt;aws sso login&lt;/code&gt;, the same flow they use for other AWS access, and every Bedrock invocation automatically carries their identity into billing data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each component can be adopted incrementally. Start by connecting your IdP to Identity Center and assigning a Bedrock permission set to get session-level attribution in CloudTrail immediately. Activate cost allocation tags to surface that data in CUR 2.0. Add Athena queries and dashboards as reporting requirements grow. Layer usage controls, from AWS Budgets to real-time gateway enforcement, based on your organization’s tolerance for enforcement latency.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Regardless of which AI tools your organization adopts today or adds tomorrow, the attribution and control mechanisms described here operate at the Bedrock platform layer. Configure once, and every tool that calls Bedrock inherits the governance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;
