		<item>
		<title>Build a model improvement flywheel with Amazon SageMaker HyperPod Data Capture</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-a-model-improvement-flywheel-with-amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-data-capture/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Shantanu Tripathi]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 13 Nov 2026 18:01:15 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker HyperPod]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">a1587ec860fedf29e005f9336e493877ba832183</guid>

					<description>Amazon SageMaker HyperPod Data Capture records live inference inputs and outputs to Amazon S3. This post walks through one end-to-end example: capturing a support router's production traffic, surfacing a new-product blind spot with Amazon Athena, fine-tuning on the captured data with the HyperPod training operator, and measuring the before-and-after lift.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Foundation models deployed on Amazon SageMaker HyperPod meet inputs that no pre-curated dataset covers: domain-specific jargon, unexpected formats, new products, and changing user behavior. Without a record of production inference data, teams can’t detect drift, find failure patterns, or use real interactions to improve the model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon SageMaker HyperPod now provides Data Capture, a built-in capability that records live inference inputs and outputs to Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) at three independent tiers, with minimal latency impact and zero impact on model availability.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walk through one complete example on a single SageMaker HyperPod cluster. A home-appliance support router captures its production traffic, Amazon Athena queries surface a new-product blind spot, a SQL policy labels the captured records, and a fine-tune on that data teaches the router the new product. We measure accuracy before and after, on data that could not have existed before the model was deployed. The inference operator serves the model on one node, and the training operator runs the fine-tuning on another.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We use synthetic data for the walkthrough. The scripts, manifests, SQL, synthetic datasets, and expected outputs are available in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/awsome-distributed-ai/tree/main/examples/use-cases/sagemaker-hyperpod-data-capture-flywheel" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;accompanying GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="three-tier-data-capture-architecture"&gt;Three-tier Data Capture architecture&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;HyperPod Data Capture provides three independent tiers, each at a different point in the request path. You can turn on any combination in the same &lt;code&gt;dataCapture&lt;/code&gt; block.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tier 1: SageMaker endpoint&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tier 2: Load balancer&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tier 3: Model pod&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;What’s captured&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Full request/response payloads&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Application Load Balancer (ALB) access-log fields: request line, status codes, timings, client IP, user agent&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Full request/response payloads plus event metadata&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Managed by&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;SageMaker native data capture&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;ALB access logs&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;HyperPod inference operator (NGINX + OTel collector + S3 uploader sidecars)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Best for&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Compliance, Model Monitor, audit&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Traffic and error-rate monitoring at low cost&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Debugging, analytics, training-data collection&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Configuration&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;sagemakerEndpoint:&lt;/code&gt; — sampling %, input/output, AWS KMS&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;loadBalancer: { enabled: true }&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;modelPod:&lt;/code&gt; — sampling %, input/output, buffer, max payload size, AWS KMS, content types&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Tiers 1 and 3 both store full payloads, so enabling both stores every record twice. Use Tier 1 for compliance and Model Monitor, Tier 2 when you only need traffic metadata, and Tier 3 when you intend to &lt;em&gt;use&lt;/em&gt; the payloads. This post uses Tier 3 only. Because Tier 3 records complete payloads, which can include names, addresses, and order numbers, protect that data before it becomes training data. Use sampling, AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS) encryption (&lt;code&gt;kmsKeyId&lt;/code&gt;), S3 lifecycle rules, and personally identifiable information (PII) redaction (for example, with Amazon Comprehend).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A SageMaker HyperPod cluster orchestrated by Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS), with the inference operator installed (&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-model-deployment-setup.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Setting up your HyperPod clusters for model deployment&lt;/a&gt;) and the training operator EKS add-on &lt;code&gt;amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-training-operator&lt;/code&gt; installed (&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-eks-operator-install.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Installing the training operator&lt;/a&gt;).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Two available NVIDIA GPUs&lt;/strong&gt;, one for serving and one for training. We used an &lt;code&gt;ml.g6e.8xlarge&lt;/code&gt; for serving and an &lt;code&gt;ml.g6e.xlarge&lt;/code&gt; for training (one 48 GB NVIDIA L40S each). The serving pod holds its GPU for the life of the endpoint, so with only one GPU the training pod stays &lt;code&gt;Pending&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;kubectl&lt;/code&gt; configured for the cluster.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An S3 bucket in the same Region.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Confirm both operators are installed. The command returns two rows:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;kubectl get crd | grep -E 'inferenceendpointconfigs|hyperpodpytorchjobs'
hyperpodpytorchjobs.sagemaker.amazonaws.com                         2026-09-21T10:57:14Z
inferenceendpointconfigs.inference.sagemaker.aws.amazon.com         2026-08-06T11:58:53Z&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the five steps of the walkthrough on one SageMaker HyperPod cluster: capture every request and response to Amazon S3, analyze the captures with Athena, label them with a SQL policy, fine-tune with the training operator on a second GPU, and redeploy side by side with capture still on.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/12/ML-22239-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/12/ML-22239-1.png" alt="Architecture of one SageMaker HyperPod cluster: a capture proxy in the serving pod copies requests to Amazon S3, Athena analyzes and labels them, a training job fine-tunes on a second GPU, and a second endpoint serves the new model with capture on." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: The five-step flywheel on a single SageMaker HyperPod cluster, from capture through Athena analysis, labeling, fine-tuning, and redeploy&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The inference operator adds three sidecars to the model pod. Every request that passes through the NGINX proxy is copied to an OpenTelemetry collector, batched, and written to S3 through a Mountpoint-S3 volume. Athena reads those files in place. The training operator runs the fine-tune as a &lt;code&gt;HyperPodPyTorchJob&lt;/code&gt; on a second GPU, and the new model is served by a second endpoint that also has capture on.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-0-set-your-variables"&gt;Step 0: Set your variables&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every later step reads these. Edit the first block, then paste the whole thing:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;# --- edit these ---
export EKS_CLUSTER_NAME="my-hyperpod-eks"
export AWS_REGION="us-east-1"
export BUCKET="amzn-s3-demo-bucket"
export SERVE_INSTANCE_TYPE="ml.g6e.8xlarge"     # node type for the first endpoint
export TRAIN_INSTANCE_TYPE="ml.g6e.xlarge"      # node type for the fine-tuning job
# --- leave as-is ---
export AWS_DEFAULT_REGION="$AWS_REGION"
export ENDPOINT_NAME="appliance-router"
export MODEL_LOCATION="models/qwen2.5-3b-instruct"
export MODEL_V2_LOCATION="models/qwen2.5-3b-router-v2"
export TRAIN_SA="flywheel"                     # service account with S3 access via EKS Pod Identity
aws eks update-kubeconfig --name "$EKS_CLUSTER_NAME" --region "$AWS_REGION"
render() { python3 -c 'import os,sys; sys.stdout.write(os.path.expandvars(sys.stdin.read()))'; }&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-1-stage-the-base-model-in-s3"&gt;Step 1: Stage the base model in S3&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We use &lt;code&gt;Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct&lt;/code&gt; because it is small enough to fine-tune in about a minute and fits on a single L40S.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;pip install -q huggingface_hub
python3 -c "from huggingface_hub import snapshot_download; \
  snapshot_download('Qwen/Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct', local_dir='qwen2.5-3b-instruct')"
aws s3 sync qwen2.5-3b-instruct "s3://${BUCKET}/${MODEL_LOCATION}/" --exclude ".cache/*"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-2-iam-permissions"&gt;Step 2: IAM permissions&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Four identities need access. For the policy statements, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-model-deployment-data-capture.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Data capture for inference on HyperPod&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The Mountpoint-S3 CSI driver role.&lt;/strong&gt; Tier 3 capture writes through a Mountpoint-S3 volume, and the driver authenticates as its own IAM roles for service accounts (IRSA) role, not as the pod’s service account. It needs &lt;code&gt;s3:PutObject&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;s3:AbortMultipartUpload&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;s3:DeleteObject&lt;/code&gt; on your capture bucket, plus &lt;code&gt;kms:Decrypt&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;kms:GenerateDataKey&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;kms:DescribeKey&lt;/code&gt; if you set &lt;code&gt;kmsKeyId&lt;/code&gt;. If this is missing, the endpoint stays healthy and requests succeed, but no captures appear. See &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/eks/latest/userguide/s3-csi.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Mountpoint for Amazon S3 CSI driver&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The inference operator execution role.&lt;/strong&gt; It needs &lt;code&gt;s3:PutObject&lt;/code&gt; on your capture bucket, plus &lt;code&gt;kms:Decrypt&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;kms:GenerateDataKey&lt;/code&gt; if you set &lt;code&gt;kmsKeyId&lt;/code&gt;. The capture files themselves are written through the CSI driver role, so when no captures appear, check that role first.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The training pod’s service account (&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;code&gt;$TRAIN_SA&lt;/code&gt;&lt;strong&gt;).&lt;/strong&gt; Read access to the base model and training data, and write access for the merged model, through &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/eks/latest/userguide/pod-identities.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;EKS Pod Identity&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Athena.&lt;/strong&gt; Read access to the capture prefix, plus a query-results location.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="enabling-data-capture"&gt;Enabling Data Capture&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The next three steps deploy the router with Tier 3 capture on, check that the pod runs the capture sidecars, and confirm that records reach Amazon S3.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-3-deploy-the-model-with-tier-3-capture-enabled"&gt;Step 3: Deploy the model with Tier 3 capture enabled&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This is the whole feature: the &lt;code&gt;dataCapture.modelPod.enabled: true&lt;/code&gt; block. Everything else is an ordinary vLLM deployment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;cat &amp;gt; endpoint.yaml &amp;lt;&amp;lt;'YAML'
apiVersion: inference.sagemaker.aws.amazon.com/v1
kind: InferenceEndpointConfig
metadata:
  name: ${ENDPOINT_NAME}
  namespace: default
spec:
  replicas: 1
  modelName: ${ENDPOINT_NAME}
  endpointName: ${ENDPOINT_NAME}
  instanceType: ${SERVE_INSTANCE_TYPE}
  invocationEndpoint: v1/chat/completions
  modelSourceConfig:
    modelSourceType: s3
    modelLocation: ${MODEL_LOCATION}
    s3Storage:
      bucketName: ${BUCKET}
      region: ${AWS_REGION}

  # &amp;gt;&amp;gt;&amp;gt; This is all you need to turn on Tier 3 data capture &amp;lt;&amp;lt;&amp;lt;
  dataCapture:
    s3Uri: "s3://${BUCKET}/capture/"
    modelPod:
      enabled: true
      initialSamplingPercentage: 100
      bufferConfig:
        batchSize: 50
        flushIntervalSeconds: 60
      payloadConfig:
        maxPayloadSizeKB: 1024

  worker:
    image: vllm/vllm-openai:v0.10.0
    modelInvocationPort:
      containerPort: 8000
      name: http
    modelVolumeMount:
      mountPath: /opt/ml/model
      name: model-weights
    args: ["--model", "/opt/ml/model", "--served-model-name", "${ENDPOINT_NAME}",
           "--port", "8000", "--max-model-len", "2048", "--gpu-memory-utilization", "0.90"]
    resources:
      requests: { cpu: "2", memory: 16Gi, nvidia.com/gpu: 1 }
      limits:   { nvidia.com/gpu: 1 }
YAML
render &amp;lt; endpoint.yaml | kubectl apply -f -&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The preceding &lt;code&gt;modelPod&lt;/code&gt; block sets 100% sampling and captures both input and output, which are the defaults. To tune it, add any of these optional fields under &lt;code&gt;modelPod&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;modelPod:
  enabled: true
  initialSamplingPercentage: 50          # capture 50% of requests (default 100)
  bufferConfig:
    batchSize: 100                       # records buffered per upload batch (default 10)
    flushIntervalSeconds: 60             # max seconds before a flush (default 60)
  payloadConfig:
    maxPayloadSizeKB: 1024               # truncate payloads larger than this (default: no limit)
  kmsKeyId: "arn:aws:kms:...:key/..."    # encrypt captures at rest&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-4-wait-for-the-pod"&gt;Step 4: Wait for the pod&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A capture-enabled pod runs &lt;strong&gt;four containers&lt;/strong&gt;: the model, &lt;code&gt;sidecar-reverse-proxy&lt;/code&gt; (NGINX), &lt;code&gt;otel-collector&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;s3-uploader&lt;/code&gt;. The model container and the pod’s &lt;code&gt;app&lt;/code&gt; label are both named after &lt;code&gt;modelName&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;kubectl get pods -l app=$ENDPOINT_NAME -o custom-columns=\
'NAME:.metadata.name,READY:.status.containerStatuses[*].ready,CONTAINERS:.spec.containers[*].name,NODE:.spec.nodeName'
NAME                            READY                 CONTAINERS                                                              NODE
appliance-router-849f466476-4f5v8   true,true,true,true   appliance-router,s3-uploader,sidecar-reverse-proxy,otel-collector   hyperpod-i-0f3fe2684afd6a583&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In our run, the pod was ready about five minutes after &lt;code&gt;kubectl apply&lt;/code&gt;, most of it spent downloading the weights.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-5-send-a-request-and-confirm-captures-land-in-s3"&gt;Step 5: Send a request and confirm captures land in S3&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Send an inference request through the capture proxy on port &lt;strong&gt;8081&lt;/strong&gt;, then, after the flush interval, list the capture prefix. The model itself listens on port 8000. Requests sent there bypass the proxy and are not captured.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;POD=$(kubectl get pod -l app=$ENDPOINT_NAME -o jsonpath='{.items[0].metadata.name}')
kubectl port-forward "pod/$POD" 18081:8081 &amp;gt;/dev/null 2&amp;gt;&amp;amp;1 &amp;amp;
sleep 5

curl -s http://localhost:18081/v1/chat/completions \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"model":"'"$ENDPOINT_NAME"'","messages":[{"role":"user","content":"my fridge is not cooling"}],"max_tokens":24}'

echo "Waiting for the capture buffer to flush..."; sleep 75
aws s3 ls "s3://${BUCKET}/capture/" --recursive | grep '\.jsonl$' | head
2026-09-28 15:47:14     634356 capture/20e4425092f90c7f/pod/AllTraffic/2026/09/28/15/28-15-47-12-999-ac31d31b-5156-46ad-9ccc-e8a040ab9ab2.jsonl&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You see one or more &lt;code&gt;.jsonl&lt;/code&gt; objects under &lt;code&gt;{s3Uri}/{uniqueID}/pod/{variant}/{YYYY}/{MM}/{DD}/{HH}/&lt;/code&gt;. The &lt;code&gt;uniqueID&lt;/code&gt; is generated per endpoint. Each line is one inference in the SageMaker data-capture format shown next.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-captured-record-format"&gt;The captured record format&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The request and response bodies are stored &lt;strong&gt;base64-encoded&lt;/strong&gt;, as the &lt;code&gt;encoding&lt;/code&gt; field shows, so reading them back requires base64-decoding and then parsing JSON:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "captureData": {
    "endpointInput":  { "observedContentType": "application/json", "mode": "INPUT",
                        "data": "eyJtb2RlbCI6ICJyb3V0ZXItZnJlc2giLCAidGVtcGVyYXR1cmUiOiAwLCAi...", "encoding": "BASE64" },
    "endpointOutput": { "observedContentType": "application/json", "mode": "OUTPUT",
                        "data": "eyJpZCI6ImNoYXRjbXBsLTctMTc5MDYxMDM2OS4wMDUtMjMxMiIsIm9iamVj...", "encoding": "BASE64" }
  },
  "eventMetadata": { "eventId": "26859f07-d60b-4683-94aa-d21e7f19e746", "inferenceTime": "2026-09-28T15:46:09Z" },
  "eventVersion": "0"
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Decoded, &lt;code&gt;endpointInput.data&lt;/code&gt; is the request body the client sent and &lt;code&gt;endpointOutput.data&lt;/code&gt; is the response the model returned.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-scenario-a-support-ticket-router-meets-a-product-launch"&gt;The scenario: A support-ticket router meets a product launch&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The deployed model is a support-ticket router for a home-appliance retailer. It uses vLLM guided JSON decoding to classify each ticket into one of six categories: &lt;code&gt;ProductFridge&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;ProductWasher&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;ProductOven&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;ProductDishwasher&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;ProductMicrowave&lt;/code&gt;, or the catch-all &lt;code&gt;Other&lt;/code&gt;. Guided decoding means it can only return a valid object with one of those labels:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{"category": "ProductWasher", "confidence": 1}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After the model was deployed, the retailer launched its own line of air conditioners, the &lt;strong&gt;Arctis&lt;/strong&gt; series (Arctis 9K, 12K, Mini, and so on). The router has no category for them. It can’t say “this is new.” It can only pick the closest label it knows. Unless every request and response is recorded, the evidence disappears the moment the response is returned.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-synthetic-traffic"&gt;The synthetic traffic&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We generated two synthetic datasets:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Production traffic:&lt;/strong&gt; 900 tickets, replayed in order through the capture proxy over 15 minutes, using the router’s original six-label prompt. The first 400 come before the launch and mention no air conditioners. In the remaining 500, the share of Arctis tickets ramps from 15% to 55%.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Evaluation set:&lt;/strong&gt; 280 labeled tickets, 40 per category, including the new &lt;code&gt;ProductAC&lt;/code&gt;. It is held out &lt;em&gt;by content&lt;/em&gt;: it uses symptoms, sentence templates, and Arctis model names that never appear in the traffic.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each production request looks like this (system prompt abbreviated):&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{"model": "appliance-router", "temperature": 0, "max_tokens": 40,
 "messages": [{"role": "system", "content": "You are a support-ticket router for a home-appliance retailer. ... category is one of: ProductFridge, ProductWasher, ProductOven, ProductDishwasher, ProductMicrowave, Other. ..."},
              {"role": "user", "content": "Hi, our Arctis 12K blows warm air since last week. Order #66759."}],
 "guided_json": {"type": "object", "required": ["category", "confidence"],
                 "properties": {"category": {"enum": ["ProductFridge", "ProductWasher", "ProductOven",
                                                      "ProductDishwasher", "ProductMicrowave", "Other"]},
                                "confidence": {"type": "number"}}}}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To replay the traffic, clone the accompanying repository, generate the datasets, and send them through the capture proxy. &lt;code&gt;scripts/pod.sh&lt;/code&gt; runs the client inside the model container, so no load balancer or DNS is needed. The client prints the three timestamps that the Athena steps use: &lt;code&gt;RUN_START&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;LAUNCH_TS&lt;/code&gt; (the first post-launch ticket), and &lt;code&gt;RUN_END&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;git clone https://github.com/awslabs/awsome-distributed-ai.git
cd awsome-distributed-ai/examples/use-cases/sagemaker-hyperpod-data-capture-flywheel
(cd data &amp;amp;&amp;amp; python3 ../scripts/gen_dataset.py)
scripts/pod.sh $ENDPOINT_NAME drive /tmp/tickets_traffic.jsonl --seconds 900
RUN_START=2026-09-28 15:52:40
  sent 100/900
...
LAUNCH_TS=2026-09-28 15:59:20
...
RUN_END=2026-09-28 16:07:40&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Export the three values that your run printed, and wait one flush interval:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;export RUN_START="2026-09-28 15:52:40" LAUNCH_TS="2026-09-28 15:59:20" RUN_END="2026-09-28 16:07:40"
sleep 75    # one flush interval&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="surfacing-the-problem-with-amazon-athena"&gt;Surfacing the problem with Amazon Athena&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Offline benchmarks still look unchanged after the launch, because they are frozen in the past. The shift is visible only in the captured production traffic. This section shows how a data scientist finds it with Athena, without provisioning any infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-6-set-your-variables-and-configure-an-athena-helper-function"&gt;Step 6: Set your variables and configure an Athena helper function&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;export ATHENA_DB="flywheel_demo"
export ATHENA_OUTPUT="s3://${BUCKET}/athena-results/"
# Each endpoint writes under its own uniqueID. Pick the prefix whose captured requests name this
# endpoint, not whichever prefix sorts first (the Step 15 endpoint adds a second one).
for id in $(aws s3 ls "s3://${BUCKET}/capture/" | awk '/PRE/{print $2}'); do
  key=$(aws s3 ls "s3://${BUCKET}/capture/${id}pod/AllTraffic/" --recursive | awk '/\.jsonl$/{print $4; exit}')
  [ -n "$key" ] || continue
  model=$(aws s3 cp "s3://${BUCKET}/${key}" - 2&amp;gt;/dev/null | head -n 1 | python3 -c 'import sys, json, base64; r = json.loads(sys.stdin.readline()); print(json.loads(base64.b64decode(r["captureData"]["endpointInput"]["data"]))["model"])')
  if [ "$model" = "$ENDPOINT_NAME" ]; then export CAPTURE_LOCATION="s3://${BUCKET}/capture/${id}pod/AllTraffic/"; break; fi
done
echo "$CAPTURE_LOCATION"     # s3://&amp;lt;bucket&amp;gt;/capture/&amp;lt;uniqueID&amp;gt;/pod/AllTraffic/

# run_athena "&amp;lt;SQL&amp;gt;": runs one statement, prints tab-separated rows, and sets LAST_QID
run_athena() {
  local qid st
  qid=$(aws athena start-query-execution --query-string "$1" \
        --query-execution-context "Database=$ATHENA_DB" \
        --result-configuration "OutputLocation=$ATHENA_OUTPUT" \
        --output text --query 'QueryExecutionId') || return 1
  while :; do
    st=$(aws athena get-query-execution --query-execution-id "$qid" \
         --output text --query 'QueryExecution.Status.State')
    case "$st" in
      SUCCEEDED) break ;;
      FAILED|CANCELLED) aws athena get-query-execution --query-execution-id "$qid" \
          --output text --query 'QueryExecution.Status.StateChangeReason' &amp;gt;&amp;amp;2; return 1 ;;
    esac
    sleep 2
  done
  LAST_QID=$qid
  aws athena get-query-results --query-execution-id "$qid" \
    --output text --query 'ResultSet.Rows[*].Data[*].VarCharValue'
}
run_athena "CREATE DATABASE IF NOT EXISTS ${ATHENA_DB}"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-7-declare-the-captures-to-athena-two-layers"&gt;Step 7: Declare the captures to Athena (two layers)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The first layer is the envelope. An external table describes the outer JSON of each record, and partition projection over &lt;code&gt;year/month/day/hour&lt;/code&gt; maps the hourly S3 prefixes to partitions without a crawler or &lt;code&gt;ADD PARTITION&lt;/code&gt;. The &lt;code&gt;data&lt;/code&gt; fields are declared as plain strings, because they are base64 text.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;run_athena "
CREATE EXTERNAL TABLE IF NOT EXISTS inference_captures (
  captureData struct&amp;lt;
    endpointInput:  struct&amp;lt;observedContentType: string, mode: string, data: string, encoding: string&amp;gt;,
    endpointOutput: struct&amp;lt;observedContentType: string, mode: string, data: string, encoding: string&amp;gt;
  &amp;gt;,
  eventMetadata struct&amp;lt;eventId: string, inferenceTime: string&amp;gt;,
  eventVersion string
)
PARTITIONED BY (year string, month string, day string, hour string)
ROW FORMAT SERDE 'org.openx.data.jsonserde.JsonSerDe'
LOCATION '${CAPTURE_LOCATION}'
TBLPROPERTIES (
  'projection.enabled'='true',
  'projection.year.type'='integer',  'projection.year.range'='2026,2035',
  'projection.month.type'='integer', 'projection.month.range'='1,12', 'projection.month.digits'='2',
  'projection.day.type'='integer',   'projection.day.range'='1,31',   'projection.day.digits'='2',
  'projection.hour.type'='integer',  'projection.hour.range'='0,23',  'projection.hour.digits'='2',
  'storage.location.template'='${CAPTURE_LOCATION}\${year}/\${month}/\${day}/\${hour}/'
)"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The second layer is a view that decodes each record once. &lt;code&gt;from_utf8(from_base64(...))&lt;/code&gt; turns the payloads back into JSON. Without it, every extraction returns &lt;code&gt;NULL&lt;/code&gt;. The ticket is the user message (&lt;code&gt;messages[1]&lt;/code&gt;, because &lt;code&gt;messages[0]&lt;/code&gt; is the system prompt). The model’s answer is a JSON string inside the response JSON, so it needs a second extraction.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;run_athena "
CREATE OR REPLACE VIEW preds AS
WITH decoded AS (
  SELECT CAST(from_iso8601_timestamp(eventMetadata.inferenceTime) AS timestamp) AS ts,
         from_utf8(from_base64(captureData.endpointInput.data))  AS req,
         from_utf8(from_base64(captureData.endpointOutput.data)) AS resp
  FROM inference_captures
  WHERE year = '${RUN_START:0:4}' AND month = '${RUN_START:5:2}' AND day = '${RUN_START:8:2}'
)
SELECT ts,
       json_extract_scalar(req, '\$.messages[1].content') AS ticket_text,
       json_extract_scalar(json_extract_scalar(resp, '\$.choices[0].message.content'), '\$.category') AS predicted_category,
       CAST(json_extract_scalar(json_extract_scalar(resp, '\$.choices[0].message.content'), '\$.confidence') AS double) AS confidence
FROM decoded
WHERE ts &amp;gt;= timestamp '${RUN_START}' AND ts &amp;lt; timestamp '${RUN_END}'
"
run_athena "SELECT count(*) AS n, count_if(predicted_category IS NULL) AS unparsed FROM preds"
n   unparsed
900 0&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two notes on the view:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Always filter on partitions.&lt;/strong&gt; Athena reads raw JSON, so a query without a partition filter scans every object you have ever captured. The view filters to one day, and the time bound limits it to our traffic run: the same prefix also holds the request from Step 5.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;messages[1]&lt;/code&gt; &lt;strong&gt;assumes one system message followed by one user message.&lt;/strong&gt; Captured traffic is whatever clients actually sent, so adjust the extraction for multi-turn or few-shot requests.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-8-is-the-category-mix-changing"&gt;Step 8: Is the category mix changing?&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;run_athena "
SELECT date_trunc('minute', ts) AS minute, count(*) AS tickets,
       count_if(predicted_category = 'Other') AS other,
       round(100.0 * count_if(predicted_category = 'Other') / count(*), 1) AS other_pct
FROM preds GROUP BY 1 ORDER BY 1"
minute                   tickets  other  other_pct
2026-09-28 15:52:00.000       20      0        0.0
2026-09-28 15:53:00.000       60      3        5.0
2026-09-28 15:54:00.000       60      4        6.7
2026-09-28 15:55:00.000       60      4        6.7
2026-09-28 15:56:00.000       60      2        3.3
2026-09-28 15:57:00.000       60      1        1.7
2026-09-28 15:58:00.000       60      3        5.0
2026-09-28 15:59:00.000       60      9       15.0
2026-09-28 16:00:00.000       60     17       28.3
2026-09-28 16:01:00.000       60     14       23.3
2026-09-28 16:02:00.000       60     19       31.7
2026-09-28 16:03:00.000       60     21       35.0
2026-09-28 16:04:00.000       60     20       33.3
2026-09-28 16:05:00.000       60     32       53.3
2026-09-28 16:06:00.000       60     28       46.7
2026-09-28 16:07:00.000       40     26       65.0&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;(Output is tab-separated. We aligned it here for readability. Our run compressed “weeks” into 15 minutes. In production, bucket by&lt;/em&gt; &lt;code&gt;date_trunc('day', ts)&lt;/code&gt; &lt;em&gt;or&lt;/em&gt; &lt;code&gt;'week'&lt;/code&gt; &lt;em&gt;instead.)&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following chart plots the per-minute share of tickets routed to &lt;code&gt;Other&lt;/code&gt; as bars, and the cumulative share as a line, with the launch marked.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/12/ML-22239-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/12/ML-22239-2.png" alt="Chart of the share of tickets routed to Other. Before the Arctis launch the per-minute share stays under 7%. After it the bars climb to 65%, while the cumulative line reaches only 22.6%." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Per-minute and cumulative share of tickets routed to Other, before and after the Arctis launch&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before the launch, &lt;code&gt;Other&lt;/code&gt; stays under 7% per minute. It jumps to 15% in the launch minute and keeps climbing, reaching 47–65% in the last three minutes. The cumulative line in the chart only reaches 22.6%, because it averages the post-launch tickets with all the traffic from before the launch. That is why the per-minute rate, not the cumulative share, is what you would put behind an alarm.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Split at the launch, the whole category mix looks like this:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;run_athena "
SELECT IF(ts &amp;lt; timestamp '${LAUNCH_TS}', '1-before launch', '2-after launch') AS period,
       predicted_category, count(*) AS n
FROM preds GROUP BY 1, 2 ORDER BY 1, 3 DESC"
period           predicted_category    n
1-before launch  ProductFridge        91
1-before launch  ProductWasher        83
1-before launch  ProductDishwasher    74
1-before launch  ProductOven          74
1-before launch  ProductMicrowave     59
1-before launch  Other                19
2-after launch   Other               184
2-after launch   ProductFridge        84
2-after launch   ProductOven          66
2-after launch   ProductDishwasher    64
2-after launch   ProductWasher        58
2-after launch   ProductMicrowave     44&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;Other&lt;/code&gt; went from &lt;strong&gt;4.8%&lt;/strong&gt; of tickets before the launch (19 of 400) to &lt;strong&gt;36.8%&lt;/strong&gt; after it (184 of 500), and became the largest category.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-9-what-is-inside-the-other-bucket"&gt;Step 9: What is inside the Other bucket?&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;run_athena "
SELECT word, count(*) AS freq
FROM preds CROSS JOIN UNNEST(regexp_extract_all(lower(ticket_text), '[a-z0-9-]{4,}')) AS t(word)
WHERE predicted_category = 'Other'
  AND word NOT IN ('this','that','with','from','have','since','week','help','need','again','time',
                   'month','second','take','look','someone','order','last','after','when','onto')
GROUP BY 1 ORDER BY 2 DESC, 1 LIMIT 10"
word      freq
arctis     141
unit        40
mini        33
cooling     30
link        30
lost        30
loudly      30
rattles     30
wi-fi       30
blows       28&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;Other&lt;/code&gt; bucket isn’t a mix of unrelated tickets. It’s dominated by one product name, &lt;code&gt;arctis&lt;/code&gt;, and the vocabulary of a cooling appliance, which points to a new category the router has no label for.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-10-how-big-is-it-and-does-the-models-confidence-flag-it"&gt;Step 10: How big is it, and does the model’s confidence flag it?&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Search the whole traffic stream for the new product, not just the &lt;code&gt;Other&lt;/code&gt; bucket:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;run_athena "
SELECT IF(ts &amp;lt; timestamp '${LAUNCH_TS}', '1-before launch', '2-after launch') AS period,
       predicted_category, count(*) AS n
FROM preds WHERE regexp_like(lower(ticket_text), 'arctis|air condition')
GROUP BY 1, 2 ORDER BY 1, 3 DESC"
period          predicted_category    n
2-after launch  Other               164
2-after launch  ProductFridge        14&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;There are &lt;strong&gt;178 air-conditioner tickets, 19.8% of all traffic. None of them appeared before the launch.&lt;/strong&gt; They fall into two groups:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;164 went to&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;code&gt;Other&lt;/code&gt;&lt;strong&gt;.&lt;/strong&gt; Under the six-label schema, that is the &lt;em&gt;correct&lt;/em&gt; answer: &lt;code&gt;Other&lt;/code&gt; exists for exactly this case. The router isn’t broken. The taxonomy is missing a category, so these tickets reach a generic queue instead of the team that supports the product.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;14 went to&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;code&gt;ProductFridge&lt;/code&gt;&lt;strong&gt;.&lt;/strong&gt; These are true misroutes: a refrigerator team is now getting air-conditioner tickets.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Now test the model’s confidence on these known misroutes:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;run_athena "
SELECT predicted_category, confidence, count(*) AS n FROM preds
WHERE regexp_like(lower(ticket_text), 'arctis|air condition')
GROUP BY 1, 2 ORDER BY 1, 2 DESC"
predicted_category  confidence    n
Other                      1.0  157
Other                      0.9    7
ProductFridge             0.95    1
ProductFridge              0.9    9
ProductFridge              0.8    2
ProductFridge              0.7    1
ProductFridge              0.0    1&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Of the 14 misrouted tickets, 10 reported a confidence of 0.9 or higher. A labeling rule such as “keep predictions with &lt;code&gt;confidence &amp;gt;= 0.9&lt;/code&gt;” would have kept 71% of the known-wrong labels. That’s because &lt;code&gt;confidence&lt;/code&gt; is a field we asked the model to write, not a calibrated probability. If you need a real uncertainty signal, request token &lt;code&gt;logprobs&lt;/code&gt; from vLLM and read the probability at the category token instead of the model’s self-report. Budget for the extra payload size, because the &lt;code&gt;logprobs&lt;/code&gt; are captured with every response.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="from-diagnosis-to-labeled-training-data"&gt;From diagnosis to labeled training data&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A data scientist doesn’t hand-label hundreds of tickets. Instead, they write the Athena finding down as a &lt;strong&gt;labeling policy&lt;/strong&gt;: a SQL view that assigns a corrected label to every captured record. We relabel the misrouted air-conditioner tickets to the new class.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-11-the-labeling-policy"&gt;Step 11: The labeling policy&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;run_athena "
CREATE OR REPLACE VIEW labeled_training_data AS
WITH t AS (SELECT ticket_text, predicted_category, lower(ticket_text) AS txt FROM preds)
SELECT ticket_text, predicted_category,
  CASE
    -- 1. The new class, identified by keyword evidence, not by what the model predicted
    WHEN regexp_like(txt, '\\barctis\\b|air condition|\\bac unit\\b|mini split')
     AND NOT regexp_like(txt, 'fridge|refrigerat|freezer|washer|\\boven\\b|\\brange\\b|dishwasher|microwave')
      THEN 'ProductAC'
    -- 2. Keep the production decision when nothing hints at the new product
    WHEN NOT regexp_like(txt, '\\barctis\\b|air condition|\\bac\\b|mini split')
      THEN predicted_category
    -- 3. Mixed signals: send to human review (Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth)
    ELSE NULL
  END AS corrected_category
FROM t"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The doubled backslashes (&lt;code&gt;\\b&lt;/code&gt;) are shell escaping inside the double-quoted string. Athena receives &lt;code&gt;\b&lt;/code&gt;, a regular-expression word boundary.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Check what the policy produced:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;run_athena "
SELECT coalesce(corrected_category, '(human review)') AS label, count(*) AS n,
       count(DISTINCT ticket_text) AS distinct_texts,
       count_if(corrected_category &amp;lt;&amp;gt; predicted_category) AS relabeled
FROM labeled_training_data GROUP BY 1 ORDER BY 2 DESC"
label               n    distinct_texts  relabeled
ProductAC         178               114        178
ProductFridge     161               110          0
ProductWasher     141                96          0
ProductOven       140                97          0
ProductDishwasher 138                95          0
ProductMicrowave  103                82          0
Other              39                 8          0&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This heuristic works because the new category is clear-cut (keyword-identifiable). For ambiguous tickets or overlapping categories, the NULL rows would route to Ground Truth for human labeling.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-12-build-the-training-file"&gt;Step 12: Build the training file&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Export the labeled view and convert it to chat-format JSONL. The allowed category set now includes &lt;code&gt;ProductAC&lt;/code&gt;, the same seven-label prompt clients will send after the redeploy. Save this as &lt;code&gt;build_training_file.py&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;cat &amp;gt; build_training_file.py &amp;lt;&amp;lt;'PY'
import csv, json, sys
CATS = ["ProductFridge", "ProductWasher", "ProductOven", "ProductDishwasher", "ProductMicrowave", "ProductAC", "Other"]
PROMPT = ("You are a support-ticket router for a home-appliance retailer. Route each ticket to the product it "
          'concerns. Respond with ONLY a JSON object {"category": "...", "confidence": &amp;lt;0-1&amp;gt;} where category is '
          "one of: " + ", ".join(CATS) + '. Use "Other" if the ticket does not clearly concern one of these products.')
n = 0
with open(sys.argv[1], newline="") as fin, open(sys.argv[2], "w") as fout:
    for row in csv.DictReader(fin):
        fout.write(json.dumps({"messages": [
            {"role": "system", "content": PROMPT},
            {"role": "user", "content": row["ticket_text"]},
            {"role": "assistant", "content": json.dumps({"category": row["corrected_category"], "confidence": 1.0})},
        ]}) + "\n"); n += 1
print(f"wrote {n} training examples -&amp;gt; {sys.argv[2]}")
PY

run_athena "SELECT ticket_text, corrected_category FROM labeled_training_data
            WHERE corrected_category IS NOT NULL" &amp;gt; /dev/null
aws s3 cp "${ATHENA_OUTPUT}${LAST_QID}.csv" labeled.csv --only-show-errors   # Athena's own CSV: header + proper quoting
python3 build_training_file.py labeled.csv train.jsonl
aws s3 cp train.jsonl "s3://${BUCKET}/training/train.jsonl"
wrote 900 training examples -&amp;gt; train.jsonl&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="fine-tuning-on-captured-data-with-the-hyperpod-training-operator"&gt;Fine-tuning on captured data with the HyperPod training operator&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We fine-tune on the same cluster that serves the model, using the HyperPod training operator on the second GPU. The first endpoint keeps serving throughout.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-13-the-training-script"&gt;Step 13: The training script&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The script downloads the base model and training data from S3 and trains a low-rank adaptation (LoRA) adapter. The loss is computed only on the assistant turn, so the model learns to produce the label rather than repeat the prompt. The script then merges the adapter back into the base weights, so vLLM can serve the result unchanged, and uploads the merged model to S3. The job mounts the script into the training pod from a ConfigMap.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;cat &amp;gt; train.py &amp;lt;&amp;lt;'PY'
import os, boto3, torch
from datasets import load_dataset
from peft import LoraConfig, get_peft_model
from transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM, AutoTokenizer, Trainer, TrainingArguments

s3 = boto3.client("s3")
def split(uri): b, _, k = uri[5:].partition("/"); return b, k

# 1. Stage base model + training data from S3
bucket, prefix = split(os.environ["BASE_MODEL_S3"])
for page in s3.get_paginator("list_objects_v2").paginate(Bucket=bucket, Prefix=prefix):
    for o in page.get("Contents", []):
        dst = os.path.join("/work/base", os.path.relpath(o["Key"], prefix))
        os.makedirs(os.path.dirname(dst), exist_ok=True); s3.download_file(bucket, o["Key"], dst)
s3.download_file(*split(os.environ["TRAIN_FILE_S3"]), "/work/train.jsonl")
print("[stage] base model and training data downloaded", flush=True)

# 2. LoRA SFT, loss only on the assistant turn (the label), not the prompt
tok = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained("/work/base")
def encode(ex):
    full = tok.apply_chat_template(ex["messages"], tokenize=False)
    prompt = tok.apply_chat_template(ex["messages"][:-1], tokenize=False, add_generation_prompt=True)
    ids = tok(full, add_special_tokens=False)["input_ids"]
    n = len(tok(prompt, add_special_tokens=False)["input_ids"])
    return {"input_ids": ids, "labels": [-100] * n + ids[n:]}
ds = load_dataset("json", data_files="/work/train.jsonl", split="train")
print(f"[data] {len(ds)} examples", flush=True)
ds = ds.map(encode, remove_columns=ds.column_names)

def collate(batch):
    m = max(len(b["input_ids"]) for b in batch)
    pad = lambda xs, v: [x + [v] * (m - len(x)) for x in xs]
    return {"input_ids": torch.tensor(pad([b["input_ids"] for b in batch], tok.pad_token_id)),
            "labels": torch.tensor(pad([b["labels"] for b in batch], -100)),
            "attention_mask": torch.tensor(pad([[1] * len(b["input_ids"]) for b in batch], 0))}

model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained("/work/base", torch_dtype=torch.bfloat16, device_map="cuda:0")
model = get_peft_model(model, LoraConfig(r=16, lora_alpha=32, lora_dropout=0.05, task_type="CAUSAL_LM",
    target_modules=["q_proj", "k_proj", "v_proj", "o_proj", "gate_proj", "up_proj", "down_proj"]))
model.print_trainable_parameters()
Trainer(model=model, train_dataset=ds, data_collator=collate, args=TrainingArguments(
    output_dir="/tmp/ckpt", num_train_epochs=float(os.getenv("EPOCHS", "2")), learning_rate=1e-4,
    per_device_train_batch_size=8, bf16=True, logging_steps=10, save_strategy="no", report_to=[],
    warmup_ratio=0.1, lr_scheduler_type="cosine")).train()

# 3. Merge the adapter into the base weights (a plain model vLLM can serve) and upload it
model.merge_and_unload().save_pretrained("/work/out", safe_serialization=True)
tok.save_pretrained("/work/out")
bucket, prefix = split(os.environ["OUTPUT_S3"])
for f in os.listdir("/work/out"):
    s3.upload_file(os.path.join("/work/out", f), bucket, f"{prefix.rstrip('/')}/{f}")
print(f"[done] merged model uploaded to {os.environ['OUTPUT_S3']}", flush=True)
PY
kubectl create configmap router-train --from-file=train.py&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-14-submit-a-hyperpodpytorchjob"&gt;Step 14: Submit a HyperPodPyTorchJob&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;cat &amp;gt; finetune-job.yaml &amp;lt;&amp;lt;'YAML'
apiVersion: sagemaker.amazonaws.com/v1
kind: HyperPodPyTorchJob
metadata:
  name: router-ft
  namespace: default
spec:
  nprocPerNode: "1"
  runPolicy:
    jobMaxRetryCount: 2
    cleanPodPolicy: All              # release the GPU as soon as the job finishes
    logMonitoringConfiguration:      # restart a job that never starts, or goes quiet mid-run
      - name: JobStart
        logPattern: '.*loss.*'
        expectedStartCutOffInSeconds: 1200
      - name: JobHangingDetection
        logPattern: '.*loss.*'
        expectedRecurringFrequencyInSeconds: 600
  replicaSpecs:
    - name: pod
      replicas: 1
      template:
        metadata:
          labels: { app: router-ft }
        spec:
          serviceAccountName: ${TRAIN_SA}        # EKS Pod Identity -&amp;gt; S3 read/write
          nodeSelector:
            node.kubernetes.io/instance-type: ${TRAIN_INSTANCE_TYPE}
          restartPolicy: Always                  # required by the operator; retries live in runPolicy
          containers:
            - name: pytorch
              image: vllm/vllm-openai:v0.10.0    # already has torch + transformers + boto3
              command: ["bash", "-c"]
              args:
                - pip install -q peft==0.14.0 datasets==3.2.0 hyperpod-elastic-agent==1.1.3 &amp;amp;&amp;amp;
                  hyperpodrun --nnodes=1 --nproc_per_node=1 /scripts/train.py
              env:
                - { name: BASE_MODEL_S3, value: "s3://${BUCKET}/${MODEL_LOCATION}/" }
                - { name: TRAIN_FILE_S3, value: "s3://${BUCKET}/training/train.jsonl" }
                - { name: OUTPUT_S3,     value: "s3://${BUCKET}/${MODEL_V2_LOCATION}/" }
              resources:
                requests: { cpu: "2", memory: 24Gi, nvidia.com/gpu: 1 }
                limits:   { nvidia.com/gpu: 1 }
              volumeMounts:
                - { name: scripts, mountPath: /scripts }
                - { name: work,    mountPath: /work }
                - { name: shm,     mountPath: /dev/shm }
          volumes:
            - { name: scripts, configMap: { name: router-train } }
            - { name: work,    emptyDir: {} }
            - { name: shm,     emptyDir: { medium: Memory, sizeLimit: 8Gi } }
YAML
render &amp;lt; finetune-job.yaml | kubectl apply -f -
kubectl logs -l app=router-ft -f&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;logMonitoringConfiguration&lt;/code&gt; &lt;strong&gt;is the training operator’s distinguishing feature.&lt;/strong&gt; If no &lt;code&gt;loss&lt;/code&gt; line appears within 20 minutes of start, or the log goes quiet for 10 minutes mid-run while the process is still alive, the operator restarts the job instead of letting it burn GPU hours.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The job installs three Python packages when it starts.&lt;/strong&gt; The training node needs outbound internet access, for example through a NAT gateway, and so does pulling the &lt;code&gt;vllm/vllm-openai&lt;/code&gt; image from Docker Hub. On a cluster without internet egress, bake the packages into a custom image in Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR) and drop the &lt;code&gt;pip install&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An excerpt of the job log:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;{'loss': 0.2484, 'grad_norm': 3.387, 'learning_rate': 3.91e-05, 'epoch': 0.09}
{'loss': 0.018, 'grad_norm': 0.0037, 'learning_rate': 8.26e-05, 'epoch': 0.18}
...
{'loss': 0.0, 'grad_norm': 0.000105, 'learning_rate': 2.93e-07, 'epoch': 1.95}
{'train_runtime': 59.68, 'train_samples_per_second': 30.16, 'train_loss': 0.0123, 'epoch': 2.0}
[done] merged model uploaded to s3://amzn-s3-demo-bucket/models/qwen2.5-3b-router-v2/
[HyperPodElasticAgent] ... Transitioned from AgentState.RUNNING to AgentState.COMPLETED
kubectl get hyperpodpytorchjob router-ft -o jsonpath='{range .status.conditions[*]}=  {end}'
Created=True  Completed=True&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Training took &lt;strong&gt;60 seconds&lt;/strong&gt; on one L40S. LoRA at rank 16 trains about 29.9 million parameters, under 1% of the 3.1-billion-parameter model. The average training loss was 0.0123, and the per-step loss reached 0.0 within the first epoch, which shows the model memorizes 900 templated examples readily. On real data, hold out a validation split and stop early. Here we rely on the held-out evaluation set.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-15-deploy-the-fine-tuned-model-side-by-side-with-capture-still-on"&gt;Step 15: Deploy the fine-tuned model side by side, with capture still on&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Instead of editing the running endpoint, deploy the new model as a &lt;strong&gt;second endpoint&lt;/strong&gt; from the same manifest, onto the GPU the training job just released. The first endpoint keeps serving until you cut clients over, so the redeploy causes no downtime, and rolling back means pointing clients back at the first endpoint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;export ENDPOINT_NAME="appliance-router-v2" MODEL_LOCATION="$MODEL_V2_LOCATION" SERVE_INSTANCE_TYPE="$TRAIN_INSTANCE_TYPE"
render &amp;lt; endpoint.yaml | kubectl apply -f -
kubectl get pods -l app=$ENDPOINT_NAME -o custom-columns='NAME:.metadata.name,READY:.status.containerStatuses[*].ready,NODE:.spec.nodeName'
NAME                                  READY                 NODE
appliance-router-v2-c96594c5-fhs2x    true,true,true,true   hyperpod-i-0e0b756209b5e497a&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The new endpoint writes captures under its own &lt;code&gt;uniqueID&lt;/code&gt;. Because &lt;code&gt;ENDPOINT_NAME&lt;/code&gt; is now &lt;code&gt;appliance-router-v2&lt;/code&gt;, rerunning the &lt;code&gt;CAPTURE_LOCATION&lt;/code&gt; lookup from Step 6 finds that prefix, so you can point a second Athena table at it for the next round of analysis. When you move clients over, update the endpoint, the label set, and the guided-JSON enum in the same release.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="measuring-the-lift"&gt;Measuring the lift&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We ran the same 280 held-out tickets against the fine-tuned endpoint:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-text"&gt;overall 280/280 = 100.00%
  ProductFridge      40/40
  ProductWasher      40/40
  ProductOven        40/40
  ProductDishwasher  40/40
  ProductMicrowave   40/40
  ProductAC          40/40
  Other              40/40&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Metric&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Before fine-tuning (as launched)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;After fine-tuning&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Held-out accuracy (280)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;235 (83.9%)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;280 (100%)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;ProductAC&lt;/code&gt; (40)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0/40 — all routed to &lt;code&gt;Other&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;40/40&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;Other&lt;/code&gt; (40)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;35/40&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;40/40&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Five established product classes (200)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;200/200&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;200/200&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Hand-written probe (24, described next)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;16/24&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;20/24&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Fine-tuning taught the router the new category and caused no regression on the five established classes. It also fixed five &lt;code&gt;Other&lt;/code&gt; errors that existed before the launch which the launched router sent to &lt;code&gt;ProductFridge&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because our evaluation set comes from the same generator as the training data, we also wrote 24 probe tickets by hand, in styles the generator never produces. The fine-tuned model scored 20/24:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-text"&gt;  MISS ProductFridge -&amp;gt; ProductAC: The cooling fan in my fridge is squealing.
  MISS Other -&amp;gt; ProductAC: When will the Arctis 12K be back in stock?
  MISS Other -&amp;gt; ProductAC: Can I get a quote to install central air in my house?
  MISS Other -&amp;gt; ProductAC: Do you price match the Arctis at other stores?
overall 20/24 = 83.33%&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;It routes all eight air-conditioner probes correctly, but it learned two shortcuts: “cooling” means air conditioner, and any mention of Arctis means &lt;code&gt;ProductAC&lt;/code&gt;, including stock and price-match questions. Both come from the training data: the labeling policy sent every Arctis ticket to &lt;code&gt;ProductAC&lt;/code&gt;, and the captured traffic contained no pre-sales questions. With capture on for the new endpoint, those tickets are exactly what the next round of capture collects.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-much-latency-does-capture-add"&gt;How much latency does capture add?&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We measured this directly: the same 400 routing requests, sent from inside the pod either to port 8000 (no capture) or to port 8081 (through the capture proxy). We ran three rounds at 8 concurrent requests, plus one serial round:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Path&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Concurrency&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;p50&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;p99&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Throughput&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Direct to vLLM (8000), no capture&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;8&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;200.7 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;249.4 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;39.1 req/s&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Through capture proxy (8081)&lt;/td&gt;

&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Rows at concurrency 8 are the average of three rounds. Round-to-round variation (±1 ms at p50) was larger than the difference between the two paths.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="other-uses-for-captured-data"&gt;Other uses for captured data&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After inference data lands in S3, the same records feed many workflows:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Improve the model:&lt;/strong&gt; Use supervised fine-tuning (shown here), distill a large model’s outputs into a smaller model, or build preference pairs for direct preference optimization (DPO).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Understand the model:&lt;/strong&gt; Run Athena analytics (shown here), build golden regression sets from real prompts, set drift alarms, discover emerging intents, and detect malformed outputs and refusals.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Operate and govern:&lt;/strong&gt; Route ambiguous samples to Ground Truth for labeling, keep audit trails (Tier 1 is recommended), replay the exact input behind an incident, and analyze traffic and payloads for cost attribution.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="cost-considerations"&gt;Cost considerations&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tier&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Approximate cost impact&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Tier 1 (SageMaker endpoint)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Captured by SageMaker at the endpoint. You pay S3 storage and requests for the payloads&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Tier 2 (ALB access logs)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;S3 storage for the log files only&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Tier 3 (model pod)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Three sidecars requesting &lt;strong&gt;250m CPU + 640 MiB&lt;/strong&gt; per pod in total (NGINX proxy 100m/128Mi, OTel collector 100m/256Mi, &lt;code&gt;s3-uploader&lt;/code&gt; 50m/256Mi), plus S3 storage and requests for the payloads&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two costs scale with usage:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;S3.&lt;/strong&gt; Our records averaged about 2.3 KB each (3,955 records in 9.0 MB). Longer prompts and responses cost proportionally more. The uploader wrote one object per flush interval per pod, about 1,440 PUTs per pod per day at 60 seconds.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Athena&lt;/strong&gt; charges by data scanned, so always filter on the partition columns, and convert the raw JSON to Parquet with a &lt;code&gt;CREATE TABLE AS SELECT&lt;/code&gt; (CTAS) query for repeated analysis.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For current prices, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon S3 pricing&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/athena/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Athena pricing&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid incurring unnecessary charges, clean up the resources created in this walkthrough:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;kubectl delete inferenceendpointconfig appliance-router appliance-router-v2
kubectl delete hyperpodpytorchjob router-ft
kubectl delete configmap router-train
run_athena "DROP VIEW IF EXISTS labeled_training_data"; run_athena "DROP VIEW IF EXISTS preds"
run_athena "DROP TABLE IF EXISTS inference_captures"; run_athena "DROP DATABASE IF EXISTS ${ATHENA_DB}"
aws s3 rm "s3://${BUCKET}/capture/" --recursive
aws s3 rm "s3://${BUCKET}/athena-results/" --recursive
aws s3 rm "s3://${BUCKET}/training/" --recursive
aws s3 rm "s3://${BUCKET}/${MODEL_V2_LOCATION}/" --recursive&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we showed how you can use SageMaker HyperPod Data Capture to record live inference data and turn it into training data. With capture available at three independent tiers, you can:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Collect real-world training data with no measurable impact on serving latency.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Query inference patterns at scale with Amazon Athena, with no infrastructure to manage.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Turn an Athena diagnosis into a reproducible SQL labeling policy.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Fine-tune on captured production data and measure a hard before/after lift.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Integrate with the HyperPod training operator and SageMaker Ground Truth for the rest of the loop.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We ran one full turn of the loop on a home-appliance support router. Captured traffic revealed a new-product blind spot, and a SQL labeling policy turned those captures into training data. A 60-second LoRA fine-tune with the HyperPod training operator ran on the same cluster that served the model. It took the new category from 0% to full recall without a regression on any existing category. None of that training data existed before the model was deployed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Data Capture is available today on SageMaker HyperPod clusters with the inference operator. To get started, add the &lt;code&gt;dataCapture&lt;/code&gt; section to your &lt;code&gt;InferenceEndpointConfig&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;JumpStartModel&lt;/code&gt; resource and deploy. To close the loop on the same cluster, add the &lt;code&gt;amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-training-operator&lt;/code&gt; EKS add-on. The complete code for this walkthrough is in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/awsome-distributed-ai/tree/main/examples/use-cases/sagemaker-hyperpod-data-capture-flywheel" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;accompanying GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For more information, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-model-deployment.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker HyperPod inference documentation&lt;/a&gt; and the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-eks-operator.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;HyperPod training operator documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Deploy frontier AI models on AWS Trainium with SGLang-Neuron</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/deploy-frontier-ai-models-on-aws-trainium-with-sglang-neuron/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Alex Nails]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 12 Nov 2026 22:03:34 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon EC2]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AWS Trainium]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">d27fba94181475674c8d9e0d16f89e5b62d0fc4a</guid>

					<description>SGLang-Neuron is now publicly available through an AWS Deep Learning Container. With SGLang-Neuron, you can serve models and generate reinforcement learning rollouts with SGLang on AWS Trainium using the same SGLang interface you already know, all running on native PyTorch.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Today, Amazon announced that &lt;a href="http://github.com/sgl-project/sglang-neuron" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SGLang-Neuron&lt;/a&gt; is publicly available and can be accessed through an AWS Deep Learning Container, released by RadixArk in collaboration with Annapurna Labs. With SGLang-Neuron, you can serve models and generate reinforcement learning (RL) rollouts with SGLang on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ai/machine-learning/trainium/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Trainium&lt;/a&gt; using the same SGLang interface you use today. Trainium is a portfolio of AWS AI chips that delivers strong economics for AI training and inference at scale. Now, running SGLang on Trainium follows the SGLang workflow you already know: you launch models through SGLang’s server interface and invoke the endpoint using the same generation APIs. Underneath that interface, SGLang-Neuron runs on native PyTorch. With its out-of-tree plugin design, Trainium support and SGLang core develop independently and iterate faster.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-sglang-runs-on-trainium"&gt;How SGLang runs on Trainium&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;SGLang-Neuron is an out-of-tree plugin that connects SGLang with AWS Trainium. Installing the plugin registers the neuron platform through SGLang’s plugin entry points, and setting &lt;code&gt;SGLANG_PLATFORM=neuron&lt;/code&gt; selects it when the server starts. SGLang core continues to provide the API server, request lifecycle, RadixAttention, and prefix-cache bookkeeping. The plugin implements the Trainium-specific models, attention, mixture of experts (MoE), and the graph runner.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/12/ML-22453-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/12/ML-22453-1.png" alt="Architecture of SGLang-Neuron on AWS Trainium, showing SGLang core and the Trainium plugin components" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: SGLang-Neuron on AWS Trainium&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://awsdocs-neuron.readthedocs-hosted.com/en/latest/torch/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;TorchNeuron&lt;/a&gt; registers Trainium as a native PyTorch device. SGLang-Neuron uses standard PyTorch operations for the model execution path and incorporates kernels written with the &lt;a href="https://awsdocs-neuron.readthedocs-hosted.com/en/latest/nki/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Neuron Kernel Interface&lt;/a&gt; (NKI) for performance. It compiles these operations and kernels into executable programs using &lt;code&gt;torch.compile&lt;/code&gt;, which the graph runner prepares and executes during serving.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;SGLang-Neuron also supports &lt;a href="https://docs.sglang.io/docs/advanced_features/pd_disaggregation" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;PD disaggregation&lt;/a&gt;. In this configuration, a prefill server computes the prompt KV cache and transfers it to a decode server using &lt;a href="https://github.com/ai-dynamo/nixl" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;NIXL&lt;/a&gt; over &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSEC2/latest/UserGuide/efa-start-nixl.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Elastic Fabric Adapter (EFA)&lt;/a&gt;. The decode server then generates the remaining tokens. Each server still uses SGLang’s scheduler and request lifecycle, while the plugin manages the Trainium execution and KV-cache transfer path.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="serving-models-on-aws-trainium-using-sglang-neuron"&gt;Serving models on AWS Trainium using SGLang-Neuron&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To follow this walkthrough, you use a Trainium3 Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2) instance with a &lt;code&gt;pytorch-inference-sglang-neuronx&lt;/code&gt; container. Here are the steps:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Start the container. 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;docker run --rm -it \
--privileged --ipc=host \
-v /path/to/work:/work \
public.ecr.aws/neuron/pytorch-inference-sglang-neuron:v0.1.0-neuron-py314-sdk-2.33.0-ubuntu26.04 \
/bin/bash&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Launch the SGLang model server inside the container. 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;SGLANG_PLATFORM=neuron \
python -m sglang.launch_server \
--model-path openai/gpt-oss-120b \
--tp-size 8 \
--ep-size 8 \
--kv-cache-dtype fp8_e4m3 \
--context-length 10240 \
--chunked-prefill-size 8192 \
--max-running-requests 64 \
--max-total-tokens 655360 \
--mem-fraction-static 0.8 \
--host 127.0.0.1 \
--port 30000&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Send a request to the model server. 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;curl -s http://127.0.0.1:30000/v1/chat/completions \
-H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
-d '{
"model": "openai/gpt-oss-120b",
"messages": [
{"role": "user", "content": "Explain why the sky appears blue."}
],
"temperature": 0,
"max_tokens": 128
}'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For additional information, please see the SGLang-Neuron getting started guide for setup instructions, and explore the repository documentation for supported models, features, and validated configurations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="http://github.com/sgl-project/sglang-neuron" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SGLang-Neuron&lt;/a&gt; brings SGLang’s familiar serving workflow to &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ai/machine-learning/trainium/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Trainium&lt;/a&gt; through native PyTorch. You can get started by launching the &lt;a href="https://gallery.ecr.aws/neuron/pytorch-inference-sglang-neuron" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SGLang-Neuron container&lt;/a&gt; and following the &lt;a href="https://github.com/sgl-project/sglang-neuron/blob/main/docs/getting-started/quickstart.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;quickstart&lt;/a&gt; to serve a supported model. Try it with your workloads, contribute through the project repository, and share feedback to help shape future model and feature support.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;
