			</item>
		<item>
		<title>How Natera designed an agentic AI system for lab ops with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-natera-designed-an-agentic-ai-system-for-lab-ops-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Ariel Jirau]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 12 Nov 2026 21:47:07 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">787b1b13cc7c9546be4afedb6d427a9b0c7e6990</guid>

					<description>Natera built SWARM, an agentic AI system on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore that captures institutional lab-operations knowledge and deploys it at scale. This post walks through the full architecture, from knowledge base construction to deployment and evaluation, delivering about 130x faster first response and 96.2% automated coverage across support tickets.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;At &lt;a href="https://www.natera.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Natera&lt;/a&gt;, a leader in cell-free DNA (cfDNA) testing, keeping lab instruments up and running is critical, because equipment downtime disrupts sample processing. Natera’s lab engineering team, led by Ariel Jirau worked with AWS to build SWARM (Systematic Workflow Analytics and Resolutions Manager), an agentic AI system for lab operations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post walks through how the Natera team built an agentic AI system for lab operations using &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; that captures and deploys institutional knowledge at scale. It describes the full architecture, from knowledge base construction to automated system deployment and evaluation, so you can apply the same patterns to your own operational workflows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;SWARM delivers about 130x faster first response time and 96.2 percent automated coverage across support tickets, giving engineers immediate access to institutional knowledge the moment a ticket arrives.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-challenge-knowledge-continuity"&gt;The challenge: Knowledge continuity&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Natera’s lab operations rely on a wide range of precision instruments spanning multiple equipment families, each with its own failure modes, vendor documentation, and resolution patterns. The service engineering team responsible for maintaining this infrastructure is organized around deep individual expertise rather than standardized playbooks. When a senior technician who had spent years diagnosing a particular instrument family departed, the institutional knowledge they carried was effectively lost. No system existed to capture, organize, or deploy that expertise at scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With over 65 tickets per day across three Jira queues and an average first response time of 17.4 hours, instrument downtime delayed lab samples and patient reporting. Hiring more engineers couldn’t keep pace. SWARM had to respond in minutes, deliver accurate diagnostics across a wide range of equipment types, and improve continuously through a structured evaluation loop.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="why-natera-chose-amazon-bedrock-agentcore"&gt;Why Natera chose Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Natera team chose to build SWARM on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, a fully managed, extensible service by AWS designed to build, deploy, connect, secure, and operate AI agents at scale. Three core requirements drove the decision:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Autonomous processing of high-volume support tickets without human intervention.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Deep integration with existing operational tools such as Jira and Confluence.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Built-in security and observability (encryption, AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) integration, and distributed tracing) without requiring the team to build and maintain custom infrastructure.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;“We needed a managed runtime that would let us focus engineering effort on diagnostic accuracy rather than infrastructure plumbing—AgentCore gave us that, along with built-in identity management and the flexibility to swap models without rewriting agent logic,” Jirau said.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore provides native integration with services including &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/dynamodb/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon DynamoDB&lt;/a&gt;, a fully managed NoSQL database service, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sqs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Queue Service (Amazon SQS)&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/xray/latest/devguide/aws-xray.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS X-Ray&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon CloudWatch.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-swarm-works-two-agents-one-skill-library"&gt;How SWARM works: Two agents, one skill library&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;SWARM uses two complementary runtimes sharing a single skill library. The swarm-agent autonomously generates structured diagnostic reports before an engineer opens a ticket, while engineers use swarm-hive to interact in real time, upload images, and explore alternative troubleshooting paths.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;“The system does not replace engineers—it gives them access to the full depth of institutional knowledge the moment a ticket arrives,” Jirau said.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Four layers connect the architecture:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The event layer&lt;/strong&gt; routes Jira tickets through Amazon EventBridge and Amazon SQS.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The agent layer&lt;/strong&gt; hosts swarm-agent, swarm-hive, and the skill library, and delivers diagnostic reports back to engineers.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The context layer&lt;/strong&gt; draws on vendor manuals, historical tickets, and operational data.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The observability and delivery layer&lt;/strong&gt; captures traces, logs, and metrics.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ML-21653-1-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ML-21653-1-1.png" alt="SWARM architecture on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore showing the event, agent, context, and observability and delivery layers" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Architecture of Natera’s SWARM on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="security-architecture"&gt;Security architecture&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Natera team’s first architectural decision was where to run the agents. The team required a managed runtime that would handle container orchestration, scaling, and security without requiring them to maintain custom infrastructure. They chose AgentCore runtime, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, because it hosts both swarm-agent and swarm-hive in a secure, managed environment with built-in identity management and monitoring, avoiding the need to build and operate a custom agent execution layer. You can adopt this pattern regardless of the framework you choose.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The team chose a keyless deployment model because SWARM runs in a clinical environment where credentials exposure is unacceptable. OpenID Connect (OIDC) and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/STS/latest/APIReference/Welcome.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Security Token Service (AWS STS)&lt;/a&gt; generate temporary credentials scoped per deployment, with no long-lived keys in the pipeline. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM)&lt;/a&gt; enforces least privilege at the component level. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/vpc/latest/userguide/what-is-amazon-vpc.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Virtual Private Cloud (Amazon VPC)&lt;/a&gt; with private subnets and VPC endpoints keeps traffic off the public internet. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/secrets-manager/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Secrets Manager&lt;/a&gt; rotates the few credentials that exist on an automatic 30-day cycle.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;GitLab CI runners authenticate to AWS by presenting an OIDC token to an IAM identity provider configured in the account. AWS STS exchanges the token for short-lived credentials scoped to the deployment role (no long-lived access keys are stored in the pipeline). For setup details, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/id_roles_providers_create_oidc.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;IAM OIDC provider documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following example illustrates the pattern: an IAM OIDC trust policy for GitLab CI follows this structure:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Principal": {
        "Federated": "arn:aws:iam::ACCOUNT_ID:oidc-provider/gitlab.com"
      },
      "Action": "sts:AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity",
      "Condition": {
        "StringEquals": {
          "gitlab.com:sub": "project_path:YOUR_PROJECT:ref_type:branch:ref:main"
        }
      }
    }
  ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These infrastructure-level security controls work in concert with SWARM’s data governance policies. Together they form the full compliance posture required for operating an AI system in a regulated clinical environment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="compliance-and-data-governance"&gt;Compliance and data governance&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;SWARM runs on HIPAA-eligible AWS services under a Business Associate Agreement (&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/compliance/hipaa-compliance/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;BAA&lt;/a&gt;), reflecting Natera’s obligations as a clinical laboratory operator. Every agent invocation is audit-logged (a searchable record that satisfies data access traceability requirements). No patient-identifiable information touches the vector index or the language model. Tickets contain only equipment and workflow metadata. Teams building under HIPAA or similar regulatory frameworks should confirm BAA coverage and data separation requirements before designing the knowledge base pipeline.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="knowledge-base-and-document-pipeline-construction"&gt;Knowledge base and document pipeline construction&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Early prototypes using general-purpose retrieval without equipment-specific metadata produced inconsistent results, so the Natera team built a custom document processing pipeline tailored to technical equipment documentation. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/titan-embedding-models.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Titan Text Embeddings&lt;/a&gt; generates vector representations for semantic search, and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/knowledge-bases/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases&lt;/a&gt; provides fully managed Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG), converting documents into embeddings stored in batches of 100, a size chosen to balance indexing throughput against API rate limits. For heavily structured source documents with tables and diagrams, investing in a custom parsing pipeline rather than relying on default chunking compounds accuracy gains across every diagnosis.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock services, including Amazon Titan Text Embeddings, and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore are available in multiple AWS Regions. For the latest information, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-region-compatibility.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock supported Regions&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/agentcore-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore supported Regions&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Natera team uses the tiktoken tokenizer with the cl100k_base encoding to measure chunk boundaries. The following example illustrates the pattern, the chunking configuration follows a standard structure:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# Document chunking configuration
chunk_size = 1000  # tokens
chunk_overlap = 150  # tokens
encoding = tiktoken.get_encoding("cl100k_base")
batch_size = 100  # documents per embedding API call&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For more on batch ingestion patterns, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/kb-how-it-works.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;“Diagnostic accuracy is a data quality problem first,” Jirau said. “Adding missing vendor manuals for the lowest-scoring instrument family lifted accuracy from 71% to 75% with no model changes.”&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This shaped the Natera team’s improvement approach: when accuracy plateaus, the team checks data coverage before model tuning, because gaps in vendor documentation are the most common cause of missed diagnoses. The pipeline reprocesses new or updated documents and refreshes the vector index without disrupting active operations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="skill-library-architecture"&gt;Skill library architecture&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Natera team faced a key architectural choice: embed logic directly in agent prompts, or build a separate, versioned skill library. Natera chose the library approach because it makes SWARM auditable and improvable.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;“When a diagnosis is wrong, we need to point to a specific skill rather than debug the whole system — that’s why we built a versioned skill library from the start,” Jirau said.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The library contains 33 skills: 24 instrument-specific skills covering the full range of equipment families Natera supports, plus 9 cross-cutting workflow skills that apply broadly across ticket types. Each skill has a defined interface, is independently testable, and is evaluated against the same standards as the overall system, treated as production software, not embedded prompt logic.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The skill architecture follows a clear split: Python handles deterministic tasks such as parsing Jira fields or formatting HTML reports, while large language model (LLM) skills handle language reasoning such as interpreting symptoms, matching vendor documentation, and generating diagnostic narratives. For teams building a multi-skill agent, this split between deterministic Python skills and LLM-powered reasoning skills is one of the highest-impact decisions to make.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="agent-runtime-deployment"&gt;Agent runtime deployment&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Natera split diagnostic work into two distinct runtimes because autonomous background analysis and interactive engineering collaboration have fundamentally different latency and context requirements. The swarm-agent is built for throughput. It selects skills based on equipment type and symptoms, queries the knowledge base, and produces a structured HTML report with a lead cause hypothesis, ranked troubleshooting steps, and a confidence label. The report is delivered to Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) and linked to the Jira ticket within minutes. Swarm-hive is designed for depth: engineers can engage in interactive follow-up, upload images, and explore alternative paths, with &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/memory.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore memory, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;, retaining per-engineer conversational context across sessions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because swarm-hive accepts free-form engineer input, prompts are routed through &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/guardrails/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt; for content filtering.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="event-driven-jira-integration"&gt;Event-driven Jira integration&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;SWARM integrates with Jira through &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/scheduler/latest/UserGuide/what-is-scheduler.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon EventBridge Scheduler&lt;/a&gt; and a polling &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/lambda/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Lambda function&lt;/a&gt;, a serverless compute service that runs code in response to events, avoiding webhook configuration. Verified tickets enter an Amazon SQS queue for reliable delivery. DynamoDB handles deduplication using the ticket ID as a partition key with time-to-live (TTL) based expiry, and stores per-run metadata such as status, cost, and token usage to feed the evaluation loop. This &lt;code&gt;EventBridge-to-SQS&lt;/code&gt; pattern decouples the agent runtime from the ticketing system’s availability.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following example illustrates the pattern. The deduplication table uses a minimal schema with TTL-based expiry:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;dynamodb.create_table(
    TableName='swarm-deduplication',
    KeySchema=[
        {'AttributeName': 'ticket_id', 'KeyType': 'HASH'}
    ],
    AttributeDefinitions=[
        {'AttributeName': 'ticket_id', 'AttributeType': 'S'}
    ],
    TimeToLiveSpecification={
        'Enabled': True,
        'AttributeName': 'ttl'
    }
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="observability-design"&gt;Observability design&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Agentic systems with many interacting components are difficult to debug without per-invocation traces, so the Natera team built observability into SWARM before the first production deployment. If you’re running agents in production, per-invocation tracing is non-negotiable: without it, debugging a bad diagnosis means guessing which skill misbehaved.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In a clinical lab environment, where instrument downtime directly impacts patient reporting, the ability to trace, alert on, and audit every agent action isn’t a “nice to have”. It’s a regulatory and operational requirement.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;SWARM’s observability stack uses &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/a&gt; to capture searchable logs for every run, providing a clear record of operational health. AWS X-Ray traces each run from ticket intake to diagnostic report, recording time and cost per tool call so the team can pinpoint the specific skill responsible when something is slow or expensive rather than guessing. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sns/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Notification Service&lt;/a&gt; delivers failure alerts by email, while a Slack tracker gives the entire team real-time visibility into agent activity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="continuous-evaluation-methodology"&gt;Continuous evaluation methodology&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;SWARM uses a continuous evaluation loop rather than testing once at deployment, which is especially critical for systems handling changing documentation or evolving workflows. On every ticket processed, the agent provides a lead cause hypothesis, ranked troubleshooting steps, and a self-rated confidence label. Engineers record what they found and fixed, and an evaluator grades each response as Accurate, Partial, or Wrong, broken down by instrument family. Results are reviewed to identify where the agent is underperforming, the fix is applied (typically by updating the knowledge base), and the evaluation set is re-run to confirm improvement.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;SWARM’s self-rated confidence labels show a directional relationship with actual accuracy. Tickets labeled High confidence achieve 74 percent accuracy across 154 evaluated cases, while Moderate and Low confidence tickets both achieve 65 percent across 78 and 37 cases respectively. Note that Moderate and Low currently share the same accuracy rate. The Low confidence sample size of 37 cases is too small to draw statistically meaningful conclusions, and the Natera team expects the two tiers to diverge as the evaluation dataset expands.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The separation between High and the remaining tiers gives engineers a useful signal for how much weight to place on each diagnosis at the point of reading a diagnostic report.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="swarm-performance"&gt;SWARM performance&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Natera team evaluated SWARM across 524 support tickets to establish a performance baseline. Of the 524 tickets in the evaluation set, 269 received a High, Moderate, or Low confidence label and had a corresponding engineer-verified resolution available for comparison. The remaining tickets were either labeled none or lacked a verified resolution at the time of analysis.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Average first response time dropped from 17.4 hours to roughly 8 minutes (about 130x faster). These measurements were collected from Jira ticket metadata over a 90-day production evaluation period. Coverage matters just as much: 96.2 percent of tickets get an automated first response without an engineer having to initiate the diagnostic process. Coverage was measured as the percentage of incoming tickets that received a SWARM-generated diagnostic report before engineer review.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="lessons-from-building-agentic-ai-in-production"&gt;Lessons from building agentic AI in production&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Several architectural decisions in SWARM translate directly to teams building similar systems.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Treat chunk size and overlap as tunable parameters. The right values depend on how your source documents are structured.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Build traceability into your skill library from the start. When a diagnosis is wrong, you want to point to a specific skill, not debug the whole system.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;If your workload mixes batch processing with interactive querying, separate them into distinct runtimes.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="getting-started"&gt;Getting started&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Try these patterns yourself with the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/amazon-bedrock-agentcore-samples/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore samples repository&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/marketplace/build-learn/ai-agent-learning-series" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Building Agentic systems on AWS&lt;/a&gt; or the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/agentcore-get-started-cli.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Getting Started with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore guide&lt;/a&gt; for deploying agents securely at scale. Healthcare and life sciences organizations facing similar challenges with institutional knowledge loss, high-volume operational workflows, or clinical compliance requirements will find that the architectural patterns Natera developed with SWARM provide a replicable, production-tested foundation for building agentic AI systems on AWS.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Four Amazon Bedrock inference choices that affect cost</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/four-amazon-bedrock-inference-choices-that-affect-cost/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Sai Pavan Kasani]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 12 Nov 2026 20:51:55 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Best Practices]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">ced40265f1aaea8576eb1b20e2dbe39d4314107f</guid>

					<description>Your Amazon Bedrock inference bill can keep climbing even after you tune prompts and application logic. Four request-level choices (cache duration, service tier, reasoning effort, and maxTokens) change how many tokens the service processes and the rate applied. We show how we tested each and how to verify the effect.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Your Amazon Bedrock inference bill can keep climbing after you tune prompts and application logic. Request-level choices can still change how many tokens the service processes and the rate applied to them. Cache duration, service tier, reasoning effort, and &lt;code&gt;maxTokens&lt;/code&gt; can affect cost without changing your model or prompt.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each choice depends on the model and workload. This post shows how we tested each choice and how to verify its effect from response metadata, logs, and billing data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To try these settings yourself, start with the Amazon Bedrock Samples notebooks &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/amazon-bedrock-samples/blob/main/introduction-to-bedrock/prompt-caching/converse_api/notebooks/04_mixed_ttl_caching.ipynb" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Mixed TTL Caching&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/amazon-bedrock-samples/blob/main/introduction-to-bedrock/prompt-caching/converse_api/notebooks/05_tenant_isolation.ipynb" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Tenant Isolation with Prompt Caching&lt;/a&gt;. For &lt;code&gt;maxTokens&lt;/code&gt; and reasoning effort, see the 01-low-effort folder of the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-code-for-genai-cost-management-workshop/tree/main/samples/2-optimize/01-low-effort" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;sample-code-for-genai-cost-management-workshop&lt;/a&gt; GitHub repo. Each sample sets its own model ID, so change it, and the inference parameters, to match the model you call. For model availability by AWS Region, refer to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-cards.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Models at a glance&lt;/a&gt;. For broader decisions such as model selection, batch inference, routing, and distillation, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/effective-cost-optimization-strategies-for-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Effective cost optimization strategies for Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="where-inference-cost-comes-from"&gt;Where inference cost comes from&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every Amazon Bedrock response carries usage metadata. Input and output tokens bill at the rate for the model and tier that served the request. Cache reads and writes use separate token counters, and reasoning tokens count as output tokens.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prompt-cache-duration"&gt;Prompt cache duration&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Repeated system instructions, tool definitions, or reference context can be processed again when the cache expires before the next request. The &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/prompt-caching.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Prompt caching for faster model inference&lt;/a&gt; guide lists 5-minute and 1-hour time-to-live (TTL) values for supported Anthropic models. It also lists a 30-minute minimum TTL for supported GPT-5.6 models. Support and API fields depend on the model, so check the current support matrix before selecting a duration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For supported models, Amazon Bedrock also offers &lt;span&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/prompt-caching.html#prompt-caching-implicit" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;implicit prompt caching&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/span&gt;, which attempts to reuse eligible prompt prefixes without cache controls in your request. Because implicit caching is best effort and doesn’t guarantee a cache hit, the duration choices in this section apply to explicit caching.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This Converse API signature requests a 1-hour entry on models that support it:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{ "cachePoint": { "type": "default", "ttl": "1h" } }&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For supported Anthropic models, InvokeModel places the same &lt;code&gt;ttl&lt;/code&gt; value in &lt;code&gt;cache_control&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A longer duration is not automatically cheaper. The &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Pricing&lt;/a&gt; page lists separate rates for 5-minute writes, 1-hour writes, and cache reads. For Anthropic Claude Sonnet 5 and Anthropic Claude Opus 5 on Amazon Bedrock, a 5-minute write costs 1.25 times the input rate. A 1-hour write costs 2 times, and a cache read costs 0.1 times.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These rates set the break-even, assuming each request reuses the exact prefix and each cache read refreshes the TTL. A 5-minute cache pays back on the first reuse within 5 minutes. When requests for the same prefix arrive more than 5 minutes apart, it rewrites at 1.25 times on every request. The 1-hour cache is then cheaper from the second request within the hour.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Cache rates and checkpoint minimums differ by model. Check the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Pricing&lt;/a&gt; page and the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/prompt-caching.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;prompt caching guide&lt;/a&gt; for the model you call. Rerun the comparison for your model, then confirm it with cache read and write tokens after rollout.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Keep stable content before dynamic content, meet the model’s checkpoint minimum, and use on-demand inference because prompt caching doesn’t work with batch inference.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Changing a cached prefix, including a system instruction or tool definition, causes a miss and a new write. Checkpoints are evaluated in the order tools, system, then messages, so a change in an earlier section also invalidates the later ones. Version prompts that change often so each version gets its own cache entry. We ran six calls on Claude Sonnet 5 with a 1-hour checkpoint, changing the system prompt once and the tool definition once. Each change produced zero cache reads and a new write.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS describes cache entries as scoped to your account and Region, not to your tenants. In a multi-tenant application, prepend a tenant-specific SHA-256 hash to the cacheable prefix. The &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/optimizing-cost-and-latency-with-amazon-bedrock-prompt-caching/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Optimizing cost and latency with Amazon Bedrock prompt caching for Anthropic models&lt;/a&gt; post describes this pattern. Enforce tenant authorization before you build the prompt. The hash partitions entries inside one account. If a regulated workload needs a hard data boundary, keep each tenant in a separate AWS account.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Keep the 5-minute default for frequent reuse. Move from it to a longer supported duration when the same prefix recurs at least twice an hour with gaps longer than 5 minutes. To beat no caching at all, the 1-hour cache needs three requests an hour.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="service-tiers-for-latency-tolerant-workloads"&gt;Service tiers for latency-tolerant workloads&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Background jobs, evaluation harnesses, nightly summarization, and backfills don’t always need the latency profile used by interactive requests. For models that support Flex, select it with this request signature:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{ "serviceTier": { "type": "flex" } }&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use &lt;code&gt;default&lt;/code&gt;, not &lt;code&gt;standard&lt;/code&gt;, to request the Standard tier. You can also omit &lt;code&gt;serviceTier&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{ "serviceTier": { "type": "default" } }&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Tier support is model-specific, so record support by exact model version. The &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-card-amazon-nova-2-lite.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Nova 2 Lite&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-card-openai-gpt-oss-120b.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OpenAI gpt-oss-120b&lt;/a&gt; model cards list Flex as supported. The cards for &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-card-anthropic-claude-sonnet-5.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Anthropic Claude Sonnet 5&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-card-anthropic-claude-opus-5.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Anthropic Claude Opus 5&lt;/a&gt; on Amazon Bedrock list it as not supported. A Flex request to an unsupported model fails with a &lt;code&gt;ValidationException&lt;/code&gt; instead of silently running on Standard. Our request to Anthropic Claude Sonnet 5 returned that error.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If your policy allows Standard as a fallback, catch the unsupported-tier error and retry once with &lt;code&gt;default&lt;/code&gt;. Don’t retry every &lt;code&gt;ValidationException&lt;/code&gt;, because malformed requests raise it too. Cap application-level retries. The bounded retry strategy of the AWS SDK handles throttling and transient errors.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Set your own deadline for Flex requests. A supported response reports the resolved tier, which you can also verify with Amazon CloudWatch &lt;code&gt;ResolvedServiceTier&lt;/code&gt;. See &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/service-tiers-inference.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Service tiers for optimizing performance and cost&lt;/a&gt; and the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Pricing&lt;/a&gt; page for the exact model you call.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We tested Flex on Amazon Nova 2 Lite with 200 &lt;code&gt;default&lt;/code&gt; requests and 200 &lt;code&gt;flex&lt;/code&gt; requests, sent in interleaved pairs. Both tiers processed the same number of input and output tokens. All 400 requests resolved to the requested tier, and the median latency difference was too small to separate from noise. At the time of writing, the published Flex rate for Nova 2 Lite in us-west-2 is half the Standard rate, and our bill reflected that. Check the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Pricing&lt;/a&gt; page for current rates. Keep latency-sensitive traffic on &lt;code&gt;default&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="adaptive-thinking-effort"&gt;Adaptive thinking effort&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Reasoning tokens bill as output tokens. Claude Sonnet 5 and Claude Opus 5 use adaptive thinking at &lt;code&gt;high&lt;/code&gt; effort by default, even when a request omits the &lt;code&gt;thinking&lt;/code&gt; field. An application moved from Claude Sonnet 4.6 without changes starts billing thinking tokens, and &lt;code&gt;maxTokens&lt;/code&gt; now caps thinking plus response text. Claude Haiku 5.5 defaults to &lt;code&gt;medium&lt;/code&gt;. All three models support &lt;code&gt;low&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;medium&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;high&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;xhigh&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;max&lt;/code&gt;. Lower the effort for a task with this request signature:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "thinking": { "type": "adaptive" },
  "output_config": { "effort": "low" }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Keep effort in &lt;code&gt;output_config&lt;/code&gt;, not inside &lt;code&gt;thinking&lt;/code&gt;. Check &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/claude-messages-adaptive-thinking.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Adaptive thinking&lt;/a&gt; whenever you change models.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We tested a tool-calling agent on Claude Opus 5. Its three tasks follow the design in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-long-horizon-agents-with-grok-4-7-on-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Build long-horizon agents with Grok 4.7 on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;, and each has one correct answer. We ran each task eight times at every effort level. The table shows median output tokens:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Effort&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Correct runs&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Median output tokens&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Change from low (95% interval)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;low&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;24 of 24&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;936&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;baseline&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;medium&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;24 of 24&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1,484&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;+59% (50% to 78%)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;high (default)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;24 of 24&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2,521&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;+169% (112% to 190%)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;xhigh&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;24 of 24&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2,964&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;+217% (170% to 260%)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;max&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;24 of 24&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3,762&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;+302% (180% to 402%)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Accuracy at lower effort depends on the model and the task. Measure on tasks shaped like yours.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On the Amazon Bedrock Samples &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/amazon-bedrock-samples/blob/main/genai-use-cases/text-generation/how_to_work_with_code_generation_w_bedrock.ipynb" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Invoke Bedrock model for code generation&lt;/a&gt; workload, median output tokens were 676 at &lt;code&gt;low&lt;/code&gt; and 743 at &lt;code&gt;high&lt;/code&gt;, a difference within run-to-run variation. The programs we graded passed at each effort level.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use per-request output-token counts, CloudWatch &lt;code&gt;AWS/Bedrock OutputTokenCount&lt;/code&gt;, invocation logs, and a task pass rate to evaluate the choice. Start below &lt;code&gt;high&lt;/code&gt; for routine, well-bounded tasks. Keep &lt;code&gt;high&lt;/code&gt; where your quality tests show that the task needs it. A token reduction that breaks the task is not a saving.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="output-token-limits"&gt;Output token limits&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;maxTokens&lt;/code&gt; is an application output ceiling. It limits the output that a bad prompt, regression, or loop can produce before the request stops. The quota rule is documented in &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/quotas-token-burndown.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;How tokens are counted in Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;. Amazon Bedrock deducts your input tokens plus &lt;code&gt;maxTokens&lt;/code&gt; when a request starts, so an oversized value reduces concurrency.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An output ceiling isn’t a safety control. Use Amazon Bedrock Guardrails as a standard responsible AI control in production generative AI workloads. Guardrails can filter content, detect prompt attacks, and check grounding on supported inputs and responses. See &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/guardrails.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Detect and filter harmful content by using Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Set &lt;code&gt;maxTokens&lt;/code&gt; per task type after observing a representative output-token distribution:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{ "inferenceConfig": { "maxTokens": 155 } }&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;See &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/conversation-inference.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Inference using Converse API&lt;/a&gt; for the request parameters.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In our test on Claude Sonnet 5, five normal outputs peaked at 103 tokens, so we set an illustrative fence of 155 tokens with headroom. We then sent the same deliberately verbose prompt with adaptive thinking disabled, once under a 2,048-token ceiling and once under the 155-token fence. The first request generated 2,048 output tokens and the second generated 155, so the fence avoided 1,893 output tokens. Both stopped at &lt;code&gt;max_tokens&lt;/code&gt;, so both responses were truncated. The fence limits spend but does not make an unsuitable prompt useful, and in a multi-turn or agentic chain a truncated turn can break the steps after it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For production calibration, compare the 95th and 99th percentiles (P95 and P99) for each task type and add headroom to the selected percentile. With adaptive thinking on, &lt;code&gt;maxTokens&lt;/code&gt; also caps thinking tokens, so calibrate with thinking enabled. P95 limits exposure more aggressively but accepts more truncation risk. P99 preserves more valid long-tail responses at a higher ceiling. Monitor &lt;code&gt;max_tokens&lt;/code&gt; stops and investigate the request before raising the task’s limit.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="putting-it-together"&gt;Putting it together&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use the same test loop for each setting: confirm model support, change one request choice, measure tokens or cost, and track quality or completion. If you route across models, keep a capability record for each model version, Region, and API: supported cache duration, service tiers, reasoning levels, and output ceiling. Validate each request against that record before you send it, then check the response for the resolved tier and cache counters.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Setting&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Default or permissive state&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Result&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Risk to accept&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cache duration&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Model-specific TTL&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;From published Sonnet 5 and Opus 5 rates, a 1-hour cache wins from the second request per hour when gaps exceed 5 minutes&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A 1-hour write costs 2 times input, so low reuse loses money. Partition tenant prefixes&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Service tier&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;default&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;At the time of writing, Nova 2 Lite Flex was published at half the Standard rate, and our bill matched&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Longer processing. Set your own deadline and a fallback&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Reasoning effort&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;high&lt;/code&gt; on the tested adaptive model&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Low to high: +169% median output tokens on the agent test, no reliable difference on code generation&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Quality can drop near the task’s capability boundary. Track pass rate with tokens&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;maxTokens&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Permissive 2,048-token cap&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;An illustrative 155-token fence avoided 1,893 output tokens in one test&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Truncation can cascade through multi-turn or agentic chains. Check &lt;code&gt;stopReason&lt;/code&gt; before raising the limit&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="signals-to-watch"&gt;Signals to watch&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Setting&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Symptom&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Verify with&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;First response&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cache duration&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Repeated context does not produce the expected latency or cost benefit&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Application latency and cost telemetry&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Check prefix stability, reuse interval, and model support before changing TTL&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Service tier&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The requested tier is unsupported or the serving tier differs from expectation&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Response &lt;code&gt;serviceTier&lt;/code&gt; and CloudWatch &lt;code&gt;ResolvedServiceTier&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Use a supported tier or return latency-sensitive traffic to &lt;code&gt;default&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Reasoning effort&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Output tokens rise while quality holds&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Per-request response telemetry, CloudWatch &lt;code&gt;OutputTokenCount&lt;/code&gt;, invocation logs, and task pass rate&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Lower effort for that task class and rerun its quality test&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;maxTokens&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;stopReason&lt;/code&gt; is &lt;code&gt;max_tokens&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Response stop reason and task-scoped output-token distribution&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Inspect the prompt and task fence before raising the limit&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Derive P95 and P99 for each task type from task-tagged application telemetry or invocation logs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;All four choices live in the inference request. Recheck support when you change models, then change one setting at a time and measure the result in your application.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use these resources to evaluate the choices for your workload:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Bedrock console: &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;console.aws.amazon.com/bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Prompt caching: &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/prompt-caching.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Prompt caching for faster model inference&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Service tiers: &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/service-tiers-inference.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Service tiers for optimizing performance and cost&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Adaptive thinking: &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/claude-messages-adaptive-thinking.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Adaptive thinking&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Responsible AI controls: &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/guardrails.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Detect and filter harmful content by using Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Related reading: &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/effective-cost-optimization-strategies-for-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Effective cost optimization strategies for Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Related samples and posts: &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/amazon-bedrock-samples/blob/main/genai-use-cases/text-generation/how_to_work_with_code_generation_w_bedrock.ipynb" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Invoke Bedrock model for code generation&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-long-horizon-agents-with-grok-4-7-on-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Build long-horizon agents with Grok 4.7 on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Tokenomics at scale: Track and cap Amazon Bedrock spend per IAM principal with CUR 2.0 and AWS Budgets</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/tokenomics-at-scale-track-and-cap-amazon-bedrock-spend-per-iam-principal-with-cur-2-0-and-aws-budgets/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Arun Chandapillai]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 12 Nov 2026 19:29:55 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AWS Budgets]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">e0c369a0dd940a1e529c2070289fe5f5a34b16a2</guid>

					<description>Get per-identity Amazon Bedrock cost visibility and enforceable spend limits using only AWS-native features, with no application code for the common case. Attribute spend with IAM principal tags, report with CUR 2.0 or Cost Explorer, and enforce with AWS Budgets actions that apply a Deny policy when a threshold is crossed.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Generative AI spend is hard to attribute. Before an organization expands AI access, CFOs and CTOs usually want three answers: what do we spend per person or per team, can we cap it, and how much work does it take to get there.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In our previous post, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/tokenomics-at-scale-how-jamf-built-real-time-spend-enforcement-for-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Tokenomics at Scale: How Jamf Built Real-Time Spend Enforcement for Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;, we explained a custom enforcement loop built on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/identity-center/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS IAM Identity Center&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/athena/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Athena&lt;/a&gt; over &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-invocation-logging.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;model invocation logs&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/lambda/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Lambda&lt;/a&gt;. This approach gives near-real-time control, but it requires code you then own and operate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post shows how to get per-identity Amazon Bedrock cost visibility and enforceable spend limits using only AWS-native features, with no application code to write for the common case. The approach relies on three things: &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/cost-mgmt-iam-principal-tracking.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;IAM principal attribution&lt;/a&gt;, which Amazon Bedrock records automatically; &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cur/latest/userguide/table-dictionary-cur2.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Cost and Usage Reports&lt;/a&gt; (CUR) 2.0 or &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/aws-cost-management/aws-cost-explorer/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Cost Explorer&lt;/a&gt; for reporting; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/aws-cost-management/aws-budgets/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Budgets&lt;/a&gt; with budget actions for enforcement. By the end, you will have per-identity cost reporting and an automatic guardrail that restricts Amazon Bedrock access when a threshold is crossed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The solution has three parts: attribute, report, and enforce.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Attribute.&lt;/strong&gt; Amazon Bedrock captures the AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) principal identity, the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/id_users.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;IAM user&lt;/a&gt; or &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/id_roles.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;role&lt;/a&gt;, for every inference request. This happens automatically, with no change to your Amazon Bedrock model inference calls. If several people share one role, you add per-user detail with session tags passed at role assumption time. If each person or application has its own role, principal tags on the role are enough.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Report.&lt;/strong&gt; Activate those tags as cost allocation tags, then read the data either in Cost Explorer for a console-only view, or in a CUR 2.0 data export for detailed line-item analysis and SQL queries.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Enforce.&lt;/strong&gt; Create an AWS Budget scoped to Amazon Bedrock and filtered to one identity or tag value, then attach a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cost-management/latest/userguide/budgets-controls.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;budget action&lt;/a&gt; that applies a Deny IAM policy when the threshold is crossed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following sections show each part in more detail, alongside the implementation steps.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To deploy this solution, you need:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/account/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS account&lt;/a&gt; with Amazon Bedrock model access enabled.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Permissions to tag &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/cost-mgmt-iam-principal-tracking.html#cost-mgmt-iam-principal-tracking-principal-types" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;IAM principal&lt;/a&gt;, activate &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/awsaccountbilling/latest/aboutv2/cost-alloc-tags.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;cost allocation tags&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cur/latest/userguide/dataexports-create.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;create data exports&lt;/a&gt;, and create budgets and budget actions.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Bedrock accessed through IAM roles or IAM users.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cli/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Command Line Interface&lt;/a&gt; (AWS CLI) configured with appropriate credentials.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A CUR 2.0 data export and an &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Storage Service&lt;/a&gt; (&lt;em&gt;Amazon S3&lt;/em&gt;) bucket to receive it. &lt;em&gt;(optional, for detailed line-item analysis)&lt;/em&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Athena. &lt;em&gt;(optional, for querying the export)&lt;/em&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/aws-cost-management/aws-billing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Billing Console&lt;/a&gt; access is managed separately from most IAM permissions. Confirm you have it before starting.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="walkthrough"&gt;Walkthrough&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before configuring anything, check what you already have. Amazon Bedrock records caller identity for every inference request automatically. Open Cost Explorer, filter Service to Amazon Bedrock, and group by the available identity or tag dimension. For accounts where each application or person already uses a distinct IAM role, this may be all the visibility you need.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To configure additional visibility:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Step 1 gives you identity-level visibility.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Steps 2 through 4 add tag dimensions and detailed reporting.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Step 5 adds enforcement.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-1-decide-how-identity-maps-to-your-users"&gt;Step 1: Decide how identity maps to your users&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;How you tag depends on how your IAM principals are organized.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;One role per person or per application.&lt;/strong&gt; Attach principal tags directly to each IAM role or user. These are static and apply to every request from that principal. Use the IAM console, or the AWS CLI:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws iam tag-role \
  --role-name BedrockDeveloperRole-alice \
  --tags Key=user,Value=alice@example.com Key=team,Value=platform Key=CostCenter,Value=CC-1001&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A shared role used by several people, or a gateway.&lt;/strong&gt; Principal tags cannot distinguish users on a shared role, because every session carries the same tags. Use session tags instead, passed when the role is assumed. Session tags are dynamic and can vary per session. If a session tag and a principal tag share a key, the session tag wins for that session.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The role’s trust policy must allow &lt;code&gt;sts:TagSession&lt;/code&gt;. Session tags can be passed three ways:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;sts:AssumeRole&lt;/code&gt; with &lt;code&gt;--tags&lt;/code&gt;, for a gateway assuming a role per user or tenant.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity&lt;/code&gt;, with tags in the https://aws.amazon.com/tags claim of the OpenID Connect (OIDC) ID token (this is a claim name, not a URL you browse to).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;AssumeRoleWithSAML&lt;/code&gt;, by mapping &lt;code&gt;PrincipalTag:*&lt;/code&gt; attributes in the identity provider’s Security Assertion Markup Language (SAML) assertion.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If your organization already captures a claim like &lt;code&gt;saml:sub&lt;/code&gt; for other purposes, that alone does not make it available here. Your identity provider must separately map that attribute to a &lt;code&gt;PrincipalTag:*&lt;/code&gt; SAML attribute statement, and the role’s trust policy must allow &lt;code&gt;sts:TagSession&lt;/code&gt;, before it flows through as a session tag. The two integrations draw from the same SAML assertion but are configured independently. Common tag keys are user, team, Department, CostCenter, and Environment. You can apply up to 50 principal or session tags per IAM user or role.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/21962-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/21962-1.png" alt="Principal tags attached to an IAM role compared with session tags passed at role assumption" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Attribution with principal tags compared to session tags&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-2-activate-cost-allocation-tags"&gt;Step 2: Activate cost allocation tags&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Tags do not appear in billing data until you activate them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/aws-cost-management/billing-and-cost-management-console-home/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Billing and Cost Management Console&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Cost allocation tags&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Filter by type &lt;strong&gt;IAM principal&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Select your tag keys and choose &lt;strong&gt;Activate&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In an &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/organizations/latest/userguide/orgs_introduction.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Organization&lt;/a&gt;, activate IAM principal cost allocation tags in the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/managedservices/latest/userguide/management-account.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;management account&lt;/a&gt; (payer). Member accounts cannot activate them independently.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two things to expect:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A tag key only appears in this list after the principal has made at least one Amazon Bedrock call, so make a test call first if the list looks empty.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;It might take up to 24 hours for a newly applied tag key to appear for activation, then up to another 24 hours after you activate it before it begins populating billing data.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Activation does not apply retroactively by default, so activate tags before you need the data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-3-create-a-cur-2.0-data-export-that-includes-caller-identity"&gt;Step 3: Create a CUR 2.0 data export that includes caller identity&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Skip this step if Cost Explorer is enough for your reporting.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the Billing and Cost Management console, choose &lt;strong&gt;Data Exports&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Create&lt;/strong&gt; and create a CUR 2.0 (Standard) export.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Under &lt;strong&gt;Additional export content&lt;/strong&gt;, select &lt;strong&gt;Include caller identity (IAM principal) allocation data&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Under &lt;strong&gt;Time granularity&lt;/strong&gt;, select Hourly.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Under &lt;strong&gt;Report data integration&lt;/strong&gt;, select &lt;strong&gt;Amazon Athena&lt;/strong&gt;. This sets the file format to Parquet and, importantly, delivers a setup script to your S3 bucket alongside the export data.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose the destination Amazon S3 bucket.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Run the delivered setup script (or the Glue crawler/DDL it defines) once. This is what actually creates the Glue Data Catalog table you’ll query in Step 4. The preceding console flow does not create it for you automatically.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you already have a CUR 2.0 export created before enabling identity attribution, it will not include identity data retroactively. Create a new export with the caller identity option selected. In the export, principal and session tags appear as columns prefixed &lt;code&gt;iamPrincipal/&lt;/code&gt;, and resource tags as &lt;code&gt;resourceTags/&lt;/code&gt;. For example, a tag key team appears as &lt;code&gt;iamPrincipal/team&lt;/code&gt;. AWS publishes a ready-made query set for this exact pattern in &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-cur-iam-principal-bedrock-tracking" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;aws-samples/sample-cur-iam-principal-bedrock-tracking&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-4-read-the-data"&gt;Step 4: Read the data&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram compares the two ways to read the data: a console-only path through Cost Explorer, and a SQL path over the CUR 2.0 export with Amazon Athena.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/21962-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/21962-2.png" alt="Two reporting paths, the Cost Explorer console view and a SQL query over the CUR 2.0 export in Amazon Athena" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Reporting with Cost Explorer compared to CUR 2.0 and Amazon Athena&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Console path, no custom code.&lt;/strong&gt; Cost Explorer, filtered to Amazon Bedrock and grouped by your tag key, gives per-team or per-user spend as a chart and table. This covers most recurring reporting needs and requires no query authoring.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;SQL path, low code.&lt;/strong&gt; Point Amazon Athena at the CUR 2.0 export to group spend by &lt;code&gt;iamPrincipal/team&lt;/code&gt; and usage type:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;SELECT
    tags['iamPrincipal/team'] AS team,
    line_item_usage_type,
    SUM(line_item_unblended_cost) AS cost
FROM cur2_view
WHERE product_servicecode IN ('AmazonBedrock', 'AmazonBedrockService')
AND billing_period = '2026-09'
GROUP BY 1, 2
ORDER BY 3 DESC&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Things to note:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Service code&lt;/strong&gt;: Amazon Bedrock spend can be recorded under more than one &lt;code&gt;product_servicecode&lt;/code&gt; value, for example, &lt;code&gt;AmazonBedrock&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;AmazonBedrockService&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Table name&lt;/strong&gt;: The table name (&lt;code&gt;cur2_view&lt;/code&gt;) is whatever the setup script from Step 3 named it when it created the Glue table. Check the script or your Glue catalog if it differs. Column names match the CUR 2.0 Parquet schema regardless of table name.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Billing-period column.&lt;/strong&gt; Confirm how your export exposes the period. Some exports use &lt;code&gt;billing_period&lt;/code&gt; (YYYY-MM). Others partition on a start-date or year/month column.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two notes worth carrying into any query you write.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Count all token types&lt;/strong&gt;: Amazon Bedrock bills four token types, each at a different rate: input, output, cache read, and cache write. Each is a separate line item with its own usage type and unit price, so summing only input and output undercounts. The gap is largest for workloads that use prompt caching heavily. Refer to the AWS document &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/cost-mgmt-understanding-cur-data.html#cost-mgmt-cur-pricing" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Understanding Amazon Bedrock pricing in CUR&lt;/a&gt; for the latest information.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Match the rate to the line item, not the model&lt;/strong&gt;: Amazon Bedrock pricing in CUR is determined by three things: the token type, the service tier, and whether the request was routed through cross-Region inference. In-Region and cross-Region requests are priced differently, and service tier is reflected in the usage type as well. The usage type carries a suffix indicating cross-Region routing and service tier, so if your workloads mix these, match the rate to each line item rather than applying one rate per model. Refer to the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/cost-mgmt-understanding-cur-data.html#cost-mgmt-cur-usage-type-examples" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CUR usage type examples&lt;/a&gt; for examples.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-5-add-enforcement-with-aws-budgets-and-budget-actions"&gt;Step 5: Add enforcement with AWS Budgets and budget actions&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS Budgets performs the attach, so there is no custom code for this workflow. Budget actions attach to a specific IAM user, group, or role. This isolates one person’s access only if that person already has their own role. If your Amazon Bedrock traffic runs through a role that many people share, a Deny action attached to that role blocks everyone on it, not only the person who crossed the threshold. The following diagram explains the enforcement workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/21962-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/21962-3.png" alt="AWS Budgets threshold triggering a budget action that attaches a Deny IAM policy" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Enforcement with AWS Budgets and budget actions&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS Budgets can run an action when a threshold is crossed. Available actions include applying a custom IAM policy, applying a service control policy (SCP), and targeting Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2) or Amazon Relational Database Service (Amazon RDS) instances. For this use case, the IAM policy action is the relevant one.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;First, create the Deny policy the action will attach. This example denies the higher-cost models while leaving a lower-cost model available, so the user can keep working:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Effect": "Deny",
      "Action": [
        "bedrock:InvokeModel",
        "bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream"
      ],
      "Resource": [
        "arn:aws:bedrock:*::foundation-model/[HIGHER-COST-MODEL-ID]"
      ]
    }
  ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Replace &lt;code&gt;[HIGHER-COST-MODEL-ID]&lt;/code&gt; with the model ID you want to deny in your environment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Then set up permissions and the budget itself.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;There are two distinct roles here, and the AWS managed policy only covers one of them:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Your own user or role&lt;/strong&gt; (the one configuring the budget) needs the AWS managed policy &lt;code&gt;AWSBudgetsActionsWithAWSResourceControlAccess&lt;/code&gt;. It grants budgets permissions plus the conditioned &lt;code&gt;iam:PassRole&lt;/code&gt; needed to hand your budget action role to AWS Budgets.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The IAM role AWS Budgets assumes to perform the action&lt;/strong&gt; is not covered by a single named managed policy for this use case. Create it with a trust policy that allows the principal &lt;code&gt;budgets.amazonaws.com&lt;/code&gt; to assume it (scoped with &lt;code&gt;aws:SourceArn&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;aws:SourceAccount&lt;/code&gt; conditions), and a permissions policy granting &lt;code&gt;iam:AttachRolePolicy&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;iam:DetachRolePolicy&lt;/code&gt; (and &lt;code&gt;organizations:AttachPolicy&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;organizations:DetachPolicy&lt;/code&gt; if you are also using SCPs). Refer to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cost-management/latest/userguide/budgets-action-role.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Setting up a role for AWS Budgets to run budget actions&lt;/a&gt; for the grantable permissions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Create the budget action role described earlier.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Create a cost budget scoped to Amazon Bedrock, filtered to the identity or tag value you want to cap.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In &lt;strong&gt;Configure alerts&lt;/strong&gt;, add an alert threshold. Choose &lt;strong&gt;Actual&lt;/strong&gt; for realized spend or &lt;strong&gt;Forecasted&lt;/strong&gt; to act before the spend lands.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Under &lt;strong&gt;Attach actions&lt;/strong&gt;, choose &lt;strong&gt;Add Action&lt;/strong&gt;, select the budget actions role, and choose the IAM policy action. Select the Deny policy and the IAM user, group, or role to attach it to.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For automatic enforcement, choose &lt;strong&gt;Yes&lt;/strong&gt; to run the action automatically. Choose &lt;strong&gt;No&lt;/strong&gt; to require a human to approve each action from the &lt;strong&gt;Alert details&lt;/strong&gt; page.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Configure at least one notification so the affected user is told why access changed.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can attach multiple actions to a single threshold, and multiple thresholds to one budget. That is how you build tiers: for example, deny the premium model at 80 percent and the mid-tier model at 100 percent, while never denying the low-cost model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Only the management account can apply SCPs, and from the management account you can apply an SCP to another account.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="considerations"&gt;Considerations&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS Budgets is not near-real-time&lt;/strong&gt;: Budget data refreshes up to three times a day, so spend can overshoot a threshold before the action runs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cost allocation tags are not retroactive&lt;/strong&gt;: Expect up to 24 hours for a newly applied tag key to appear, then up to another 24 hours after you activate it before it begins populating billing data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;CUR does not contain per-request line items&lt;/strong&gt;: Both classic CUR and CUR 2.0 aggregate Amazon Bedrock cost by usage type, operation, and pricing/resource attributes over an hour or a day.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;IAM principal fields require CUR 2.0&lt;/strong&gt;: The legacy CUR format does not include them. Per-token cost breakdowns are available in both formats.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Session tags are bound at AssumeRole time, not per request.&lt;/strong&gt; Their values are constant for every call made with that session’s credentials, and they surface only as aggregated billing data. Session tags are not written to model invocation logs. Logs capture the caller’s &lt;code&gt;identity.arn&lt;/code&gt;. To distinguish users on a shared session at the request level, use a per-user &lt;code&gt;RoleSessionName&lt;/code&gt;, or set the user in request metadata on each call.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Budget actions attach to IAM users, groups, and roles.&lt;/strong&gt; If your users are federated and have no standing IAM identity, the action needs a role to target. This is why the pattern works best when each person or application has its own role. If your users share a role or are federated without their own role, see our previous &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/tokenomics-at-scale-how-jamf-built-real-time-spend-enforcement-for-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;post&lt;/a&gt; for a per-user enforcement approach.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A shared gateway role collapses attribution by default.&lt;/strong&gt; With a large language model (LLM) gateway, the identity captured is the gateway’s execution role.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;New models need attention.&lt;/strong&gt; Usage types are model-specific. When you enable a new model, confirm it appears in your queries and budget filters as expected rather than assuming it is covered.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="decision-framework"&gt;Decision framework&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Requirement&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Recommended path&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Quick reporting&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cost Explorer grouped by identity&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Per-team reporting&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cost Explorer with activated tags (Steps 1, 2, and 4)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Detailed line-item analysis&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;CUR 2.0 export (Steps 1 through 4)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Enforcement&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AWS Budgets with budget actions (Steps 1 and 5)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="cleanup"&gt;Cleanup&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, delete all the resources you created.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you learned how to get per-identity Amazon Bedrock cost visibility and enforceable spend limits using only AWS-native features, with no application code to write for the common case. As generative AI adoption scales, the organizations that win will be the ones that can answer the tokenomics question with confidence: what do we spend per person or per team, can we cap it, and how much work does it take to get there.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Now, it’s your turn to start exploring. We are here to help, and if you need further assistance, reach out to &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/contact-us/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Support&lt;/a&gt; and your AWS account team.&lt;/p&gt;
